By injecting learnable Gaussian priors, we impose structural constraints that compel the model to focus on multi-granular local contexts, effectively filtering out global noise and accurately capturing local evolutionary phases. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed model.

new Noise-Space Reward Alignment for Few-Step Generative Models

Authors: Woocheol Shin, Taeyoung Yun, Jiwon Sung, Hyeongyu Kang, Jeongmin Park, Jaewoo Lee, Kyuil Sim, Dohyun Kim, Yutong He, Jinkyoo Park

Abstract: Few-step generative models produce high-quality samples in one or a few evaluations. However, steering them toward desired properties remains difficult, especially when only black-box reward evaluations are available. While one could apply approaches developed for iterative diffusion and flow-based models, such as direct RL fine-tuning or test-time scaling, we find that neither transfers well to the few-step regime: direct fine-tuning suffers from severe reward over-optimization, and test-time scaling sacrifices the efficiency advantage of few-step generation. We propose Noise Policy Optimization (NPO), which explicitly targets the alignment problem for few-step generative models. The key idea is to learn an amortized flow policy over the initial noise space of the generator. The trained policy transports Gaussian noise to a reward-tilted noise distribution, whose pushforward distribution through the frozen generator is exactly the reward-tilted output distribution. By keeping the generator frozen, we can effectively utilize the knowledge encoded in the pretrained model, yielding a better reward-quality trade-off than direct fine-tuning. The amortized policy moves the search cost into training, so inference adds only a lightweight noise sampling step on top of the pretrained model. To improve sample efficiency during training, we use ELBO-based likelihood estimation that requires only sampled endpoints rather than simulated trajectories. Across image, language, and protein structure generation, NPO consistently achieves a better reward-quality trade-off than direct fine-tuning, and matches test-time search methods with 16 to 549 times lower inference cost.

new Predicting Live Birth After Fresh Embryo Transfer in IVF/ICSI: Differential ICM/Trophectoderm Morphology Encoding and a Hybrid Attention--Logistic Regression Ensemble

Authors: Abdul Raheem Shahzad, Jinxin Chen, Ming Zhang, Juan Deng

Abstract: Predicting live birth before fresh embryo transfer could support shared decision-making in IVF/ICSI, but routine clinical data are small, heterogeneous, and contain pathway-dependent variables prone to outcome leakage. We aimed to construct a curated single-centre IVF/ICSI dataset and develop a prediction model suited to small tabular clinical data. \textit{Materials and Methods:} We assembled 616 fresh embryo-transfer cycles (251 live birth, 365 non-live birth) with clinical, laboratory, and embryological predictors, and embryo morphology encoded with a novel differential weighting of the inner cell mass and trophectoderm. After KNN imputation, robust scaling, and selection of 50 features, we developed a Hybrid Attention Network (HAN), a learnable feature-attention gate followed by a compact multilayer perceptron, and a HAN + logistic-regression (LR) ensemble with a validation-tuned weight. Models were benchmarked against classical machine learning, TabPFN, and text-serialized language models on a held-out test set; AUC-ROC was the primary metric. \textit{Results:} The HAN + LR ensemble achieved the highest test AUC-ROC (0.710; accuracy 0.710, F1 0.630), ahead of the HAN backbone alone (0.700), logistic regression (0.667), the TabPFN + LR hybrid (0.666), and TabPFN (0.650). Text-serialized language models performed near chance (AUC $\le$ 0.61). The differentially weighted trophectoderm feature ranked highest by consensus importance across models. \textit{Discussion:} A compact attention network combined with a linear model outperformed both components and strong foundation-model baselines on a small clinical cohort. \textit{Conclusion:} The curated dataset and HAN + LR ensemble offer a transparent, reproducible basis for IVF/ICSI live-birth prediction; external validation is the next step.

new Language Models Share a Readout Bottleneck in Working Memory

Authors: Dongyu Gong

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) reproduce a human working-memory signature on the N-back task: performance declines as memory load N increases, and errors concentrate on lures, items that repeat the current stimulus at a neighboring but incorrect serial position. We ask whether this shared behavioral signature reflects a shared mechanism. The literature offers two candidate accounts. On a precision account, softmax attention cannot concentrate its weight on the item N positions back, so adjacent items are retrieved instead. On an interference account, attention selects the correct item, but the content of competing items corrupts the decision. We dissociate these accounts in 15 pretrained LLMs from three families (70 million to 14 billion parameters) and in minimal transformers trained from scratch. In all three families, the attention heads that carry the task attend to the target on most missed matches. Linear probes decode near-perfectly whether a matching letter occurred at the target or at a lure position, even on trials where the model false-alarms to the lure, although its own decisions barely distinguish the two. Sharpening attention yields negligible gains, whereas suppressing the letter identity of non-target items improves sensitivity (d') in nearly all model-by-load conditions. In larger models the task is spread over more heads, and removing interference helps more. In minimal transformers, precision limits a single attention layer, but a second layer removes this constraint. The working-memory limit of LLMs therefore lies in reading items out under interference rather than in locating them, consistent with interference accounts of human working memory.

new Judging the Judges: Confidence and Disagreement in Rubrics as Rewards

Authors: Michael Tian-Yue Liu, Anne Harrington, Trevor Darrell, Yutong Bai

Abstract: Rubrics as Rewards (RaR) extends reinforcement learning to non-verifiable domains by using an LLM judge to score responses against a structured rubric. Prior work has focused on improving the rubric or strengthening the single judge, with comparatively little attention to how different single or multiple judges might be used during RaR training. We first show that judge identity alone produces a substantial spread in scores when training Qwen3-4B-Base on RaR-Medicine and RaR-Science across five frontier and open-weight judges. Building on this, we propose RaRE (Rubrics as Rewards with Ensembles), a novel judge ensemble strategy for RaR with two mechanisms: a soft-Likert reward that uses judge logprobs to weight more confident judges more heavily, and a drop-on-disagreement mask that excludes high-disagreement rollouts from the GRPO update. We find that this design is critical: while a naive average of judge scores fails to outperform the best single judge, RaRE outperforms every single-judge RaR variant on both HealthBench and GPQA Diamond, with per-theme analysis showing that it inherits the complementary strengths of its constituent judges. Our findings establish judge ensembling as a compelling axis for improving RaR.

new Sensor Diagnosis for Fuel and Emissions Monitoring in Heavy-Duty Diesel Vehicles

Authors: Tejas Dahiya, Saurabh Kumar Gupta

Abstract: Faulty sensor readings can distort estimates of fuel use and carbon dioxide emissions from diesel engines. We evaluate two procedures that use relationships learned from healthy measurements to diagnose faults and guide fuel correction. The diagnosis procedure tests whether replacing a suspected faulty reading with an estimated healthy value helps a model predict another sensor's recorded reading more accurately. It then predicts the fault type and decides whether to report the diagnosis. We evaluate five synthetic fault types on 217 steady-state operating points from a heavy-duty diesel engine, excluding all measurements at selected engine speeds from training. Across 31,500 fault-injected groups of 20 operating points, it issues diagnoses for 77.3% of groups, with 92.1% of diagnoses issued on these groups correct. The fuel correction procedure uses a different sensor-selection rule at individual operating points, testing how each replacement changes the largest prediction error across all six sensors. Fuel correction requires a fault alarm and engine speed and torque close to the training conditions. A fuel reading is replaced only when its replacement reduces the largest prediction error sufficiently more than replacements for the other sensors. Assuming faults in 1% of one-hour scenarios, equally likely sensors and fault types, and equal time at each measured condition, selected fuel corrections reduce mean absolute error in calculated combustion CO2 by 83.8%. On this engine map, learned relationships support complete sensor diagnoses and fuel corrections that improve emissions accounting while preserving unaffected readings.

new Resisting Harmful Instructions through Training-Free Weight Editing

Authors: Ali Ebrahimpour-Boroojeny, Xiaocong Yang, Samuel Schapiro, Zahra Borhani, Francisco R. Ortega, Yujia Zheng, ChengXiang Zhai

Abstract: Instruction-following language models can comply with requests that conflict with intended behaviors and safety alignment. We introduce a training-free weight-editing approach for reducing compliance with targeted undesirable instructions. Our method needs only forward passes on a small set of prompt pairs per target behavior, and because the edit is stored in the model's weights, changing or deleting instructions in the prompt cannot undo the weight modification. We evaluate the approach on two target harmful behaviors across five instruction-tuned models: instructed falsehood and instructed sexism. On two instructed-lying benchmarks, it improves mean honest response rates by 6.2 and 4.6 percentage points over the strongest alternative, significantly on three of the five models and within noise on the other two. In a controlled study of seven prompt formats, it maintains higher honest-response rates than single-layer activation steering when orders to lie arrive in single-turn formats not used to construct the edit. For instructed sexism, it reduces the mean sexist-response rate from 57.9% to 24.3%; combining the edit with a fairness prompt reduces it further to 11.9%. These results establish our contrast-based weight editing approach as an effective training-free intervention in two distinct behavioral domains, supporting its use both independently and alongside prompt-based safeguards.

new Poisoning the corpora of protein design systems during pre-training and retrieval

Authors: Charalampos Koilakos, Kimon-Antonios Provatas, Michail Patsakis, Candace SY Chan, Georgios A. Pavlopoulos, Ilias Georgakopoulos-Soares

Abstract: Protein foundation models increasingly support the generation of novel biological sequences, yet their reliance on large public datasets creates opportunities for adversarial manipulation of the material they learn from and retrieve. We investigate two data-only poisoning attacks against protein design systems, targeting the pre-training corpus of autoregressive protein language models and the retrieval corpus of a text-guided protein generator. For the first, we pre-trained ProtGPT2 and ProGen2 variants of 300 million parameters, introducing poisoned protein sequences containing one of two triggers followed by one of three payloads. Across all poisoned runs, both architectures acquired highly reliable trigger-dependent generation under uniform and ramped poisoning schedules. Under ramped exposure, the earliest majority activation occurred after only 200 contaminated examples, while for the uniform schedule earliest activation occurred around 5000 poisoned samples. For the second attack, we inserted records into a retrieval corpus system without modifying its weights or decoding procedure. A single query-aware record captured a top-16 retrieval position for 58\% of templated requests and 97\% of free-text requests. Conditional on successful poisoned retrieval, 62\% of requests produced altered sequences, while 19-21% acquired the inserted domain by profile-HMM homology. A universal query-unaware attack using 1,500 records also achieved substantial retrieval capture and domain transfer. Together, these findings demonstrate that adversarial protein sequence contributions can establish persistent, sequence-specific behaviour during pre-training or redirect protein generation at inference, while leaving ordinary model behaviour largely preserved. Our work establishes the integrity and biological provenance of protein sequence data as critical safeguards for generative protein design systems.

new Hard Way, The Only Way: Provable Merits of Training on Hardest Examples

Authors: Elvis Dohmatob

Abstract: Despite a surge of empirical interest in the ``unreasonable effectiveness'' of drastic data pruning for AI systems, a unified theoretical understanding of this phenomenon remains elusive. In this work, we provide a precise high-dimensional theory of learning under data pruning via weighted empirical risk minimization (ERM). As in previous work, we analyze a setting where pruning is performed along a one-dimensional subspace of the input space. Our analysis precisely characterizes the limiting test error curves in the regime where the input dimension N and sample size P tend to infinity with a fixed ratio N/P. The resulting theory recovers several recent results in the literature as special cases and provides a general lens to evaluate how pruning strategies interact with the loss function and label noise. Crucially, we characterize the optimal pruning strategy in the high-sample regime where P greatly exceeds N. We demonstrate that strategic pruning can accelerate the decay of classification test error from the classical sqrt(N/P) rate, typical of vanilla ERM with loss functions without exponential tail (e.g., quadratic loss), to the much faster Bayes-optimal scaling of order N/P. In this setup, we prove that, surprisingly, this optimal performance is achieved by a drastic pruning strategy: discarding almost all training data except for a small proportion, consisting strictly of the 2N hardest training examples.

new Learning and Forecasting Multiscale Spatiotemporal Epidemic Dynamics with Lagrangian-Eulerian Graph Neural ODEs

Authors: Jinyi Duan, Qian Li, Biao Tang

Abstract: Accurate medium- to long-range epidemic forecasting requires extrapolating high-dimensional, heterogeneous and nonstationary dynamics from partial observations. Yet hybrid models often leave implicit which observations inform which epidemic processes and how learned graph interactions modify system state. We introduce LE-EpiGNODE, which replaces generic message passing with process-specific, mechanism-guided neural operators: age-contact information drives Lagrangian infection generation, whereas mobility drives conservative Eulerian population transport within a structure-preserving universal differential equation. We evaluated LE-EpiGNODE across 14 controlled synthetic mechanisms spanning time-modulated, state-dependent, nonlinear and coupled dynamics, three complementary COVID-19 settings, 21 comparator models and five targeted ablations. Forecasting gains tended to become more pronounced at longer horizons; at the longest horizon, LE-EpiGNODE achieved the lowest WMAPE in all three real-world datasets, reducing error by 17.7-40.0% relative to the best baseline. Controlled ablations linked these gains to the Lagrangian-Eulerian decomposition and process-aligned priors, while synthetic ground truth showed that learned operators and induced state fluxes track known generating dynamics. These results support mechanism-organized graph neural dynamics as a route to reliable long-horizon extrapolation with directly testable, process-level mechanistic interpretation beyond trajectory accuracy.

new Not All Change Is Damage: Update-Matched Unlearning for Large Language Models

Authors: Jie Li, Youming Tao, Bowei He, Meng Ding

Abstract: Unlearning aims to remove selected knowledge from large language models to mitigate privacy and safety risks while preserving useful capabilities. For models that continue to be fine-tuned after release, this goal is insufficient: they must also resist relearning the deleted knowledge while preserving plasticity, the ability to learn new tasks. Existing defenses train against simulated relearning while trying to preserve useful capabilities, but low relearning alone cannot distinguish selective resistance from a general loss of plasticity. Evaluating preservation after an update requires deciding what the updated model should remain close to. A pre-update reference penalizes useful changes induced by the update simply because they depart from earlier outputs. We therefore formulate the reference-timing hypothesis that, at comparable target suppression, an update-matched reference better preserves plasticity than a pre-update one. To test and address this hypothesis, we propose Update-Matched Unlearning (UMU), which applies the same simulated relearning update to the defended model and to an undefended reference initialized from the same unlearned model, and uses new-task learning to guide model selection. Extensive experiments on TOFU and FaithUn with Llama and Qwen models show that UMU improves both relearning resistance and plasticity across all three base unlearning methods, lowering harmful and benign relearning by 30.4 and 9.9 points on average while raising new-task accuracy by 3.5 points. When the reference does not receive the update, relearning remains similarly low but new-task accuracy falls below the base method, supporting the reference-timing hypothesis and showing that robust unlearning should prevent recovery selectively without suppressing learning itself.

new Compatibility Matters: A Framework for Token-Level Teacher Selection in On-Policy Distillation

Authors: Stanley Wei, Jiin Woo, Kaan Ozkara, Youngsuk Park

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) has recently been introduced as a promising paradigm for continual learning, where a student policy can be supervised at the token-level. Typically, OPD is performed using a single teacher per prompt rollout (or even per training run). However, this ignores a whole other axis of flexibility that is available due to the on-policy nature - token-level teacher selection during rollouts. Building on this insight, we theoretically explore what makes a teacher and a student compatible for OPD, and leverage our findings to derive several empirical heuristics for teacher selection. In particular, our framework captures several desiderata for OPD: compatible teacher-student reasoning patterns, teacher competence, and induced student diversity. Theoretically, we categorize several types of teacher routing schemes, and we prove that under different scenarios, different routing schemes are likely to be beneficial for optimization and student diversity. We back up our findings with extensive experimental results in domains such as math, coding, kernel generation, and visual reasoning, where we find upwards of 10 percentage points of improvement on pass@$k$ when compared to vanilla OPD and the teacher experts on certain tasks, indicating both capability improvement and diversity over high-quality responses.

new Representation Redistribution in Sparse Autoencoders

Authors: Ziye Mei, Haotian Ma

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) decompose language model activations into sparse features, but prompt transformations designed to preserve the underlying request can redistribute activity across them. A feature change observed on one transformed prompt need not recur, and even a recurring change need not affect a downstream computation. We introduce the redistribution sensitivity score (RSS), which identifies large feature responses that recur in a consistent direction, and evaluate the resulting supports on variants excluded from selection. Across 27 pretrained SAEs spanning eight language models, the tested prompt transformations produce a reproducible redistribution profile on these excluded variants. We then separate recurring representation change from signed readout contribution and decision failure. For a fixed linear readout, an exact decomposition shows how response magnitude, readout coefficients, directional alignment, and the clean margin determine downstream consequences. In two frozen safety readouts, combining RSS support erosion with the full clean margin yields recurrent failure AUROCs of 0.935 and 0.958 using median supports of 20 and 19 features, compared with 0.965 and 0.964 using full erosion. RSS therefore provides a way to identify recurring sparse representation changes and quantify their contribution to a downstream readout without equating representation change with failure. Code is available at https://github.com/OpenSAE/Redistribution-Sensitivity-Score

URLs: https://github.com/OpenSAE/Redistribution-Sensitivity-Score

new Isolating a Language Model's Judgment with a Label-Free Subspace

Authors: Chaemin Jang, Donggyu Lee, Jeesoo Kim, Dongman Lee, Jihee Kim

Abstract: Language models are often asked to make judgments: whether a proposed answer is correct, whether one statement follows logically from another, or whether a response is safe. Yet changing the instructions that specify how the judgment should be expressed can make the final answer less reliable, even for the same input. We ask whether this reflects a change in internal judgment or a failure to express it. Comparing hidden-state vectors for the same input under different instructions, we find that their differences concentrate in a low-dimensional expression subspace. We identify this subspace without benchmark labels and remove its contribution from a linear predictor to obtain an isolated judgment. Across correctness, entailment, and safety, the isolated judgment remains reliable under instructions not used to identify the subspace, even when the model's final answer and the original predictor degrade. When the judgment question is negated, isolation raises the predictor's AUROC from 0.504 to 0.825 for entailment and from 0.480 to 0.891 for safety. We also find that judgment is readable in earlier layers, before the expression subspace begins to disrupt the predictor. Together, these results provide a way to separate judgment from expression and recover internal judgments even when the model's final answers are unreliable.

new Fractional Power-of-Two Quantization for Efficient and Effective Multiplier-Free LLM Inference

Authors: Sunghyun Wee, Geunjae Choi, Hyeonjin Kim, Suyoung Kim, Nojun Kwak

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) demand efficient inference, where the multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations in linear projections dominate compute and energy cost. Post-training quantization (PTQ) reduces this cost by mapping weights and activations to low-bit integers. Power-of-Two (PoT) weight quantization promises efficient multiplier-free LLM inference via shift-add logic, but suffers significant accuracy degradation at low bitwidths due to its coarse base-2 quantization grid. We introduce Fractional PoT (FPoT), a base-$\sqrt{2}$ (half-octave) fractional PoT grid that achieves practical 4-bit weight quantization while preserving a fully integer shift-add datapath. The method combines (i) the base-$\sqrt{2}$ weight grid with uniform integer activations, (ii) a calibration-time grid-alignment refinement absorbed into the static per-channel scale, and (iii) a lightweight 1-adder $\sqrt{2}$ approximation whose error is absorbed during weight calibration via approximate-grid quantization, yielding a processing element (PE) with significantly fewer gates than a 4-bit integer multiplier. On Llama-3-8B with 4-bit weight and activation quantization, FPoT improves zero-shot commonsense reasoning performance by 8.72% relative to the PoT baseline, while maintaining performance close to the uniform integer baseline, with only a negligible 0.4%p degradation. These results hold across various configurations spanning seven models and three bit-widths. Furthermore, FPoT can be integrated with state-of-the-art LLM PTQ methods, demonstrating framework-agnostic applicability.

new Behavioral Compositionality of Sparse Autoencoder Representations

Authors: Haotian Ma, Ziye Mei, Frederic Sala

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) represent model activations with sparse, linearly decoded features, but the downstream effects of those features need not compose additively. We introduce SAE Additivity Residuals (SAR), which measure the behavioral variation left unexplained above a chosen interaction order. Under independent feature subset sampling, population SAR is exactly the normalized energy of interactions above that order and bounds coalition ordering error for the optimal low order approximation. To distinguish compositionality associated with the SAE representation from that induced by downstream computation, we compare learned SAE coordinates with sum preserving random rotations of the same selected subspace and find lower first order residuals for the learned coordinates across all 18 model and behavior settings. The coordinates are nevertheless not fully additive: pairwise terms improve held out coalition ordering in every setting and outperform parameter matched models using randomly selected third order terms. In a combined steering experiment, pairwise modeling raises rank fidelity from 0.5874 to 0.7790 and reduces ordering error from 0.1444 to 0.0606. Together, these results show that SAE coordinates support comparatively compositional behavioral descriptions while faithful joint explanations and interventions still require interactions beyond individual feature effects. Code is available at https://github.com/OpenSAE/SAE-Behavioral-Compositionality.

URLs: https://github.com/OpenSAE/SAE-Behavioral-Compositionality.

new Rethinking Learning from Label Proportions via Moment Matching

Authors: Tianhao Ma, Wei Wang, Yivan Zhang, Dong-Dong Wu, Takashi Ishida, Gang Niu, Masashi Sugiyama

Abstract: Learning from label proportions (LLP) is a weakly supervised setting where training data are grouped into bags with only bag-level label proportions observed, and the goal is to learn a classifier that predicts labels for individual instances. Recently, a large body of methods has emerged; however, most of them rely on the assumption that instance-label pairs are sampled i.i.d. and randomly assembled into bags, an assumption often violated in real-world scenarios. Our experimental results indicate that these methods perform suboptimally under non-random settings. In this work, we adopt a more realistic assumption: bags are sampled i.i.d., and instance labels are conditionally independent given the instances. Under this setting, we show that the label counts follow a Poisson multinomial distribution. Motivated by this observation, we propose LLP via moment matching (LLP-MM), a simple yet effective approach which leverages multi-order factorial moments as training objectives, encouraging the classifier predictions to match these moments and thereby more fully exploit the underlying statistical structure of the data. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets under various bag construction strategies demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach while maintaining high computational efficiency. Our code is available at https://github.com/TianhaoMa5/LLP-MM.

URLs: https://github.com/TianhaoMa5/LLP-MM.

new Regret Lower Bound for Online Learning of Quantum States with Logarithmic Loss, with Applications to Quantum State Tomography and Confidence Sequences for Matrix Means

Authors: Yen-Huan Li, Wei-Fu Tseng

Abstract: We study the problem of online learning of quantum states with logarithmic loss, which is a quantum generalization of online portfolio selection and has applications to quantum state tomography, the task of estimating an unknown quantum state from measurement outcomes. We first establish a regret lower bound, showing that the regret rates of Exponential Weights and the algorithm of Tseng et al. (2025) have optimal polynomial dependence on the dimension. Our analysis leads to two further results. First, our lower-bound proof leads to a quantum state tomography algorithm that is sample optimal up to polylogarithmic factors in the sample size. Second, we develop an algorithm for constructing confidence sequences for the mean of a matrix-valued random variable. A numerical experiment shows that the latter algorithm is competitive with existing methods.

new TimeCap: A Scalable Open-Source Pipeline for Captioning Wearable Health Time-Series Data

Authors: Max Rosenblattl, Milana Gurbanova, Karl Aurel Deck, Narayan Schuetz, Thomas Kaar, Manuel Milling, Nicolas Zumarraga, Ning Wang, Kelly W. Zhang, Anders Johnson, Robert Jakob, Bj\"orn W. Schuller, Elgar Fleisch, Daniel Seung Kim, Allan Lawrie, Patrick Langer, Maxwell A. Xu, Euan Ashley, Paul Schmiedmayer

Abstract: Time-series data generated continuously across industrial, environmental, and especially health domains produce multimodal signals whose scale and heterogeneity make their processing and interpretation particularly challenging. Time-Series Language Models (TSLMs) promise scalable interpretation of these signals, yet remain bottlenecked by a fundamental lack of text-annotated time series pairs that are signal-grounded, reproducible, and extensible across modalities and schemas. Existing paired corpora are either closed or limited in scale and modality coverage. Annotation with large language models could scale them up, but its outputs cannot be regenerated exactly, which prevents others from re-deriving, extending, or auditing the resulting corpus. To address this, we release TimeCap, an open deterministic captioning pipeline with three reusable extractor tiers and a cross-channel synthesis layer that operates directly on the signal with no language model in the loop, extensible to new schemas by writing channel definitions alone. This positions TimeCap as a reproducible mechanism for future dataset generation rather than a one-off release. Applying TimeCap to OpenMHC, the MyHeart Counts wearable dataset, yields MHCap, the largest open caption layer for multimodal wearable time-series data. MHCap spans 19 Apple HealthKit channels and 7,005,763 captions over 908,944 person-days. To showcase the impact of the corpus, we train OpenTSLM, a time-series language model, on MHCap and benchmark on health-outcome prediction. Caption-augmented training (OpenTSLM-Cap) outperforms a label-only baseline on 29 of 32 per-user binary evaluations across 8 tasks.

new Can Capability Enhancement be Localized?

Authors: Anh Tuan Nguyen, Anna Bair, Zico Kolter

Abstract: Transformer-based language models have shown strong capabilities across many tasks and domains. However, prior work shows that, for a given task, not all attention heads matter equally. Specifically, they suggest that many \textit{redundant} heads can be safely removed with little-to-no effect, while a small set of \textit{important} heads, when ablated, severely damages the model's performance on the given task. In this work, we investigate a complementary aspect, called \textit{Capability Enhancement Localization} (CEL): \textit{given a model and a task, does there exist a small set of heads whose removal improves the task performance?} We first investigate this question \textit{empirically} across models from different families. Using a simple two-stage routine that screens candidate heads and evaluates their joint ablations, we identify sparse head combinations whose removal improves task-specific performance without further fine-tuning. Motivated by these observations, we ask \textit{theoretically whether such a phenomenon can naturally arise from training dynamics, and under what conditions}. To this end, we construct a simplified multi-head softmax attention model and two tasks with conflicting features, and show that gradient descent from small random initialization provably concentrates the conflicting features into particular heads. We further show that ablating such heads improves performance on one task while harming the other, even though training converges to a global optimum of the joint objective. This establishes a possible training-induced theoretical explanation for CEL.

new Personalized Projected Ascent for Federated Stackelberg Optimization

Authors: M Yashwanth, Sharannya Ghosh, Saikiran Bulusu, Anirban Chakraborty

Abstract: Federated minimax problems, such as distributionally robust optimization and adversarial training, include an inner maximization variable built from each client's local data. These variables differ in meaning and dimension from one client to the next, so averaging them across clients, as standard federated minimax methods do, is ill-posed. We therefore study a federated minimax problem where the minimization variable $\mathbf{x}$ is shared and aggregated, while each client retains the maximization variable $\mathbf{y}_i$ (personalized). Because these applications constrain $\mathbf{y}_i$ to a compact convex set, we analyze projected ascent directly. We show that, in the nonconvex-concave regime, this projected personalized scheme attains the standard sublinear rate. In the nonconvex-PL regime, however, it hits a stepsize-invariant noise floor due to stochastic $\mathbf{y}_i$ updates. To mitigate this, we propose VR-SGDA, incorporating a variance-reduced ascent step where each client maintains a private variable snapshot. By evaluating current and snapshot gradients on the same sample, the constant variance is replaced by a vanishing drift under a two-timescale regime. VR-SGDA eliminates the noise floor, attaining sublinear leading rates while preserving personalization and projection. Experiments on subpopulation-robustness and NLP adversarial training confirm both the worst-group robustness benefits and the two-timescale tracking mechanism.

new PocketTSLM: Deploying an On-Device Soft-Prompt Time-Series Language Model for Privacy-Preserving Smartphone Reasoning

Authors: Max Rosenblattl, Nikhil Krishnaswamy, Leon Nissen, Manuel Milling, Nicolas Zumarraga, Ning Wang, Thomas Kaar, Robert Jakob, Bj\"orn W. Schuller, Elgar Fleisch, Patrick Langer, Paul Schmiedmayer

Abstract: Consumer phones and wearables increasingly collect physiological time-series data, yet most mobile systems treat these streams as summaries or text rather than as temporal evidence. This representation is poorly matched to personal health systems that need to retrieve, route, or summarize sensor evidence while preserving temporal structure. Cloud-hosted time-series language models avoid smartphone limits, but they repeatedly move sensitive physiology off the device and add latency and per-query compute cost. Mobile model alternatives that aggregate time-series data into statistical features or serialize raw samples as text either compress temporal structure or exhaust the context and memory budgets of small on-device models. We present PocketTSLM, a mobile-native deployment of OpenTSLM SoftPrompt, the soft-prompt variant of the OpenTSLM time-series language model. PocketTSLM runs fully on a consumer smartphone by injecting encoded time-series windows as embeddings instead of tokenizing time series as text. A numerical parity protocol verifies identical greedy-decoded token sequences between the Swift port and a Python reference using Apple's MLX machine-learning framework. Answers also match the PyTorch reference, although rationale wording can differ. An on-device context-economics benchmark compares the soft-prompt path with a text-serialized baseline on the same backbone. In our benchmark format, soft-prompting uses approximately 12 times fewer time-series tokens and reduces prompt-prefill cost. PocketTSLM processes 30-second Sleep-EDF electroencephalography clips for sleep staging and a 1,000-sample (10 s) 12-lead electrocardiogram clip from ECG-QA. In comparison, text-serialized baselines exceed the phone's context or memory budget at longer time-series windows. All inference runs entirely on the device, with no health data transmitted during inference.

new Spectrum Is Not Enough: A Random-Matrix Account of Data-Free Preconditioning for Differentially Private Learning

Authors: Shilin Zhang, Kangning Wang

Abstract: Data-free preconditioning builds the Kronecker factors of KFAC from synthetic probes instead of private data. DP-KFC validates a probe by matching the eigenvalue decay of the private factors; we turn this criterion into a random-matrix account of what a probe can reconstruct. A single dichotomy organizes it: in the factorization $ A \otimes G $, the error-signal factor $ G $ and the architecture-determined bulk of the activation factor $ A $ are reconstructible without data, while only the data-induced spikes of $ A $ are not. We prove the $ G $ half is data-insensitive up to an explicit error-energy bound, with zero violations over a full training trajectory across four modalities and a natural-image benchmark. We locate the failure boundary at a Baik-Ben Arous-P\'ech\'e threshold: a bulk-only probe suffers an exact damage $s^2/(1-s^2)$ from a data-induced spike, which becomes invisible once it exceeds $(d/n)^{1/4}$, confirmed on an injected-spike surrogate. Holding the probe spectrum fixed and varying only its eigenbasis, we show that spectral-shape matching is neither sufficient nor the operative object: a probe with a near-exact matched spectrum (log-ratio dispersion $0.02$) still inflates the residual $2 \times $-$90 \times $ when its eigenvectors are random. A closed-form whitened-volume law explains this and predicts the within-spectrum ordering of the critical volume, though not its cross-dataset magnitude. Downstream, per-layer tuning does not transfer to accuracy and the headroom is a small oracle-minus-white gap. Under a privacy mechanism the source ordering survives while learning does, then inverts at a noise cross-over where the flat probe overtakes the matched ones.

new Ranking and Partitioning of Attention Heads for Sparse and Continual Reinforcement Learning Fine-tuning

Authors: Ruizhe Shi, Zaiwen Yang, Qiubo Wang, Banghua Zhu, Maryam Fazel

Abstract: We study sparse and continual reinforcement learning fine-tuning of language models through attention-head ranking and partitioning. For sparse learning, we rank attention heads by gradient norm to identify a small subset of heads that can be trained effectively. For continual learning, we partition heads across tasks to reduce cross-task interference and preserve previously learned capabilities. Theoretically, we relate gradient norm to training effectiveness, and further characterize why sparse, separated head updates can remain stable. Empirically, across math, instruction-following, and coding tasks, tuning higher-ranked heads consistently outperforms tuning lower-ranked ones while often matching denser baselines, and head partitioning improves retention while preserving adaptation to new tasks.

new Does On-Policy Distillation Need to Complete Every Rollout?

Authors: Zhengxin Zhang, Maosen Tang, Ning Wang, Changhao Li, Jingren Zhou, Bo Dai, Sainyam Galhotra, Alex Townsend, Claire Cardie

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) effectively transfers capabilities from a teacher to a student through feedback on student-generated rollouts. However, generating complete student rollouts and computing teacher feedback for every token make OPD computationally expensive. We introduce Stochastic Truncated On-Policy Distillation (STOPD), which accelerates OPD by randomly truncating student rollouts and correcting the resulting sampling bias with blockwise inverse-probability weighting (IPW). At each training step, we sample a stopping position for each rollout before generation and let the student generate until that position or natural termination. We obtain teacher feedback on every generated token and use blockwise IPW to compensate for the lower probability of retaining later tokens while controlling the weights. We prove that this weighting preserves Vanilla OPD's gradient sum in expectation and derive one-step update-error bounds for SGD, Adam, and Muon. We evaluate STOPD across multiple teacher-student model pairs, task domains, and optimizers. STOPD reduces generated tokens by 25-48% while maintaining accuracy comparable to Vanilla OPD, achieving 1.3-2.2x training speedups.

new Online Quantile Omniprediction for Proper Losses

Authors: Hyunsuk Kim, Isaac Gibbs, Michael W. Mahoney, Ryan J. Tibshirani

Abstract: The task of constructing a predictor that is simultaneously accurate under multiple evaluation metrics has been popularized recently under the name omniprediction. Prior work in this area has largely focused on binary prediction tasks, and existing results for multiclass and continuous outcomes typically suffer from slow error rates. In this paper, we consider forecasts expressed as a set of quantiles over a discrete but otherwise arbitrary set of probability levels. We develop sample-efficient algorithms for constructing forecasts that are accurate simultaneously across all proper losses that satisfy mild regularity conditions, where proper losses are those minimized by predicting the true quantiles. Our algorithms are online and can operate in a dynamic environment, while simultaneously taking multiple losses into account, and their forecasts offer the potential to provide diverse end-users with rich information for evaluating risk and informing a range of practical decision-making tasks. As an example, we demonstrate how our procedure can be used to ensemble quantile forecasts of COVID-19 hospitalizations made by experts during the pandemic.

new Honesty is the Best Policy ... or is it? Demand Management for Probabilistic Service Options

Authors: Daniela Sailer, Robert Klein, Claudius Steinhardt

Abstract: To enable well-informed customer choices and redistribute demand across service options, providers can display probabilities regarding predicted service levels at the time of booking, effectively offering probabilistic service options. In many service settings, the underlying truthful probabilities endogenously depend on the system state, rather than being static product attributes controlled by the provider. While customers rely on this information when making decisions, they cannot readily verify its accuracy. Consequently, providers may be tempted to manipulate the displayed probabilities to guide customers' choices in their favor, creating a novel lever for demand management. In this work, we model the resulting problem as a sequential decision process, define truthful probabilities recursively, and introduce transparency constraints that limit their distortion. Because the exact computation of truthful probabilities is intractable for realistic settings, we develop an adapted reinforcement learning algorithm to approximate them. Furthermore, we propose a sampling-based mixed-integer program to optimize the information communicated by the provider in each state. Our framework enables providers to gauge the benefits of dynamically disclosing probabilistic information at the time of booking and sheds light on incentives for manipulation. Our computational study is motivated by out-of-home delivery, i.e., the delivery to parcel lockers or shops, and reveals a trade-off between individual-level transparency and aggregate customer utilities.

new Same Target, Different Origins: Aligning Latent Rollout for Consistent PDE Emulation

Authors: Xinying Li, Jing Xu, Wenjun Wang, Limei Han, Fenglei Cao, Ruoxi Jiang, Yuan Qi

Abstract: Neural PDE solvers commonly generate multi-step predictions through autoregressive rollout, recursively applying a learned evolution operator and feeding each prediction back as input for the next step. Approximation errors can therefore propagate through the rollout, causing predictions at a shared target time to depend on their temporal origins and rollout depths. For a well-posed deterministic PDE flow, however, states at different times along the same physical trajectory should, under exact evolution, reach the same target state. To this end, we propose Align Latent Rollout (ALR), a regularization approach that promotes consistency across rollout origins through the neural PDE solver's internal representations. A second-order Taylor analysis of latent discrepancy propagation motivates two objectives: zeroth-order latent state consistency, which aligns representations at a shared target time, and first-order latent dynamics consistency, which aligns local evolution responses to shared perturbations. ALR can be integrated into different neural PDE solvers by regularizing suitable internal representations, without modifying their backbone architectures. Experiments across multiple PDE benchmarks and neural-operator backbones show that ALR improves prediction accuracy while reducing shared-target discrepancies across rollout origins.

new Memorilla: Latent Semantic Memory for LLMs

Authors: Adibvafa Fallahpour, Parsa Idehpour, Vignesh Kothapalli, Nikita Mounier, Shirley Wu, Shayan Pardis, Jure Leskovec

Abstract: Memory enables large language models (LLMs) to use information beyond their parameters, such as past user conversations, long documents, or an agent's earlier actions. For a frozen LLM, access to this accumulated knowledge is limited to what a memory system places in its context window. However, gathering and combining relevant information needed for each query while keeping the model's context short remains challenging for existing systems. Here we introduce Memorilla, a memory system that gathers information from an entire document collection into a fixed number of query-dependent 'memory tokens' for a frozen LLM. Memorilla encodes documents (such as user chats, book chapters) once into reusable embeddings, then learns to retrieve and place query-specific information in the LLM's input embedding space. Using only 16 memory tokens, Memorilla achieves the best performance against baselines including Mem0 and MemoRAG on five of eight benchmarks spanning personalization, long-document and knowledge-intensive question answering. Notably, on FactKG, it reaches 84.33% accuracy versus 80.43% with full-context inference, while using 108x fewer tokens to represent the source documents. Furthermore, Memorilla can facilitate continual learning in agentic systems where the document count (the agent's past turns) increases throughout the trajectory. In a TextWorld environment, we show that Memorilla reduces the agent's tendency to wander between rooms it has already explored, and improves its average success rate from 18.6% with full-history in-context learning to 24.2%. Overall, Memorilla aids frozen LLMs in accumulating and using knowledge effectively across long-context interactions and takes a step towards building continually learning AI systems.

new Does Compression Imply Generalization? A Minimum Description Length Perspective

Authors: Lincen Yang, Jiayang Shi, Nan Pu, Dani\"el M. Pelt, Matthijs van Leeuwen, Zhong Li, Shujian Yu

Abstract: A central question in modern learning theory is what notion of compression, if any, explains why over-parameterized neural networks generalize. Existing answers measure complexity at different levels, ranging from weights to representations; however, estimating the associated quantities either requires held-out data in principle or relies on the challenging (ultra) high-dimensional mutual information estimation. To overcome these challenges, we approach the question through the minimum description length (MDL) principle, and specifically, we propose the prequential regret as a complementary signal that is computable from the training trajectory alone. We establish that, in the (nearly) interpolating regime, the expected prequential regret upper-bounds the description length of the trained parameter under the algorithm-induced distribution, and that this description length translates into a high-probability generalization bound in the PAC-MDL setting. Along the way, we show that the encoder-complexity correction recently added to the algorithm-level information bottleneck (IB) is upper-bounded by an MDL parameter description length, \new{bringing together} the two views of compression from MDL and IB. Empirically, the prequential regret tracks the generalization error on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100, with Pearson correlations exceeding 0.9.

new RDL-Bench: A Benchmark for Task-Aware Question Answering over Relational Databases

Authors: Yao Cheng, Siqiang Luo

Abstract: Relational databases (RDBs) remain the primary infrastructure for storing and managing structured data across enterprise and scientific domains. Natural-language (NL) interfaces to RDBs aim to let users ask questions without writing SQL, but real user needs go beyond SQL-style lookup: some questions require reasoning over the observable database state, while others require predictive inference from historical relational records under temporal constraints. Existing benchmarks address these goals in isolation. To address this gap, we introduce RDL-Bench, a benchmark for task-aware question answering (QA) over real RDBs. RDL-Bench organizes diverse user questions into reasoning-oriented questions, whose answers are determined by the current database state, and prediction-oriented questions, whose answers depend on future outcomes under temporal constraints. Each instance contains an NL question, task metadata, schema information, temporal context, either executable evidence or target-construction logic, and denotation-level ground truth. RDL-Bench supports two evaluation settings: native solvers under their intended capabilities, and task-aware modular systems where a router dispatches each question to an appropriate execution module.

new Fault Detection and Bit-Error Rate Diagnostics in Optical Wireless Links Using Edge-AI

Authors: Sarthak Bhattarai, Sheshadri Shikhar Bandyopadhyay, Suprabh Dhakal, Jitendra Kumar Manandhar

Abstract: Optical Wireless Communication (OWC) systems provide high-bandwidth, spatially secure, and RF-interference-free digital transmission. However, direct free-space optical links suffer from extreme sensitivity to line-of-sight disruptions, optical attenuation, high ambient light saturation, and transmitter-receiver misalignment. Traditional channel diagnostic frameworks rely on external computing nodes or cloud-based processing, which introduces severe diagnostic latency and reduces edge node autonomy. This paper presents an autonomous, self-diagnosing optical wireless receiver node that integrates physical-layer signal telemetry extraction with an on-device TinyML classification engine. Built around an ESP32-S3 microcontroller, the receiver recovers 1000 bit/s On-Off Keying (OOK) digital payloads using a 5-sample majority voting mechanism while concurrently extracting real-time channel features: Bit Error Rate (BER), received signal voltage (Vsignal), Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNRdB), and ambient light baseline (Lambient). Evaluated against a Random Forest baseline (77.22% accuracy) and a floating-point neural network (78.48% accuracy), a post-training INT8 quantized TensorFlow Lite Micro model occupying only 3.32 kB of SRAM runs directly on the receiver firmware. The quantized model achieves 74.68% overall accuracy across four distinct operational conditions: normal transmission, optical filter attenuation, ambient light interference, and severe obstruction/misalignment. On-device deployment tests confirm real-time, deterministic fault classification, establishing a practical approach for self-aware optical IoT nodes.

new Multi-Reward Hacking: From Aggregation-Induced Drift to Trust-Anchored Defense

Authors: Yushu Li, Jiin Woo, Xiaoxiao Li, Youngsuk Park

Abstract: To improve task performance while encouraging desirable response properties, LLM post-training often combines a primary reward (e.g., correctness) with auxiliary rewards (e.g., brevity or efficiency). However, how these rewards are aggregated can critically affect training outcomes. We show that symmetric aggregation, which treats reward channels equally, can induce a failure where aggregate reward improves while primary-task performance degrades, even when individual rewards are meaningful. We investigate this aggregation-induced failure through a one-step multi-reward decision formulation, characterizing when it constitutes multi-reward hacking. We prove that, under symmetric aggregation, deterministic conditional-mean GRPO converges to auxiliary-favoring behavior when auxiliary rewards are more reliably obtained, regardless of initialization. We propose Trust-Anchored Gating (TAG), which first elects an anchor channel based on the policy drift induced by optimizing each channel alone, then gates auxiliary credit on anchor satisfaction. Unlike calibration-sensitive static reweighting, TAG structurally eliminates credit for auxiliary-only outcomes while preserving additional credit for joint success. In the same analytical setting, TAG provably reverses this selection, converging to the primary-favoring action from any initialization when the primary channel is anchored. Experiments across Qwen and Llama models on mathematics and program synthesis reveal substantial degradation under symmetric, constrained, and normalized aggregation, whereas TAG mitigates this failure. On Qwen3-4B with a brevity reward, TAG achieves 0.581 pass@4 versus 0.220 for additive aggregation, while shortening responses by 44% relative to correctness-only training.

new Learning to Forecast on Multiple Spatiotemporal Hierarchies

Authors: Valentina Moretti, Cesare Alippi, Andrea Cini

Abstract: Hierarchical forecasting exploits aggregation structures that expose multiple views of the same underlying spatiotemporal forecasting problem, while ensuring coherence among forecasts. Individual series provide fine-grained information, while progressively coarser aggregates reveal temporal and spatial patterns at different scales. In the associated deep learning architectures, the hierarchy determines which aggregate series are available and how they are processed together with individual series (e.g., through reconciliation or message-passing operators). Different hierarchies thus provide distinct learning signals and inductive biases for forecasting the same collection of time series. Nonetheless, existing architectures are typically trained on a single, often fixed, structure. In this work, we propose a graph deep learning framework that leverages variations across hierarchies as multi-task regularization. We show that jointly learning across these structures can reduce over-specialization to any single hierarchy and improve forecasting performance. Indeed, training across hierarchies provides an inductive bias toward representations that are useful across different structures, and as such, generalize better. Making forecasts coherent with the aggregation constraints for each sampled hierarchy is computationally expensive: to complement the framework, we propose a novel operator-based on a top-down approach-that produces coherent forecasts with linear time and space complexity w.r.t. the hierarchy size. Experiments show that our approach improves forecasting accuracy over training on a single structure and is competitive with current state-of-the-art methods.

new What Does History Add? Calendar Controls and Information-Timing Audits for University Trajectory Forecasting

Authors: Zepei Cao

Abstract: We audit the predictive value of historical summaries in a rule-based university trajectory simulator. Seventeen ridge specifications and persistence separate current plans, past-state and past-plan summaries, and calendar-only history-length indicators. Across training, validation and two family-held-out evaluations, we reconstruct 16,750 routes and 804,000 monthly records. On the original 40-family set, six-month history lowers six-step normalized RMSE from 0.159234 to 0.151522, but its difference from a length-only control is -0.000895 (descriptive paired 95% interval [-0.001957,0.000248]). Fixed weights on 30 families unused in identified prior tasks reproduce this pattern: 0.156480 to 0.150074 versus current-state prediction, but -0.000558 [-0.001783,0.000728] versus the length control. These uncertain comparisons do not generalize to all-history summaries, which improve on their own calendar control. Exploratory decomposition finds clearer preparation-index and funds benefits, whereas fatigue dominates the six-month model's loss and has an uncertain length-controlled benefit. Moreover, 28.20% of its six-step fatigue predictions violate the simulator's [5,90] support. We document family dependence, prior exposure, strict temporal indexing, matched recursive references and independent numerical replay. The task conditions on recorded future plans; it does not evaluate real-student forecasts, causal action effects, autonomous planning or language-model memory. This is an evaluation audit, not a new memory architecture.

new Each Reward to Its Relevant Tokens: Reward-Specific Token-Level Credit Assignment for Multi-Reward RL

Authors: Skylar Zhai, Haonan Wang, Zhining Liu, Linxin Song, Xuwei Ding, Jingcheng Liang, Jian Kang

Abstract: Language models are increasingly trained against several verifiable rewards at once, such as the correctness and the format of a tool call, or the individual constraints of a complex instruction. Reward-decoupled normalization (GDPO) normalizes each reward separately so that different reward combinations keep distinct advantages, but it still spreads every reward's advantage evenly over the whole response, although each verifier checks only a few positions. We propose MRTPO, which assigns the advantage of each reward to the tokens relevant to it, using the information its verifier checks, without auxiliary models or token-level annotations. For each reward, the policy replays its own response with and without that reward's verifier information, and the per-token Jensen-Shannon distance between the two next-token distributions measures each token's relevance and reassigns the advantage. On text-to-SQL, multi-constraint instruction following and tool calling, MRTPO converges faster and achieves better performance than the compared methods.

new V-SHARE: Provable Convergence Guarantees for Advantage-based Algorithms

Authors: Kintan Saha, Hamed Masoudi, Xinyi Sheng, Dominik Baumann, Joni Pajarinen, Arsenii Mustafin

Abstract: In reinforcement learning, advantage-based value-learning methods, unlike standard Q-learning, share information across actions by decomposing the action-value function into a state-dependent value component and action-specific advantages. While this mechanism has proven effective in both tabular and deep reinforcement learning, its theoretical understanding remains incomplete. We introduce V-SHARE, an advantage-based value-learning algorithm that combines cross-action value sharing with an importance-weighted Bellman-residual correction. To our knowledge, we provide the first complete theoretical characterization of an advantage-based algorithm, including both asymptotic convergence and non-asymptotic finite-sample guarantees. We identify a regime in which V-SHARE mitigates the rare-action bottleneck of asynchronous Q-learning and uncover a contraction--variance tradeoff between stronger deterministic contraction and increased importance-weighting noise. Motivated by this tradeoff, we develop decreasing residual-preconditioning schedules that combine faster early contraction with lower asymptotic noise. We further develop a two-timescale analysis that removes the single-timescale residual-weight threshold while preserving convergence guarantees. Experiments on discrete Markov decision processes (MDPs) and deep reinforcement-learning benchmarks corroborate our results.

new Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks: Curse of Dimensionality and Structural Bias

Authors: Yunhao Ni, Yuxin Guo, Jie Luo, Wenjun Wu, Lei Huang

Abstract: Kolmogorov-Arnold networks (KANs) have a dimension-independent approximation exponent when the target admits a sufficiently smooth representation by univariate edge functions. We ask whether this favorable scaling belongs to the architecture or to the assumed target class. For arbitrary-depth standard spline-KANs, we prove a worst-case lower bound on isotropic Sobolev balls: below a dimension-independent accuracy threshold, the required number of spline coefficients grows at least exponentially with input dimension. Existing composition-matched MLP results, by contrast, show that favorable exponents on low-arity targets are not exclusive to KANs. We also revisit empirical comparisons. On four scaling-law targets, spline-KAN approximants constructed from known decompositions transfer exactly to MLPs with the cubic ReLU activation $\sigma(x)=(\max\{0,x\})^3$, although their full dense parameter counts are larger. In controlled ridge-profile scans, KANs have a strong advantage when each nonlinear component depends on one input coordinate. When a component couples multiple input coordinates, this advantage may narrow sharply. At tight budgets, interacting targets often favor MLPs. In the random-label test on MNIST, ReLU-MLPs fit the randomized training labels more efficiently than spline-only KANs. Together, these results locate favorable scaling in target structure and a compatible inductive bias, not a general KAN-specific escape from the curse of dimensionality.

new Phase Kernel Lifts Capacity of Dense Associative Memory

Authors: Yifei Zhao, Ying Tang

Abstract: Dense associative memory underlies modern attention mechanisms; however, Modern Hopfield Networks (MHNs) with linear kernels yield overlapping attractor basins and spurious states under high memory load, limiting their theoretically exponential storage capacity in practice. We construct a class of nonlinear phase kernels that modify the energy landscape and retrieval dynamics, and prove that these kernels preserve exponential storage capacity and local fixed-point convergence under given conditions on kernel smoothness, memory separation, and memory distributions. The framework is instantiated by the Sigmoidal Phase Hopfield Network (S-Hop), which guarantees monotone energy descent and mitigates practical capacity loss. S-Hop has $8.3\times$ and $4\times$ gains in one-step noisy retrieval load over Uniform Hopfield Networks (U-Hop) on CIFAR-10 and TinyImageNet respectively, and reaches a $50\times$ gain in first-failure load for ImageNet. The results demonstrate that phase kernels provide a principled route to robust dense associative memory.

new Activation-Adjacent Layer Normalization: Effects on Approximation Power

Authors: Yunhao Ni, Yuxin Guo, Jie Luo, Wenjun Wu, Lei Huang

Abstract: Layer normalization (LN) and root mean square normalization (RMSNorm) depend on their inputs and remain nonlinear at inference. Inserting either next to an activation may therefore change the function a network represents. We study when shallow activation-normalized (AN) and preactivation-normalized (PN) networks can approximate a prescribed width-$N$ one-hidden-layer MLP. Viewing RMSNorm as input-dependent scaling, we use width augmentation to locally linearize normalization on compact feature sets. For AN networks, an unbounded activation range allows width $N+1$, while every continuous nonconstant activation admits width $N+r$ with error $O_f(r^{-1})$. For PN networks, affine parameters or, without them, degree-one positive homogeneity recover width $N+1$; general continuous activations admit width $N+r$ with error tending to zero. The corresponding LN constructions use centered coordinate pairs, yielding widths $N+2$ and $N+2r$, respectively. We prove that $N+1$ for RMSNorm and $N+2$ for LN are also the worst-case bound for approximations, and show that applicable universal-approximation theorems for ordinary MLPs transfer to these normalized architectures. Numerical experiments recover the predicted approximation rates for both normalizations across all five settings and illustrate the shifted-ReLU finite-width obstruction.

new Federated Art Chase: Privacy Leakage and Data Poisoning in Federated Fine-Tuning

Authors: Vasileios Perifanis, Nikolaos Pavlidis

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) is increasingly used to adapt a pretrained, centrally fine-tuned model to data that remain on user devices, beyond training a model from scratch. In this report, prepared for the 4th Federated Machine Learning International Summer School, we study the privacy and integrity of this approach with a reproducible pipeline. An ImageNet-pretrained MobileNetV4-Conv-Small model is fine-tuned centrally on ten Quick, Draw! classes (97.50% test accuracy) and then adapted with head-only FedAvg over ten non-IID clients, which raises client training accuracy from 90.34% to 91.49% while preserving global accuracy. We evaluate four adversarial scenarios. First, from a single one-step client update, an honest-but-curious server recovers the complete private label set, but its reconstructions follow class prototypes rather than the private drawings (mean SSIM 0.130). Second, from a one-round FedAvg aggregate, label-count recovery falls from 87.5% with one client to 75.0% with five and ten clients, below the 80.0% of an uninformed uniform guess. Third, under FedAvg, a backdoor injected by 30%, 50%, and 100% of the clients reaches an attack success rate (ASR) of 21.9%, 31.3%, and 54.7% at a cost of at most 0.8 percentage points of clean accuracy, whereas label flipping is effective only when most of the source-class data are relabeled. Fourth, FLTrust with a root set retained from centralized fine-tuning lowers the backdoor ASR to 1.8% and 4.0% at 30% and 50% malicious clients and the label-flipping ASR from 12.6% to 0.8% without measurable clean-accuracy loss, but provides no protection when every client is malicious. The report closes with a live session in which participants draw, fine-tune, and poison the model through Indigma's FedMaestro orchestrator. Code and executed notebooks, runnable on Google Colab, are at https://github.com/Indigma-Innovations/fl-summer-school.

URLs: https://github.com/Indigma-Innovations/fl-summer-school.

new Finite-Time Guarantees for Constrained Reinforcement Learning under Partial Observability

Authors: Valentina Zaccaria, Philip Jordan, Tingting Ni, Gian Antonio Susto, Maryam Kamgarpour

Abstract: We study reinforcement learning in constrained partially observable Markov decision processes (CPOMDPs), where an agent maximizes reward subject to a cost constraint using only incomplete information about the underlying state. The lack of state observability may require optimal policies to depend on arbitrarily long observation-action histories, making planning and learning challenging. We leverage recent advances in finite-memory approximations of POMDPs and represent finite history windows as states of a constrained MDP. We then develop a primal--dual algorithm that provides the first non-asymptotic guarantees that jointly bound average reward suboptimality and constraint violation in the original CPOMDP. A key challenge is that this finite-window approximation affects both reward values and the set of feasible policies. Under a filter-stability assumption, we derive bounds relating optimality and feasibility in the finite-state model to those in the original CPOMDP, with approximation errors that decay geometrically with the window length. Experiments in partially observable gridworlds demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach.

new Uncertainty Quantification in Pairwise Difference Learning for Classification

Authors: Mohamed Karim Belaid, Maximilian Rabus, Eyke H\"ullermeier

Abstract: Instead of learning a mapping from instances to outcomes in the standard way, the key idea of pairwise difference learning (PDL) is to learn a function that takes two instances as input and predicts the difference between the respective outcomes. Given a function of this kind, predictions for a query instance are derived from every training example and then averaged. We propose a version of PDL for the task of classification and propose a suitable meta-learning technique for this purpose. Moreover, we address the problem of uncertainty quantification, i.e., of representing and quantifying the uncertainty of a PDL classifier's predictions in a faithful manner. Building on recent work on the decomposition of total uncertainty into aleatoric and epistemic components, we propose different ways of extending corresponding measures to the setting of PDL, depending on how and to what extent uncertainty is represented by the underlying base learner. In large-scale empirical studies, we analyze both the predictive accuracy and the uncertainty awareness of our PDL classifier.

new When Does Muon Actually Win? A Paired Data-, Time-, and Tuning-Budget Benchmark

Authors: Mushir Akhtar, Hazem N. Nounou, Majdi Mansouri, Mohamed N. Nounou

Abstract: Muon's gains in data efficiency motivate evaluating its performance under explicit data, time, and tuning budgets. On a single A100 GPU, we compare a reference single-device Muon implementation with AdamW across 17.7M- and 33.6M-parameter causal language models and 2.70M-parameter vision transformers. A common 23-candidate development budget selects configurations evaluated with five matched seeds, full validation trajectories, optimizer-step timing, and a frozen one-shot test. Muon reduces validation-loss area under the curve by 3.25--22.44\% at equal data and lowers test loss in all 20 primary pairs. These results characterize small models with reference kernels; their wall-clock ordering does not establish a ranking for larger models or optimized implementations.

new A Quantum Machine Learning Approach to Content-Based Media Recommendation

Authors: Mina Abbaszadeh, Ouissal Moumou, Anna Pearson, Bob Coecke, Rob Cooper, Alessandro Piscopo, Mehrnoosh Sadrzadeh

Abstract: We use quantum machine learning techniques to develop a content-based media recommender system and evaluate its performance on TV programmes using textual descriptions from one week of BBC iPlayer data. Grounded in the Quantum Natural Language Processing framework (QNLP), programme synopses are mapped to variational quantum circuits using their semantic structure. The parameters of these circuits are trained via a supervised pairwise similarity learning task. We benchmark the quantum recommender against a classical BiLSTM baseline trained on the same dataset and objective. While both models achieve comparable performance on the similarity learning task, the quantum model requires significantly fewer parameters (47k vs. 17.8M). When the BiLSTM is constrained to a matching parameter budget, its performance degrades substantially (by 3.63 percentage points in Mean Average Precision (MAP)), indicating that the structured quantum representation provides a highly parameter-efficient alternative in this setting. We demonstrate the feasibility of executing the trained models on quantum hardware by deploying the circuits on Quantinuum's System Model H2 (and accompanying emulator) using a SWAP-test-based protocol. The results confirm that the similarity signal is preserved under realistic, noisy conditions. Taken together, these findings highlight the potential of variational quantum circuits for recommendation tasks and represent a practical step toward their real-world deployment.

new Sharp and Stable Label-Free Calibration Certificates from Logical Coherence

Authors: Takahiro Kato (NP-hard, Inc.)

Abstract: Query-conditioned probabilistic predictors answer logically related questions one at a time, and labels to check their calibration are often missing. For each restriction on what a bettor's stakes may use, a minimax theorem identifies, for finitely supported laws of the answers, the sharp label-free lower bound on the corresponding calibration residual. On bundles of different events its mean, marginal and joint stages can separate strictly, and a bundle's bound cannot be attributed to one question without assumptions on the others. The sharp marginal bound is discontinuous in the answers, and no label-free lower bound continuous in the Wasserstein distance stays within a constant factor of it. Lipschitz stakes give a stable bound, sharp for a smooth calibration residual and, for every positive Lipschitz constant, positive exactly when the sharp marginal bound is. On i.i.d. streams, projected online gradient ascent at fixed nodes gives fixed-horizon certificates whose shortfall is bounded by grid, learning and statistical terms, and predictable betting gives anytime-valid ones. In a preregistered study of four predictors on two data sets, all seven confirmatory certificates were positive (the eighth pair did not pass the pilot). A certificate shows only that calibration is at least this bad; zero means "not refuted".

new From Perception to Prediction and Intervention: Benchmarking Proactive Video Assistants in Real-Life Scenarios

Authors: Boyu Cui, Junhao Luo, Zhihao Wang, Linghan Chen, Zejun Li, Jiwen Zhang, Siyuan Wang, Zhongyu Wei

Abstract: Recent multimodal large language models have advanced video understanding, but existing benchmarks mainly evaluate whether models can interpret observed visual content. In real-world scenarios, assistants must additionally anticipate what assistance is needed and decide when to provide it. To address this gap, we introduce \textsc{P$^3$IBench}, a benchmark for proactive video understanding in real-life streaming scenarios. It evaluates three complementary capabilities: daily perception, risk prediction and intervention. We benchmark 12 representative open-source and proprietary video-language models, including streaming and adapted non-streaming systems. Our evaluation shows that current models struggle to make well-calibrated response decisions. Finally, we construct additional proactive training data and train \textsc{P$^3$I-Assistant-4B}, achieving improvements on several tasks, though gains remain uneven across capabilities. Together, these findings show that effective proactive assistance requires not only visual understanding, but also precise response timing and appropriate restraint.

new Learning to Search for Executable Patternmaking from Certified Programs

Authors: Zhuotao Xu, Chi Zeng, Liying Yang, Xinxing Yu, Yanyan Liang

Abstract: Recent models generate sewing patterns from images or text, and program-based representations make these patterns executable. These systems produce a finished pattern and do not model how a patternmaker turns one pattern into another. In practice a new style is made by editing an existing pattern: lengthening the bodice, moving a dart, swapping a sleeve. Pattern archives record the results of such edits and none of the steps. We observe that the steps can be recovered by searching over editing actions until one pattern becomes the other. A deterministic compiler makes the recovered steps checkable. To this end, we present SemDraft, the first framework that learns to search for executable patternmaking programs from paired before-and-after sewing patterns without recorded editing steps. Specifically, a bounded search recovers a program of editing actions and certifies it only after three checks: it recompiles from scratch, every pair of its actions is classified as ordered or interchangeable, and an independent replay reaches the same pattern. The certified programs then train a model that ranks candidate actions inside the same search. On two frozen draws of 80 order-sensitive cases, with every other search component fixed, the learned ranker certifies 63 and 64 cases within 1,000 search executions, against at most 53 and 52 for non-learned rankers.

In practice, historical observations come from a previous adaptive decision process whose policy is unknown. We study this setting for bounded rewards in $[0,1]$. Before the online game starts, the learner receives, for each arm $k$, $N'_k$ observations drawn i.i.d.\ from an unknown distribution $\nu'_k$, collected by an arbitrary and possibly data-dependent strategy, so the sample sizes $N'_k$ may be random.
