new Effectiveness of Hidden-State Probes for Localizing Reasoning Failures

Authors: Shubham Subhnil, Yannis Belkhiter, Erik Miehling, Adam Dahlgren Lindstr\"om, Elizabeth M. Daly

Abstract: Verifying the final answer of a long reasoning trace may show that the trace is wrong but it does not distinguish three cases. The problem may lie beyond the model's capability, another attempt would succeed, or one step in an otherwise recoverable trace made the trace fail. Standard hidden-state probes are trained on final-answer correctness, a label that conflates these cases. Monte Carlo rollouts distinguish the cases and locate the failing step at additional inference cost. We test the hidden states of 4B and 8B reasoning models for the failure type and the failing step and compare each probe with the strongest baseline that uses no hidden states. On incorrect 8B traces, the confidence probe, a calibrated correctness probe at a midpoint cutoff, misses 81% of cases in which a single step makes the trace unrecoverable and 47% of cases in which another attempt would succeed. A probe with a different training design over the same states reverses the ordering of these two kinds of failure, so either ordering can be read from those states. At both scales, no single tested probe locates the failing step more accurately than a rule based only on trace length, and at 4B a probe trained to predict that step fails to outperform this rule in any of 10 runs on identical traces. The confidence probe therefore indicates that an answer is wrong, but its failure-type ordering depends on its training design.

new Decoding Naturalistic Speech from fMRI with Temporal Modeling and Multimodal Alignment

Authors: Corentin Caris, Louis Jalouzot, Nona Rajabi, G\'eraud Ilinca, Ahmed Hassayoune, Asilbek Muminov, Thadd\'ee Delebarre, Clara Fonteneau, Charles Maussion, Bertrand Thirion, Alexis Thual, Antoine Collas

Abstract: Reconstructing what a person perceives from their brain activity is a stringent test of whether neural recordings carry meaningful information. Naturalistic speech offers a rich testbed for this challenge: it unfolds over multiple timescales, but fMRI's noisy, temporally smeared measurements make this structure hard to recover. We introduce NOEMA, a temporal convolutional decoder that aligns windows of consecutive fMRI measurements with multimodal stimulus embeddings. These embeddings combine complementary text, audio, and speech representations of the same stimulus segment, enabling its retrieval directly from brain activity. On two naturalistic speech datasets, Cephalonauts One and LeBel, NOEMA retrieves the correct segment among 2,000 candidates within the top 10 for more than 42% of held-out queries. This substantially outperforms baseline decoders, an advantage that persists when the candidate pool is extended with thousands of distractors absent from training. Controlled ablations establish the contributions of temporal modeling and complementary stimulus representations.

new ABNAT: Attention-Based Noise-Aware Training for Robust Transformers on Analog Compute-in-Memory Systems

Authors: Wenshuai Yao, Wenyong Zhou, Hanyong Shao, Zhiyuan Ning, Yizhe Chen, Yuannuo Feng, Kechao Tang, Ru Huang

Abstract: Transformer-based models have become the backbone of a wide range of tasks, but their intensive computation and data movement incur substantial energy overhead. Analog compute-in-memory (CIM) offers an energy-efficient alternative, yet its inherent hardware non-idealities perturb stored weights and can significantly degrade model performance. Although existing noise-aware training (NAT) methods improve robustness by injecting noise during training, they largely overlook the critical role of attention mechanisms in Transformer inference.To bridge this gap, we analyze how CIM-induced noise reshapes attention in Transformers and identify two key observations: (1) the divergence between clean and noisy attention maps increases substantially in deeper layers, and (2) conventional NAT can remain overconfident even when its attention routes deviate markedly from the clean reference. Motivated by these findings, we propose ABNAT, an attention-based noise-aware training framework that teaches the noisy student to preserve the clean teacher's headwise attention routes rather than merely reproduce answers, without hardware changes or inference overhead; selective variants further reduce training cost.Extensive experiments across vision, language, and multimodal Transformers show that ABNAT consistently improves robustness over corresponding NAT baselines, improving noise-degraded accuracy by up to 11.28% and reducing attention deviation by up to 85.77%.

new Can AI Forecast the Extremes? Evaluating Probabilistic Weather Models on Tropical Cyclones

Authors: Nathan Chalumeau, Xavier Renard, Remi Meynadier, Hugo Rakotoarimanga, Marcin Detyniecki, Emmanuel de Bezenac

Abstract: Weather prediction is undergoing an important shift, with data-driven AI models outperforming leading numerical weather prediction systems on average at a fraction of the computational cost. Yet, whether this success extends to extreme events remains contested. Earlier evaluations have exposed important shortcomings, but have largely focused on deterministic models and metrics, leaving the capabilities of the latest probabilistic models less understood. We conduct an extensive benchmark of tropical cyclone forecasting, comparing GenCast, FourCastNet 3, and FGN with ECMWF's operational physics-based ensemble, IFS-ENS. We develop a suite of probabilistic metrics and diagnostic analyses to assess cyclone tracking, intensity biases, uncertainty, and the wind-pressure relationship that characterizes storm structure. We find that, despite remaining limitations in intensity and ensemble spread, leading AI models substantially outperform IFS-ENS in cyclone tracking: FGN achieves better probabilistic location scores for 90% of evaluated storms in 2025. Further analyses reveal model-specific failure modes and suggest a lingering influence of pretraining data, pointing to opportunities for targeted improvement. Nonetheless, these results suggest that AI models are capable of capturing physical relationships that remain predictive well beyond typical weather conditions, motivating further investigation into how these capabilities emerge and how to strengthen them.

new TIPS: A Tiny Inference-time Policy for Scaling in Diffusion Language Models

Authors: Ya-Shi Zhang, Jian Tang, Pierre-Andr\'e No\"el, Oleksiy Ostapenko

Abstract: Masked diffusion language models expose an inference-time choice: which positions become visible after each denoising pass. Many existing decoding methods use hand-designed rules or require visibility to grow monotonically. We present a $\textbf{T}$iny $\textbf{I}$nference-time $\textbf{P}$olicy for $\textbf{S}$caling ($\textbf{TIPS}$), an approximately $600$K-parameter controller that learns both unmasking and remasking while keeping the underlying language model frozen. TIPS uses visibility-aware confidence features and a shallow transformer to select which tokens should be visible, while a fixed schedule controls the expected visible fraction. We train the controller through supervised warmup followed by reinforcement learning from terminal correctness rewards. On GSM8K and MATH-500 with LLaDA-8B-Instruct, the full-canvas and two-block TIPS variants achieve $58.46\%$ and $57.97\%$ micro-averaged accuracy at a budget of $32$ decoder evaluations, exceeding the strongest baseline in our main comparison by $12.1$ and $11.6$ percentage points, respectively. Both variants are trained at a budget of $32$ evaluations and remain competitive at higher budgets without retraining. These results demonstrate that learning when to reveal and reconsider tokens can improve the accuracy-compute trade-off without updating the decoder backbone.

new Scene Reconstruction Using Ubiquitous Wireless Signals via Diffusion Posterior Inference Over physics-Grounded Representations

Authors: Vahid Yazdnian, Yasaman Ghasempour

Abstract: Knowing the layout of the physical world matters for many applications, yet the sensors we rely on for it (e.g., cameras and LiDAR) can fail in darkness, in adverse weather, or behind walls. Wireless signals do not face these limitations: every link between a base station and a user is shaped by the buildings it passes, reflects from, or is blocked by, leaving information about the surrounding scene embedded in the wireless channel measured at the endpoints. However, mapping channel measurements to the environment is a severely ill-posed problem: a measurement records only the structures that interact with the transmitted electromagnetic waves along its propagation paths, and even these observations can often be explained by multiple plausible scenes. We therefore formulate wireless scene reconstruction as posterior inference and develop a hierarchical framework with two complementary stages to approach this problem. A wireless foundation model, pretrained to respect propagation physics, transforms each channel measurement into a representation of the scene structure that shaped the observation. A diffusion model then jointly integrates representations from multiple measurements to model and sample the posterior over building occupancy, with consensus over samples producing the final reconstruction. We demonstrate that the framework can accurately recover building footprints from wireless channel measurements alone and show that its performance depends critically on both design components: physics-grounded representations expose scene-relevant information, while joint posterior inference coherently integrates complementary observations.

new Beyond Fixed Acquisition Functions: Temporal Difference Active Learning for Perturbation Discovery

Authors: Xudong Zhang, Jianzhe Li, Yitong Tang, Peijie Zhou

Abstract: Genetic and drug perturbation screens are important tools for elucidating disease mechanisms and validating therapeutic targets, but experimental costs limit the number of candidates that can be tested. Existing perturbation screening methods typically update the surrogate model with feedback while keeping the acquisition function fixed, limiting their ability to adapt selection to different response landscapes. Experimental feedback reveals current hits and informs subsequent selection by updating candidate predictions, providing a training signal for learning how to select experiments. We formulate budgeted perturbation discovery as a sequential decision problem and propose Temporal Difference Active Learning (TDAL), which learns an acquisition function online through temporal difference learning from a single screening trajectory, accounting for current hits and future discoveries. TDAL uses the Hellinger distance between hit probabilities for unmeasured candidates before and after an experiment to weight successor value, and reuses measured outcomes through leave-batch-out (LBO) to construct virtual transitions and alleviate training data scarcity. Across eight public screens spanning single, double, and triple perturbations under varied experimental configurations, TDAL achieves an average rank of 1.63 among eleven methods and ranks first on four tasks, demonstrating its adaptability across response landscapes.

new Causal Integrated Gradients: Scalable Feature Attribution Along Causal Paths

Authors: Joran Michiels, Anis Ismail, Maarten De Vos, Alejandro Sifrim

Abstract: Existing feature attribution methods that account for feature dependencies do not scale to high dimensions. We propose causal integrated gradients (CIG) and causal expected gradients (CEG), which extend integrated gradients to incorporate prior causal structure among input features while retaining its computational efficiency and, under conditions on the causal graph, its axiomatic guarantees. In many settings, some causal structure between features is already known, for example, that a transcription factor causally regulates a target gene's expression. Yet existing gradient-based methods cannot exploit this structure, instead crediting a feature's downstream effects over its true upstream cause, such as ranking the target gene above the transcription factor that regulates it. Our key idea is to embed this causal structure by routing the integration path along the causal graph. The method thereby decomposes each feature's attribution into a direct effect and effects mediated through its causal descendants. We evaluate CIG in three settings of increasing realism. First, on a synthetic benchmark with a known causal model, CIG's attributions best match the ground-truth causal effect from Pearl's do-calculus, matching the accuracy of causal Shapley values at a fraction of the computational cost. Second, on simulated single-cell gene expression data with a known gene regulatory network (Dyngen), CIG most accurately recovers the transcription factors driving the simulation. Third, on real interferon-$\beta$ stimulation data, where only an approximate causal graph is available, CIG better prioritizes the transcription factors driving the measured response than DeepSHAP and integrated gradients. Together, these results show that incorporating causal structure into gradient-based attributions recovers upstream drivers that existing methods miss, without sacrificing scalability.

new Specification Gaming and a Constrained Reinforcement Learning Mitigation in a Cleaning Gridworld

Authors: Hilda Adwubi Osei

Abstract: Reinforcement learning agents optimize exactly the reward signal they are given, which can diverge sharply from an intended true objective when that signal is only a proxy for it; this failure mode is known as specification gaming. Existing evidence for this failure mode is largely observational, with comparatively few demonstrations pairing a controlled, deliberately constructed proxy/true gap with a mitigation that carries a verifiable guarantee rather than only an empirical improvement. We construct a minimal gridworld in which a cleaning-robot agent can either genuinely remove dirt or conceal it from a cheap sensor, and show that a standard deep Q-network trained only on the sensor's proxy reward converges to a policy that games it completely, reaching near-maximal proxy reward while never achieving the true objective. We then introduce a constrained reinforcement learning fix, penalizing the specific behaviors through which the agent exploits the gap, and show that it eliminates the gaming behavior from the final policy while substantially recovering true-objective performance. We further derive and empirically verify an exact accounting identity relating the proxy reward, true reward, and our cost signal for this environment, showing that the cost signal measures the proxy/true gap exactly rather than merely tracking it, and yields a deterministic guarantee under a hard-constrained variant of the method.

new The importance of tensor handling and implementation of physics-informed DeepONets

Authors: Nahum Banks, Gustavo A. M. de Almeida, Sergio Maldonado

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) reduce or eliminate the need for large labelled datasets by enforcing governing equations during training. Physics-informed Deep Operator Networks (PI-DeepONets) extend this approach to learning solution operators, enabling rapid prediction across families of problems. Despite these advantages, we show that their application to higher-dimensional problems remains limited by the computational cost of training. To address this issue, we propose a modified PI-DeepONet architecture that reduces this cost by removing redundancy in how the Branch network processes input functions across collocation points. The proposed formulation is mathematically equivalent to the original architecture but reduces input tensor sizes and the number of Branch network evaluations. We analyse the computational complexity of both architectures and derive theoretical scaling laws for training time with respect to the number of collocation points and input functions. The proposed architecture is evaluated on 1D, 2D, and 3D linear advection problems. In all cases, it achieves comparable predictive accuracy to the original PI-DeepONet while consistently reducing training time. In 2D experiments, a maximum speed-up of $3.75\times$ is observed, with empirical training-time scaling agreeing with the predicted linear dependence on collocation points and input functions. Furthermore, training a 3D model on an NVIDIA A100 GPU is only feasible using the proposed architecture, as the original formulation exceeds memory limits. These results demonstrate that careful consideration of how PI-DeepONets are implemented can yield substantial gains, enhancing the practical performance of PI-DeepONets and enabling their application to otherwise intractable physics-informed learning problems.

new LCT: Losslessly Compressed Tensors with GPU friendly execution for Training

Authors: Max Zhu, Bohang Sun, Pietro Lio, Jialin Yu

Abstract: Lossless tensor compression could reduce memory usage during training, but training requires tensors to be repeatedly re-encoded. We introduce Losslessly Compressed Tensors (LCT), a GPU execution framework designed for this repeated encode-decode regime. LCT exploits the natural redundancy in floating point data to reduce storage for weights, activations and optimizer states. Highly optimized GPU kernels implement Huffman coding with precomputed codebooks for low compression and decompression overhead, essential for training. Our analysis characterizes LCT's storage costs and adaptation to magnitude shifts. LCT achieves a compression ratio of up to $1.455\times$, and is demonstrated in training and finetuning settings. On a 162M-parameter NanoGPT, LCT reduces memory usage by $24.6\%$ for a $7.2\%$ step-time increase. In Qwen3-4B fine-tuning, LCT saves up to $23.8\%$ of peak memory, enabling a $3.5\times$ larger context length in training on the same GPU.

new Generalization Guarantees of Randomized Block Coordinate Descent for Nonconvex Learning

Authors: Ruixi Hu, Liang Wu

Abstract: Randomized block coordinate descent (RBCD) is a popular optimization algorithm for many large-scale machine learning problems. The existing theoretical analysis of RBCD mainly focuses on its convergence behavior from an optimization perspective, while its generalization behavior remains less understood for nonconvex problems. In this paper, we establish high-probability generalization guarantees for RBCD with nonconvex objectives. In the general nonconvex setting, we derive bounds on the gradients of both empirical and population risks, requiring only standard smoothness assumptions. With polynomially decaying step sizes, we tune the iteration number to balance optimization and generalization, thereby quantifying how early stopping leads to the optimal convergence rate for the population gradients. In the gradient-dominated setting, we provide bounds in terms of functional suboptimality, which nearly match the optimistic rates in convex and low-noise settings. Finally, we develop dimension-independent bounds for loss functions of a specific structure, making the generalization guarantees applicable to high-dimensional problems.

new OS-Helix: Unifying GUI Interaction and Code Execution in Native Computer-Using Agents

Authors: Bowen Yang, Kaiming Jin, Zichen Ding, Zhenyu Wu, Zehao Li, Zhoumianze Liu, Kanzhi Cheng, Fangzhi Xu, Yanan Sun, Qiushi Sun, Yu Qiao

Abstract: Real desktop automation is neither purely visual nor purely programmatic. Agents must understand user-facing interfaces while efficiently manipulating files, scripts, and system state. However, native GUI/code coordination cannot be obtained by prompt-level tool composition. In our preliminary experiments, naively adding a code tool degrades a GUI agent, revealing that GUI/code routing must be internalized through training on high-quality hybrid data. We present OS-Helix, an end-to-end trained multimodal computer-using agent that unifies GUI actions and code execution in one action space. To construct the required supervision, we introduce application-artifact composition, pairing desktop applications with authentic file artifacts to instantiate executable Ubuntu states, and a decoupled instruction/evaluator generation pipeline that produces environment-grounded tasks whose final states can be verified regardless of the solution modality. We cold-start the policy with SFT on filtered hybrid data and further improve it through online RL in live desktop environments. For RL, we propose Step-balanced GSPO, which aggregates importance weight by action step rather than raw assistant tokens to prevent long code actions from dominating heterogeneous-action optimization. On OSWorld-Verified, OS-Helix-27B and OS-Helix-35B-A3B achieve relative improvements of 35.0% and 33.7% over their matched Qwen3.5 hybrid baselines, while OS-Helix-27B-RL further improves by 42.9% to reach 66.44% success. Ablations provide evidence consistent with contributions from learned GUI/code coordination, scalable verifiable supervision, and action step balanced optimization, while demonstrating cross-platform transfer on WindowsAgentArena. Our project homepage is at https://os-copilot.github.io/OS-Helix/.

URLs: https://os-copilot.github.io/OS-Helix/.

new Training-Free On-Policy Distillation

Authors: Bangji Yang, Jiajun Fan, Zhaoyu Zhu, Hongbo Ma, Ge Liu

Abstract: On-policy distillation consolidates specialist models into one student at the price of rollouts, teacher passes and gradient steps for every teacher. We show that the consolidation does not need training: with forward passes only, the capabilities of separately tuned specialists can be aggregated into one model that is, on average, more accurate than either specialist alone. When specialists share a base model, distillation has an exact layer-wise solution: each linear layer of the fused model must reproduce every specialist's output on the inputs that specialist receives while reading its own answers, a least-squares problem whose solution needs only the second moments of those inputs. One forward pass over a few hundred rollouts provides them. We call this training-free on-policy distillation (\method). What the rollouts contain matters more than the solver: answers truncated in repetition loops are few among a mathematics specialist's rollouts but carry a large share of their tokens, and removing them lifts the fused model on AIME25 from task arithmetic's level to the specialist's at matched precision. On Olmo~3 7B, a few GPU-hours of forward computation per specialist yield a generalist that keeps most of each specialist's gain on each of seven mathematics and code benchmarks.

new Query-Efficient Rejection Sampling for Parallel Masked Diffusion

Authors: Shiyuan Zhang, Xuheng Li, Qiwei Di, Quanquan Gu

Abstract: Masked diffusion models (MDMs) accelerate generation by unmasking multiple tokens in parallel, but sampling these tokens independently ignores their correlations and biases the output. Anari et al. (2026) corrected this bias with rejection sampling, decomposing the log acceptance probability into a sum of token-level terms, but the evaluation requires one score query per unmasked token. We propose the Single-Increment Rejection Sampler (SIRS), which provides an estimate of the log acceptance probability from a single randomly sampled increment at the cost of one query. SIRS then converts a Poisson number of clipped estimates into accept/reject decisions through a Bernoulli factory adapted from Chen et al. (2026). Since each query returns the conditionals of all remaining tokens in the block, we further Rao-Blackwellize each increment by averaging over these tokens at no extra cost, thereby reducing clipping. We show that the KL-divergence between the data distribution and the SIRS output is bounded by a clipping term plus the model's training error. With exact scores, parallel decoding pays the full within-block dependence, whereas SIRS pays only the part beyond the clipping range.

new Explore Once, Evaluate Many: Multiple Policy Evaluation for Stochastic Linear Bandits

Authors: Spyros Dragazis, Yilei Chen, Aldo Pacchiano

Abstract: We study how to evaluate $K$ target policies simultaneously in stochastic contextual bandits with linear reward structure. Given a known $d$-dimensional feature map and reward-free access to policy queries, we propose PRISMA (Policy evaluation through Regularized Information Sharing and Mixture Allocation), an algorithm that estimates every target policy's value to accuracy $\varepsilon$ with probability at least $1-\delta$. The algorithm uses $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\!\left( \frac{(L^2S^2+\beta^2)(1\vee\rho^\star)}{\varepsilon^2} \cdot \log(K) \right)$ context draws and reward observations, where $\rho^\star\le d$ is an instance-dependent measure of the difficulty of designing a shared sampling distribution. Here, $L$, $S$, and $\beta$ bound the feature norm, reward parameter norm, and reward noise, respectively, and the suppressed factors are logarithmic in $K$, $d$, and $1/\delta$. Our method combines reward-free planning, a regularized sampling design, and a single ridge regression estimator shared across all target policies. An adaptive doubling procedure selects the regularization scale without knowledge of $\rho^\star$. The guarantees require no numerical lower bound on second-moment eigenvalues and have no explicit dependence on the cardinalities of the context and action spaces. Policy-query costs are accounted for separately, with $K$ queries per shared planning context.

new BitSparse: Compact Activation Storage for ReLU Feed-Forward Networks

Authors: Max Zhu, Bohang Sun, Pietro Lio, Jialin Yu

Abstract: Reducing memory use in large language model (LLM) training is an ongoing challenge. Activation checkpointing saves memory by recomputation, while lossy compression changes the stored activations. The reemergence of sparse $\relu$ and $\relu^2$ activations creates a third opportunity: losslessly compressing the activation cache itself. We present BitSparse, a GPU-resident lossless compression format for sparse activations. Compression and reconstruction run in custom Triton kernels fused into an autograd function, shrinking the activation cache while recovering it exactly for the backward pass. Bfloat16 (BF16) and FP8 activations are supported. Optional sign-bit packing further increases compression up to close to the theoretical limit. We analyze its compression ratio and compare it to the theoretical optimum. BitSparse lowers per-token VRAM by 31\% and 30\% Modded-NanoGPT and Nemotron-H respectively, with overheads of no more than 1\% at practical sequence lengths. Additionally, a new preallocated buffer system avoids host synchronization during training when variable size tensors are allocated.

new Topology-Inspired Fine-Tuning for Wind-Field Super-Resolution

Authors: Arjun Dadhwal, Brian Summa

Abstract: Scientific super-resolution (SR) models are typically optimized with pointwise reconstruction losses, yet scientific interpretation often hinges on extrema, connected regions, and multiscale scalar structure. We characterize this tension with pretrained CNN and GAN wind-field SR models. Pointwise metrics, bottleneck persistence-diagram (PD) distance, and merge-tree (MT) distance favor the CNN, whereas order-2 Wasserstein PD distances favor the sharper GAN, so even different distances on the same topological descriptor reward different properties. Motivated by this disagreement, we fine-tune the pretrained CNN with differentiable topology-inspired auxiliary losses on scalar speed, gradient magnitude, soft high-speed level sets, and local maxima, and with fixed-pair supervision, which constrains predicted values and persistence at ground-truth persistence-pair endpoints located offline with the Topology ToolKit. A loss study of 16 fine-tuning configurations, each compared with a matched standard fine-tuning control that uses only the original reconstruction loss, on a fixed 168-field benchmark (with topology evaluated on a fixed 160 by 160 subregion of each field), shows that, among the scalar-field losses, gradient supervision provides the strongest PD improvement but does not improve mean MT distance, whereas fixed-pair supervision gives the largest and most consistent joint improvement in PD and MT distances. The final objective combines these two signals. Relative to the pretrained CNN, it lowers mean bottleneck, $W_{2,\infty}$, and $W_{2,2}$ distances by 31%, 25%, and 27% (and by 32-35% relative to the control, which on its own worsens all three), lowers all three on 150 of 168 fields, and gains 1.30 dB PSNR, about half the gain of standard fine-tuning. Mean MT distance also improves, but on only 91 of those 150 fields, indicating that PD and MT capture complementary structure.

new Baselines and Score Centering in Policy Gradient Estimation

Authors: Shuo Han, Ziqing Hu

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has become a widely used approach to post-training large language models. In a typical implementation, learning is off-policy: The policy generating the responses differs from the policy being trained because of training-inference mismatch or policy staleness in asynchronous training. This discrepancy is commonly addressed by importance sampling, whose ratio is usually regularized in practice through truncation, masking, or clipping. Under regularized importance sampling, the importance-weighted policy gradient is no longer invariant to constant shifts in the reward. This invariance is recovered by using the covariance between the reward and the importance-weighted score as the estimand, which coincides with the policy gradient when the ratio is not regularized. This note develops this covariance perspective for both sequence-level and token-level importance sampling. The covariance perspective reveals the roles of the group-mean baseline and score centering in policy gradient estimation. Both perform centering in covariance estimation, but they differ in the mean being subtracted: The group-mean baseline uses a sample mean, whereas score centering uses a population mean. The two lead to distinct unbiased covariance estimators with different variances. Whether both forms of centering should be used simultaneously depends on whether importance sampling is applied at the sequence level or at the token level. With sequence-level importance sampling, score centering adds no benefit once the group-mean baseline is used; with token-level importance sampling, using the group-mean baseline without score centering generally introduces bias when the importance ratio is regularized.

new Librarian Agents: Training LLMs to Curate Long-Context Workspaces that Anticipate Future Queries

Authors: Anikait Singh, Teresa Zhang, Yoonho Lee, Roshen Sanjay Nair, Aviral Kumar, Chelsea Finn

Abstract: Reasoning over long document contexts remains a challenge for large language models (LLMs). Standard retrieval methods are inherently reactive, bottlenecking agents with complex, cross-document reasoning at inference time, while precomputed structures like knowledge graphs often fall short because they rely on rigid heuristics. Instead, can we allow agents to perform anticipatory directed curation: learning to proactively reorganize unstructured corpora prior to inference to efficiently serve diverse future queries. We build on a key asymmetry in modern agentic harnesses (e.g., Claude Code): while LLM agents are adept at navigating existing filesystems to solve an immediate task, they are rarely trained to construct reusable workspaces for future use. To instantiate this capability, we introduce Librarian Agents, a framework in which a builder agent transforms raw documents into a hierarchical filesystem of synthetic files and reference links, which forms a workspace tailored for a downstream answering agent. We formulate workspace construction as a Markov decision process and optimize the builder policy via reinforcement learning to directly maximize downstream answering accuracy. Empirically, Librarian Agents outperforms the strongest retrieval and precomputation baselines by 33 percentage points on BrowseComp-Plus, amortizes upfront construction costs across repeated queries, and generalizes zero-shot to unseen domains on BRIGHT with a 31-point average accuracy improvement.

new Learning Rational Strategies for Temporal Objectives in Multi-Agent Systems

Authors: Omar Adalat, Edwin Hamel-De le Court, Francesco Belardinelli

Abstract: We study rational synthesis for stochastic multi-agent systems with unknown dynamics, where each agent is equipped with its own probabilistic temporal objective specified using Probabilistic Linear Temporal Logic on Finite Traces ($\mathsf{PLTL}_f}$). We impose solution concepts (e.g., Nash equilibrium, subgame-perfect equilibrium) as constraints on the joint strategy profile, using payoffs derived from each agent's probability of satisfying its temporal objective. In particular, we formulate per-agent rational synthesis as finding a strategy profile -- if one exists -- such that (i) the designated agent's probabilistic temporal constraint is satisfied, and (ii) the profile represents a stable solution under a chosen solution concept. We give conditional convergence guarantees for tabular Nash-Q learning, certify satisfaction with simultaneous finite-sample confidence bounds, and assess strategic stability with approximate best-response checking. Our approach operates in a stochastic \emph{Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning} (MARL) environment, where model-free learning algorithms attempt to attain extremal satisfaction probabilities for temporal objectives automatically compiled into reward automata. Our approach is empirically validated using 8 general-sum concurrent stochastic games.

new CAT-alyzing Honesty: Continuous Adversarial Training for Transferable LLM Honesty

Authors: Yuan-Chen Chang, Niklas Herbster, Gauthier Gidel, Tommaso Tosato

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are becoming prevalent in scenarios where honesty is critical. However, despite alignment training, these models still exhibit dishonest behaviour, such as outputting claims that contradict some knowledge or are not supported by what is accessible in context. We introduce Continuous Adversarial Training for Consistent Honesty (CATCH), inspired by a method originally developed to robustly refuse harmful queries. CATCH trains the model towards an honest answer and away from a dishonest one while maintaining the model's capabilities. We evaluate two architectures (Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct and Qwen3-3.6-27B). We show that trained with only hundreds of targets built from self-reporting scenarios, CATCH learns an honest behaviour that robustly generalizes and transfers across distinct benchmarks. It consistently reduces dishonesty under pressure on MASK, increases confessions in the multi-agent game of Among Us, and improves the discovery rates of the model's hidden behaviours in AuditBench. We show that CATCH outperforms prompting and supervised fine-tuning in improving honesty, and it is on par with state-of-the-art honesty activation steering. However, CATCH surpasses honesty steering in maintaining utility, thanks to a novel choice of utility data.

new Towards Continual Learning: Mitigating Forgetting With Subspace Tuning

Authors: Arnav Chavan, Kusha Sareen, Rishabh Tiwari, Abhranil Chandra, Johan Obando-Ceron, Kurt Keutzer, Scott Niekum, Inderjit S Dhillon, Devvrit Khatri

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) acquire new skills through fine-tuning, but it can destroy capabilities they already have: catastrophic forgetting remains a central obstacle to continual learning in LLMs. Recent works connect forgetting to how far, and along which directions, fine-tuning moves the weights. On-policy methods such as reinforcement learning and on-policy distillation avoid updating the principal singular directions of weight matrices and thus forget less. In contrast, SFT concentrates updates along these directions and is susceptible to catastrophic forgetting. In this work, we introduce Subspace Tuning (SST), which restricts each weight update to the bottom singular subspace, eliminating changes along the principal directions. SST is optimizer-agnostic and composes well with fine-tuning recipes including SFT and self-distillation. SST combines tight subspace protection, dense gradient estimation, and a simple refresh mechanism, enabling stronger learning than prior orthogonal fine-tuning methods. With these mechanisms, protecting as much as 99\% of the weight subspace substantially improves retention while preserving strong target-task performance. This behavior strengthens with model and data size and nearly eliminates forgetting in the large-model regime. SST also keeps the model close to its base distribution, inducing $2$--$4\times$ less KL drift than the baseline recipes. Overall, SST advances the learning--forgetting Pareto frontier, making it a practical default for fine-tuning pre-trained language models.

new Learning to Steer LLM Agents and Multi-Agent Systems

Authors: Leyang Shen, Yaxin Gong, Yang Zhang, Chenxiao Fan, Jiakai Wang, Dong Wang, Yang Liu, Fuli Feng, Chun Kai Ling, Tat-Seng Chua

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based agents and multi-agent systems increasingly tackle complex tasks through long-horizon tool use and inter-agent collaboration. However, these systems are prone to deviating from the overall goal as execution unfolds, which wastes execution resources and leads to undesired outcomes, a failure termed goal drift. In this work, we propose a steering agent that supervises execution on the fly and steers the system back on track when drift occurs. The steering agent observes the execution context, deciding when to intervene with a low-cost fast path and how to intervene with a high-cost slow path. To unlock its steering intelligence, the ability to assess goal drift and generate effective interventions, we jointly train the two paths with reinforcement learning, combining a pairwise ranking objective with group-relative policy optimization (GRPO). Experiments on information-seeking search tasks show that the steering agent provides effective supervision for both single-agent and multi-agent systems, improving their performance and efficiency. The learned steering intelligence generalizes to out-of-domain tasks, transfers to unseen executors, and supports controllable intervention frequency without retraining.

new Twice Upon a Time: Complementary Representations for Time-Series Reasoning

Authors: Hedi Zisling, Liran Nochumsohn, Ilan Naiman, Mihir Parmar, Omri Azencot

Abstract: Time Series Language Models (TSLMs) enable language-based understanding and reasoning over temporal data, yet a fundamental question remains: what information should their time-series representations preserve, and what variation should they factor out? Existing TSLMs typically expose the language model to a single representation, imposing a fixed inductive bias across downstream tasks. We instead introduce complementary representations as a design principle for time-series reasoning. Our model combines two visual views: line plots preserve absolute measurements and the observed coordinate frame, while normalized delay embeddings factor out positive amplitude rescaling and offsets while exposing temporal structure. Dedicated visual encoders integrate both views into a shared language model through visual tokens and multi-level feature fusion. Evaluation-time interventions verify that the two views exhibit distinct invariance profiles, while single-view ablations show that their combination is particularly beneficial beyond basic perception, with the large gains in prediction and reasoning. Built on Qwen3-VL-8B, our dual-view model improves overall TSRBench accuracy from 44.5% with line plots alone to 53.2%, substantially exceeding the strongest evaluated dedicated TSLM baseline at 36.7%. It further achieves 89.9% accuracy on TSExam and 86.8% numerical accuracy on multivariate ChatTS alignment. Together, these results demonstrate that complementary representations provide an effective design principle for general-purpose TSLMs, enabling models to exploit temporal structure without sacrificing access to the original measurement frame.

new Robust Reinforcement Learning for Dynamic Treatment Regimes under Model and Measurement Uncertainty

Authors: Chengze Xie, Xuan Wang, Ying Ding, Hairong Wang, Yan Li

Abstract: We develop a robust reinforcement learning (RL) framework for learning adaptive treatment policies that generalize across patients and remain reliable under errors in the measured biomarker and shifts in the disease dynamics. By modeling the patient as a hidden context, we address generalization and robustness in a unified manner through robust contextual Markov decision processes. Applying our framework to adaptive therapy for metastatic prostate cancer in a simulator calibrated to clinical data, we obtain generalizable and robust policies that substantially outperform the existing adaptive therapy method. In particular, we attain robustness with almost no loss of performance when the biomarker is accurate and the disease evolves as anticipated, and find that the resulting policy Pareto-dominates the existing method across a diverse array of scenarios. Our findings suggest that robust RL holds great promise as a principled methodology for learning reliable adaptive treatment policies.

new Lean Graph Search for Formal Theorem Proving

Authors: Yihan Xu, Jia-Chen Gu, Junyi Zhang, Di Wu, Haikang Deng, Po-Nien Kung, Kai-Wei Chang, Amit Sahai, Wei Wang, Nanyun Peng

Abstract: Retrieving relevant lemmas is crucial for formal theorem proving, enabling provers to build machine-checkable proofs from established mathematical results. Recent retrieval systems match library declarations to a target theorem or its intermediate proof steps, but make limited use of multi-hop dependencies among declarations. These dependencies can reveal supporting lemmas that are not necessarily lexically similar to the target theorem but recur in proofs of related problems. To this end, we propose LeanGraphSearch, a graph-based retrieval method for Lean 4 that constructs a graph from Mathlib's forward dependencies and traverses it to uncover supporting lemmas through multi-hop dependency chains. Specifically, LeanGraphSearch links each declaration to the premises it depends on, uses a graph-based ranking algorithm to propagate relevance from semantically matched declarations to their dependencies, and reranks the expanded candidates against the original query. Across two mathematical retrieval benchmarks, LeanGraphSearch improves recall by up to 12% over the strongest baseline, LeanSearch v2, on MathlibQR and increases coverage of complete annotated proof routes on MathlibMPR, covering up to 3.7% of extra groups. More importantly, we evaluate whether these retrieval gains translate into improved end-to-end theorem proving by integrating LeanGraphSearch into an iterative proving loop guided by Lean feedback. Across three theorem proving benchmarks, LeanGraphSearch improves pass rate at {8, 16, 32} reflection turns by as much as 40% compared to vanilla prover and at most 10% compared to LeanSearch v2, showing higher accuracy-cost efficiency. These findings highlight the value of dependency graph search for theorem proving and motivate further research on structure-aware search for proof discovery.

new Rethinking Routing Exploration in Modern Mixture-of-Experts

Authors: Bingcong Li, Hao Ma, Michael Muehlebach, Niao He, Yi Ma

Abstract: Mixture-of-experts (MoE) models have become an important approach for scaling model parameters. Modern MoE architectures increasingly combine two design choices: fine-grained expert segmentation and shared experts. The former increases expressiveness by enabling more expert combinations at a fixed activation ratio, and the latter introduces experts that are active for every token. We identify an exploration dilemma between these designs: shared experts can make routing less exploratory by reducing the variability of token representations. This conflicts with the greater need for exploration induced by fine-grained expert segmentation due to the combinatorially enlarged routing space. Consequently, the additional expressiveness offered by fine-grained segmentation may not be fully realized in practice. MirrorMoE is proposed to reconcile this exploration dilemma by shrinking the exploration space through token-adaptive suppression of unnecessary experts. MirrorMoE consistently improves performance across model scales.

new PathFM: Fast Generative Forecasting of Multivariate Trajectories

Authors: Jiachen Yao, Xiyuan Zhang, Annan Yu, Boran Han, Abdul Fatir Ansari, Shuai Zhang, Michael Bohlke-Schneider, Bernie Wang

Abstract: Time series foundation models are becoming increasingly popular for general-purpose forecasting. However, most leading models predict marginal quantiles, leaving the dependence structure of future trajectories unspecified. This distinction matters for trajectory-dependent quantities such as cumulative demand, peak load, and event timing, yet point and marginal scores do not reveal it. To model this dependence, we introduce PathFM, a trajectory-based forecasting model that uses flow matching to generate complete future paths. We further distill the model to one or two steps and share history attention cache across draws. Together, these enable PathFM to generate multiple paths for a single series in roughly the time needed for one query to a quantile model. The distilled sampler also makes it practical to optimize a calibration loss directly. PathFM achieves competitive marginal accuracy on the established forecasting benchmarks fev-bench and TIME. To complement these evaluations, we introduce Forecast Path Score Bench (fps-bench), which augments fev-bench tasks with energy and variogram scores and downstream metrics. PathFM achieves a higher aggregate path score on fps-bench than all evaluated external baselines. Experiments on chaotic systems further show that our flow model sustains long-run variability, while strong baselines rapidly collapse. PathFM makes generative time series forecasting both competitive and practical, enabling diverse downstream applications.

cross Iterative Neural Variational Solver of Committor Function in Under-damped Langevin Dynamics

Authors: Tiejun Li, Zhaoqi Liu, Xiang Zhou

Abstract: The committor function describes the probability that a stochastic dynamical system, while transitioning between two metastable states, will first reach one state rather than the other. It is a central object in transition path theory, providing the accurate reaction rate and the characterization of the transition mechanism through reactive probability flux and the transition state ensemble. While the numerical methods for this function are well studied for over-damped Langevin dynamics where the friction damping coefficient goes to infinity, its computation in the general under-damped case remains an open challenge due to the lack of a variational structure in the underlying backward Kolmogorov equation. This restricts the quantitative investigation of the impact of the finite damping coefficient on rare transitions in real systems. In this work, by decomposing the infinitesimal generator of the underdamped Langevin dynamics into a self-adjoint and a skew-adjoint part, we develop an iterative scheme in which each subproblem has a variational form and can be solved efficiently using neural networks. Fundamentally, this approach can be viewed as a generalized Concus-Golub-Widlund (CGW) splitting method and each subproblem has a variational form, which can be solved efficiently. This iterative scheme also has the flexibility of introducing a preconditioning operator associated with a new overdamped Langevin dynamics, which naturally leads to the design of enhanced adaptive sampling for the subproblems. We validate the efficiency and accuracy of our method on benchmark systems including a one-dimensional double-well potential, the Muller-Brown potential, and alanine dipeptide. The proposed iterative variational framework provides a practical and extensible numerical tool for probing rare transition events in Langevin dynamics across varying damping coefficients.

Abstract: Super-resolution machine learning methods hold promise for addressing the high computational costs associated with high-dimensional features in traditional transport simulations. cross Exploring the Design Space of Permutation Diffusion LLMs

Authors: Zhe Xu, Mingfu Liang, Kavosh Asadi, Jean-Pascal Billaud, Tianyi Chen, Yanhui Guo, Christina Huang, Baekjin Kim, Jimmy Kim, Yufei Li, Daqi Sun, Jiachen Sun, Dian Yu, Zaiwei Zhang, Kaushik Rangadurai, Frank Shyu, Sandeep Pandey, Xi Liu, Luke Simon

Abstract: Permutation generation with language models requires both text-conditioned ranking and a global one-to-one assignment between candidates and output positions. cross AbridgeKV: Compressing Long Reasoning into the KV Cache of Learned Pause Tokens

Authors: Jack Cai, Lijie Yang, Vasilis Kontonis, Dimitris Papailiopoulos, Shivam Garg

Abstract: Long chain-of-thought decoding is memory-bound: the KV cache of the generated tokens grows linearly with the test-time compute being spent. Evicting cache entries does not fix this, because a model never trained on a cache with entries removed generates longer, often up to the length limit. We instead train the model to compress its own output. AbridgeKV compresses each completed block of generated text into the KV entries of a few learned pause tokens. These are extra input tokens with trained embeddings that produce no output. Once the pause tokens have encoded the block, AbridgeKV frees the block's original cache; subsequent tokens attend to the pause tokens' KV entries in its place. Three novel choices make this work: the pause tokens' KV entries take positions inside the block they replace, the cache budget is allocated across layers according to the measured value of each layer's entries, and compression operates on relatively large blocks. On 446 competition problems from AIME, HMMT, AMC, BRUMO, CMIMC and SMT with 32K-token generations, keeping a quarter of the generated cache matches an identically trained full-KV model in accuracy and output length; the strongest published evictor at a matched budget writes far longer, and the closest trained compressor, retrained on the same data, loses 10 points. Served in vLLM, the cache is 3.5x smaller per sequence and a saturated H100 finishes a problem 3.2x faster than with full KV.

cross Global-in-time Spectral Duhamel-Picard Method for Post-Exposure Bake Simulation and Physics-Informed Neural Operators for EUV Lithography

Authors: Doyun Kim, Mircea Dusa, Werner Gilijns, Philippe Leray

Abstract: We present a global-in-time spectral Duhamel-Picard method with polynomial expansions for post-exposure bake (PEB) simulation. The coupled acid-quencher reaction-diffusion equations are reformulated as nonlinear Volterra integral equations using the Duhamel principle. Full trajectories are represented by finite polynomial expansions, with temporal coefficient fields determined over the bake interval by Anderson-accelerated Picard iteration, thereby avoiding sequential time marching. In 3-D PEB simulations, we employ Chebyshev and Legendre temporal bases while evaluating the Duhamel integrals using the corresponding Gaussian quadrature rules. We demonstrate that the proposed method simulates a large domain of $1200\times1200\times100$ voxels on the order of one minute. The representation is further incorporated into a physics-informed neural operator (PINO) with a mixed-transform variant of a factorized Fourier neural operator, which is trained without precomputed solution trajectories. Together, the integral formulation and its PINO extension enable accelerated, time-marching-free prediction of concentration fields at arbitrary times within the bake interval.

cross Latent Annealed Posterior Sampling with a Variance-Preserving Bridge for Sparsely Observed Random Fields

Authors: Jiankun Lei, Tian Qi

Abstract: Posterior inference for random fields from sparse observations presents two major challenges: accurately characterizing complex spatial uncertainty and preventing conditional sampling costs from growing substantially as the spatial grid is refined. To address these challenges, we propose latent annealed posterior sampling (LAPS). LAPS first maps the random field into a latent space with reduced dimensionality, while a coordinate decoder composed of Branch and Trunk networks enables field evaluation at arbitrary spatial locations. Flow matching is then used to learn the latent prior distribution. Based on this learned prior, posterior sampling is performed entirely in the latent space, with the observation likelihood incorporated progressively through an annealing procedure. To control local covariance inflation, we introduce a variance-preserving (VP) bridge that allocates a covariance budget between the transported latent state and a Gaussian perturbation. Numerical experiments demonstrate that LAPS accurately approximates posterior distributions, preserves distinct posterior modes, and reduces the dependence of conditional sampling costs on spatial resolution. Across two Gaussian benchmarks, LAPS attains small errors against exact posterior references, with sliced Wasserstein distances only 2 to 3 percent higher than the reference values obtained from exact posterior samples. In a bimodal posterior problem, LAPS reduces the sliced Wasserstein distance from 0.2693 to 0.1499 and consistently recovers both modes across all test scenarios. For a two dimensional Navier-Stokes random field, LAPS substantially improves marginal posterior calibration, while its evaluation time remains nearly constant as the output grid is refined from 64^2 to 512^2. These results demonstrate that LAPS achieves a favorable tradeoff among posterior accuracy, uncertainty calibration, and computational efficiency.

cross Graph Additive Models disentangling feature, topology, and interactions

Authors: Clara Alvarez-Rodriguez, Steve Azzolin, Vincenzo Marco De Luca, Filippo Maria Bianchi, Andrea Passerini, Antonio Longa

Abstract: Graph Neural Additive Networks (GNANs) have emerged as effective, interpretable-by-design architectures for graph learning. They achieve transparency by combining per-feature shape functions with simple distance-based reweighting. However, GNANs have three limitations. First, node-wise relevance scores aggregate information from all graph nodes, allowing irrelevant nodes to inherit spurious relevance from relevant ones. Second, topology is represented only through shortest-path distances, so structurally distinct graphs may share identical distance profiles. Third, feature and distance information are combined multiplicatively, entangling feature, distance, and interaction effects in a single rescalable term. We introduce AMIGA, which represents topology through a vocabulary of mined motifs and decomposes each node contribution into three additive channels: feature, structure, and feature-structure interaction. The channels are centered around a graph-balanced training baseline, making the decomposition well-defined and unique under the chosen reference distribution. Across ten graph classification and regression benchmarks, AMIGA outperforms GNAN on most datasets and narrows the gap with standard black-box GNNs. Because each channel is centered and assigned to a single node, AMIGA produces node-local explanations that reconstruct the model output exactly and support interpretation at multiple levels of granularity.

cross Evaluating Model Capabilities After Benchmark Saturation

Authors: Morio Hamada, Jehyeok Yeon, Matteo Migliarini, Justin Olive, Magda Dubois

Abstract: Rapid advances in AI capabilities are causing benchmarks to saturate. Once several models approach perfect scores, a benchmark loses its power to differentiate between them, and with it its utility for informing AI safety cases and frontier policies. Yet benchmark items produce far more information than the scores we typically extract from them: a full transcript of how each model solved the task. We investigate whether these transcripts can be used to assess model capabilities even after benchmark scores have saturated. We simulate saturation retrospectively using historical benchmark and model releases, and test whether protocols that access only correctly solved transcripts from saturated benchmarks can predict capabilities measured on held-out, unsaturated items. We evaluate representative contemporary approaches for extracting information from model transcripts: pairwise judging, single-transcript absolute scoring, and predictions from deterministic transcript features or embeddings. We find that saturated transcripts retain capability information beyond benchmark accuracy. Amongst our evaluated protocols, pairwise judging performs best, reducing prediction error by 5.3 times compared to a baseline using only saturated benchmark scores. This capability-relevant information is distributed across transcript sections, but concentrated in specific channels of information such as reasoning and tool-calls. However, the predictive power of protocols does not always generalise across distribution shifts -- for example by underestimating the capabilities of the first reasoning models introduced in early 2025. Saturated benchmarks thus retain capability-predicting signal in their task transcripts, but extending the lifetime of existing benchmarks requires methods designed to extract these signals robustly.

cross CascadeLedger: Counterfactual Auditing of Information and Resource Integrity in LLM Cascades

Abstract: We introduce CascadeLedger, a counterfactual execution-audit framework for budgeted language-model cascades. cross Value-Informed Schedule Trimming for Accelerated Sequential Monte Carlo Guidance in Discrete Diffusion

Authors: Jaehyeon Kim, Ziseok Lee, Siyoung Choi, Kyungsu Kim

Abstract: Reward-guided discrete diffusion enables test-time alignment with downstream objectives, but applying Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) guidance at every denoising step is computationally expensive. Active guidance steps require additional reward evaluations and, for gradient-based proposals, value-gradient computations. Sparse guidance reduces this cost by guiding only a subset of timesteps, but commonly used schedules are fixed heuristics whose effectiveness can vary substantially across tasks. We propose VISTA, a training-free method that automatically selects guided timesteps under a fixed budget. VISTA formulates sparse guidance as approximation of the ideal full-guidance probability path and derives an estimable upper bound on \(\ell_1\) path deviation. Across text, biological sequence, and image generation tasks, VISTA discovers task-dependent schedules and outperforms hand-designed sparse schedules in most tested settings, while approaching or exceeding full-guidance performance on several task metrics. cross On the Stability and Convergence of Flow Map Training

Authors: Ishan Nath, Tom Stanic, Avishek Joey Bose, Gauthier Gidel, Quentin Bertrand

Abstract: Flow maps are a recent method of probabilistic generation that amortize diffusion trajectories into one-step samplers, unlocking efficient generation. While progress has been made in improving their empirical performance, there is still a lack of understanding behind the mechanisms underlying their success. Specifically, many flow map learning methods employ stop gradient operators as a crucial part of their training target. Adaptive weighting also drastically improves the generation capabilities of flow maps. Although many interpretations have been proposed to support the inclusion of these heuristics, none have analyzed the impact of these assumptions on the training dynamics of flow maps themselves. In this work, we address this issue by providing grounded theory explaining why such tricks are required. Analyzing the functional gradient flow induced by the MeanFlow and Lagrangian losses, we show their convergence to the true flow map, settling the validity of these objectives. For finite updates, we highlight a severe roadblock to the usage of these losses in practice: the possible unboundedness of the update. Adaptive normalization not only provides bounded updates, but, combined with a specific weighting, additionally provides convergence guarantees towards a neighborhood of the solution. On synthetic data, we show that our functional gradient flow analysis exactly describes the true training dynamics for flow maps. Experiments on standard image datasets demonstrate that a proper reweighting scheme is essential for strong empirical performance, which is only achieved for a specific range of reweighting strengths.

Authors: Zhanke Zhou, Zongze Li, Weikai Huang, Xuan Li, Chentao Cao, Xiao Feng, Xiangyu Lu, Jinbo Hu, Yi Xie, Nico Pelleriti, Shiyang Liu, Menghan Lu, Max Zimmer, Brando Miranda, Jiangchao Yao, Bo Liu, Sanmi Koyejo, Sebastian Pokutta, Bo Han

Abstract: Agents pair reasoning models with harnesses that organize model calls, execute tools, load skill instructions, and retain history in memory. cross Purify Before You Align: Improving Representation Alignment for Diffusion Models

Authors: Yingheng Wang, Yaoqiang Li, Yaqin Wu, Junwen Bai, Jiatao Gu, Christopher De Sa, Volodymyr Kuleshov

Abstract: Representation alignment improves and accelerates diffusion training by encouraging intermediate features to match a pretrained visual encoder. cross When Reality Outpaces Cognition: A New Scaling Principle for Reliable Intelligence

Authors: Ahsan Adeel, Talha Bin Riaz

Abstract: This paper challenges the understanding capabilities of existing AI paradigms and emerging world models by asking a fundamental question: How quickly can reality change before an intelligent system can no longer reliably perceive, reason, and act? We formulate this as a theory- and model-agnostic challenge in which systems must infer reliable actions for increasingly distant future states across a growing observation--reality gap. We operationalize this challenge through the effective Speed of Coherent Cognition (\(S_C^{\mathrm{eff}}\)), defined as the highest tested Speed of Reality (\(S_R\)) through which a system continuously satisfies a predefined behavioural reliability criterion from baseline. As a proof of concept, reinforcement-learning agents are trained at the baseline \(S_R\) and evaluated, without retraining, as \(S_R\) is increased by up to \(50\times\) while computational resources remain fixed. We compare Transformer and gated-MLP baselines with \(Co^4\), an architecture inspired by cellular mechanisms implicated in conscious processing and awake thought. Across the tested scenarios, \(Co^4\) achieves substantially higher \(S_C^{\mathrm{eff}}\) than the baselines, motivating \(S_C^{\mathrm{eff}}\) as a new benchmark and the scaling of coherent cognition as a principle for reliable AI.

cross Verdict: Encode Once, Decide Many

Authors: Avdhesh Singh Chouhan, Jack Perry

Abstract: Automating organizational work means answering many small typed questions about one piece of state (does this email need a reply, how urgent is it, who owns it) with probabilities good enough to act on without a human. The open typed-decision model we compare against, like a standard cross-encoder, answers each question with a separate pass over the input. We present Verdict-MQ, an open encoder that reads a case once and answers all of its questions: given identical retained state tokens after truncation (verified on every reported case), a structured attention mask makes the state encoding independent of the questions, keeps questions from influencing each other, and makes option order irrelevant by construction. On the typed-decisions benchmark, a 395M Verdict-MQ matches the soft gold labels significantly better than the released 421M Laya specialist on all five fresh seeds (KL 0.080 vs 0.117, Brier 0.045 vs 0.061), an advantage that survives per-system temperature scaling; its higher accuracy (0.801 vs 0.767) lies past the benchmark's own saturation point, so we read that margin as closer agreement with the benchmark's labelling teacher. On RACE, BoolQ and MultiRC, against a matched fine-tuned baseline it wins on MultiRC and loses on RACE and on BoolQ, where its training is unstable; we measure zero-shot references ourselves, including a closed general system (Jev) that beats every fine-tuned encoder. A controlled comparison measures what the shared state costs: giving each question its own question-aware copy of the state gains 0.9-3.5 points on RACE and MultiRC at 1.4-2.5x the per-case latency, and nothing measurable on typed decisions, with the invariance guarantees unchanged.

cross Privacy-Preserving Text Inference for Health-Misinformation Classification and Semantic Retrieval

Authors: Jasleen Kaur, Chlo\'e Cachet, Koray Karabina, Jean Belo Klamti, Philippe Lamontagne

Abstract: Health-misinformation analysis presents a dual challenge: misleading claims can affect public well-being, while systems designed to detect them may expose sensitive user-submitted text. cross Feasibility Ceilings Before Learning: Sizing and Timing Bounds for Drone-Mesh Interception

Authors: Dmytro Babarytskyi

Abstract: A mesh of interceptors defending a fixed site must spread over the predicted arrival point of an approaching object. This is a recurring task in multi-agent robotics: N agents, each covering a disc of radius r, spread over a probability density so that a point drawn from it falls within some agent's disc. We give closed forms for the quantities that bound performance before any policy is chosen, and validate each by Monte Carlo. The uncertainty of a point predicted by extrapolating a noisy linear track follows from least-squares variance propagation plus a delta-method term for the uncertain arrival time, agreeing with simulation to within 1.8%. The coverage probability of a packed mesh admits a closed form accurate to 0.007 absolute, with a volumetric analogue. A naive area ratio misses a ceiling: while agents stay at least 2r apart, no mesh exceeds 90.69% coverage in the plane or 74.05% in a volume. This ceiling belongs to the separation constraint, not to the problem - permitting overlap substitutes Kershner's thinnest-covering density and removes it, the better regime above a crossover at p=0.80. Hexagonal packing is optimal among five arrangements; the centroidal-Voronoi configuration coverage control supplies reaches only half its coverage at small budgets. The Gaussian assumption is conservative under heavy tails and anisotropy but fragile under bias: a one-sigma aim-point offset costs 14 points of coverage. A repositioning team must also choose a commit time, and the admissible window can be empty. Against the applicable ceiling, analytic placement attains 85% of what the configuration permits while centralised PPO averages 15% over three trainer seeds, with a paired bootstrap against a null policy containing zero at every seed. We argue such ceilings should be computed before any learning experiment and quoted alongside every result.

cross Linear Independence of Polynomial Compositions with Bounded Input Degree

Abstract: We prove the weak composition-independence conjectures of Kohn, Marchetti, Massarenti and Mella. cross CoTranslator: Reverse to Forward Chain-of-Thought Translator for Reasoning Distillation

Authors: Zinan Tang, Zhuoshi Pan, Kairui Chen, Ze Yan, Shaomian Zheng, Yukun Zhang, Gang Wang, Biqing Huang

Abstract: Reasoning distillation requires chain-of-thought (CoT) data with correct answers, but obtaining such data efficiently remains challenging. Forward CoT generation (FCG) followed by rejection sampling (RS) produces verified, question-only responses, yet may require many teacher calls for difficult problems. Reverse CoT generation (RCG) is cheaper because it conditions on the known answer, but access to this answer can lead to post-hoc rationalization and a mismatch with the student's answer-free inference setting. We propose CoTranslator, which learns to translate reverse CoT responses toward the distribution of verified forward responses. Without retraining, the same translator transfers to a new mathematics dataset, a different teacher, and STEM problems, improving average STEM accuracy by $4.88$ and $4.31$ percentage points for 4B and 8B students, respectively. These results show that CoTranslator offers an efficient and transferable approach to reasoning distillation.

cross Confidence-Calibrated Inference Expansion for Evaluator-Guided Test-Time Reasoning

Authors: Weida Liang, Kenji Kawaguchi

Abstract: Evaluator-guided reasoning presents a local control problem at every step: trust the current answer, return a fallback, or spend more inference. We formulate this choice as a compute-aware three-action controller, scoring terminal actions by predicted answer success and expansion by a rollout-based continuation-success surrogate, with compute cost priced explicitly. A local action-gap analysis characterizes when score errors can change a decision, while an expected regret bound accounts for errors along the realized expansion path. Guided by this analysis, we learn and calibrate action-specific confidence estimates, with local uncertainty penalties for ambiguous decisions. On held-out width expansion and revision tasks, the controller improves observed utility over calibration-selected fixed policies while reducing normalized online compute by about 30% and 16%, respectively. cross A Phenomenology of Neural Geometry in Vision-Language-Action Models

Authors: Mozes Jacobs, Aiden Swann, Mathilde Papillon, Siddharth Boppana, Fenil R. Doshi, Usha Bhalla, Thomas Icard, Matt Feiszli, Thomas McGrath, Vasudev Shyam, Demba Ba, Leon Bergen, Nina Miolane, Owen Lewis, Jack Merullo, Ekdeep Singh Lubana, Atticus Geiger, Matthew Kowal, Thomas Fel

Abstract: Robotic manipulation is inherently geometric, but what shape does the world take inside the policy that performs it? Prior work on vision-language-action (VLA) policies establishes that task-relevant variables are linearly readable from activations, yet readability certifies only that a variable is present and says nothing about the shape its values trace, which may be curved and multi-dimensional. Using controlled sweeps, natural rollouts, linear probes, and activation interventions, we build a taxonomy of seven task-relevant concepts in VLA models. We find that realized geometry is predominantly multi-dimensional and is shaped by the constraints under which the policy acts. Physical joint limits, for example, leave wrist orientation an open arc rather than a circle, and distance to the target, a single scalar, is realized in two orthogonal subspaces, a coarse ruler for the approach and a fine ruler for the grasp. The same geometries reappear in two further VLA models on real-world robot data, indicating that they are neither model-specific nor artifacts of simulation. We demonstrate how the findings are actionable by steering along the geometry a concept realizes. Overall, this work lays out an initial phenomenology of realized geometry in VLA policies and demonstrates that a concept's shape carries as much importance as its readability.

cross Analog RF Computing for Integrated Data and AI Flows Over Wireless Networks

Authors: Wentao Yu, Vincent W. S. Wong

Abstract: Many resource-constrained edge devices require artificial intelligence (AI) services but are not powerful enough to store or execute neural networks (NNs). Analog radio frequency (RF) computing addresses this limitation by letting a base station (BS) multicast a waveform encoding NN weights to different clients. Each client then multiplies the received weight-encoded waveform with its locally generated input-encoded waveform through a passive mixer in its RF frontend, thereby realizing the matrix-vector multiplications (MVMs) needed for AI inference with an extremely low power and memory requirement. This can enable telecom operators to provide AI inference services to the edge devices connected to their networks. In practice, however, this multicast AI flow must share the spectrum, power, and spatial resources with other data flows. This paper considers a multi-user multiple-input multiple-output (MU-MIMO) downlink scenario and studies the resulting relative aggregate data rate reduction from integrating the multicast AI flow with the unicast data flows. We formulate a joint beamforming problem for the data and AI flows to maximize the aggregate data rate subject to computing accuracy, client energy, total power, and hardware-related constraints. We develop a weighted minimum mean-square error (WMMSE)-based algorithm to solve the problem. Numerical results based on 3rd Generation Partnership Project (3GPP) channels show that the relative aggregate data rate reduction from integrating an AI flow with data flows is below 5% in representative settings. This indicates that telecom operators can use analog RF computing to provide edge AI services while providing data communication services simultaneously.

cross Complete or Sparse: A Tale of Two Identifiabilities

Authors: Junze Zhou, David Klindt

Abstract: Understanding when neural networks learn interpretable representations is central to mechanistic interpretability. We study this through identifiability: when can supervised learning recover the true latent variables underlying the data? We introduce two data generating processes distinguished by their causal direction where either labels cause latents (generative) or latents cause labels (discriminative). When the representation has at least as many dimensions as the latent space, we prove that the optimal encoders are linear in the latents. Conversely, when dimensions are insufficient, identifiability generically fails and encoders become nonlinear. We then show that additional constraints yield recovery of individual latent variables, not just linear mixtures. In the generative setting, non-negative activations and energy efficiency force each neuron to encode exactly one latent, providing a precise account of selective grandmother cell-like responses. In the discriminative setting, when latents are sparse and the true measurement matrix satisfies the spark condition, the learned encoder inherits compressed sensing structure, enabling sparse dictionary learning to perfectly recover individual latents. This constitutes, to our knowledge, the first identifiability result for undercomplete nonlinear independent component analysis. Our framework unifies nonlinear ICA, compressed sensing, and neural network interpretability, delineating exactly when representations transition from nonlinear distortions to linear representations and, under additional constraints or steps, to clean, axis-aligned codes. Theoretical predictions are validated on synthetic benchmarks and on activations drawn from vision models, large language models, and macaque inferotemporal cortex.

cross Does Morphological Correctness Matter for Tokenization? A Scrambled Control Study in Tagalog

Authors: Abdul Rehman Antall, Baidya Nath Saha

Abstract: Typology-aware tokenizers are motivated by the claim that segmentation which respects morpheme boundaries preserves information that frequency-driven segmentation destroys. Studies routinely establish that it helps while assuming the boundaries must be linguistically correct. We separate them. Tagalog marks voice with infixes (s-um-ulat 'wrote', s-in-ulat 'was written'), so the root is not contiguous and no sequence of BPE merges can recover it. We build MorphAustro-Tok, a tokenizer that emits the root as a contiguous unit followed by a positional infix token, and compare it against a corpus-matched BPE baseline and, at two of three fertility levels, against a scrambled-morpheme control that preserves morph count, invertibility and per-type consistency while randomising the pseudo-infix. cross Time-partitioning Gaussian Process Mixture Model

Authors: Guilherme Piantino, Hedibert F. Lopes

Abstract: We propose the Time-partitioning Gaussian Process Mixture Model (TPGPMM), a Bayesian nonparametric framework for identifying persistent regimes and modelling nonstationary dependence in time series. The model combines an adaptive Pitman--Yor prior on contiguous segmentations with regime-specific Gaussian processes, without imposing Markovian within-regime dependence. Learning the Pitman--Yor discount parameter allows adaptation to homogeneous and heterogeneous regime lengths. A split--merge Metropolis-within-Gibbs sampler with exact Gibbs boundary updates jointly learns change points and model hyperparameters, quantifying uncertainty over segmentations and within-regime dynamics. With an RBF kernel and a fixed, conservative observation-noise level, TPGPMM favours sparse segmentations that distinguish persistent shifts from transient shocks. Simulations demonstrate recovery of the regime structure. Applications to the CBOE Volatility Index (VIX) and the 10-year United States Treasury yield identify regimes aligned with major market and monetary-policy events. Variants that estimate observation noise improve predictive accuracy relative to the main specification, but can produce less sparse or less event-aligned segmentations. A variant with regime-specific means fits better than a fitted stationary Gaussian process and forecasts about as accurately. These results highlight a trade-off between interpretable regime identification and predictive accuracy.

cross SNOAR: Self-training with Noise filtering, Observation adding, and Remixing for Speech Denoising Without Clean References

Authors: Samuele Cornell, Matthew Maciejewski

Abstract: Unsupervised speech denoising methods such as MixIT and noisy-target training can be trained directly on noisy recordings, avoiding the sim-to-real gap of synthetic training data, but remain well below supervised performance. Teacher--student self-training, as in RemixIT, narrows this gap by remixing a teacher's estimates into new mixtures that supervise a student; however, since the student is trained only toward the teacher's estimates, teacher errors, in particular over-suppressed speech, are never recovered and propagate as the teacher is updated from the student. We propose SNOAR, a self-training framework that corrects the teacher's estimates before use: the noise leaked into its speech estimate is removed by differential noise filtering (DNF), and part of the noisy observation is added back in to restore suppressed speech and attenuate artifacts. We show that the two corrections depend on each other, as without DNF the loop collapses to the identity mapping. Compared to RemixIT, SNOAR is more stable along training and outperforms it in signal-based, perceptual, and word error rate metrics, reaching 97\% of the SI-SDR improvement of a fully-supervised topline on WHAM!; on real CHiME-3 recordings, it improves every metric of three different teachers, while RemixIT degrades their perceptual quality.
