Authors: Felix Parker, Danielle A. Ripsman, Emmett Springer, Kimia Ghobadi

Abstract: Learned warm starts for the simplex method build starting bases using predictions of which variables will be basic at optimality. Prior work has shown that starting bases predicted by graph neural networks can sharply reduce iteration counts, but the time saved may fall well short of the iterations saved. On 25 classes of linear programs (LPs), we find that a strong supervised basis predictor removes two thirds of the dual simplex's iterations but only a third of its time. Two effects explain the gap: the iterations removed by a good start tend to be cheap, and closeness to an optimal basis does not rank starts by cost. We prove that two starts with equal overlap with an optimal basis and equal pivot counts can differ in linear-algebra work by a factor that grows with the square root of the number of rows. Empirically, across the starts of one LP, the pivot count explains 71% of the variance of log solve time, while the CPU instruction count explains 98.5%. We introduce CostStart, which uses supervised prediction for the basis and trains a small policy on measured instruction counts to choose, per LP, how many predicted columns to use, which algorithm and pricing rule to run, and whether to warm-start at all. On unseen LPs of the training classes, including LPs four times the training size, CostStart roughly halves the solve time relative to the solver's default start and is 1.4 to 2.2 times as fast as two published methods retrained on the same data.

cross Stochastic Gradient Descent: A Nonlinear Time Series Perspective

Authors: Jiaqi Li, Zhipeng Lou, Stefan Richter, Wei Biao Wu

Abstract: This paper investigates the statistical behavior of Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD) with constant step size under the framework of iterated random functions. Compared to previous studies establishing the convergence of SGD in probability measure, e.g., Wasserstein distance, our approach directly provides the convergence in Euclidean distance by showing the Geometric Moment Contraction (GMC) of SGD. cross Defining and Generating Compositional Structure

Authors: Eric Elmoznino, Tejas Kasetty, Blaise Ag\"uera y Arcas, Yoshua Bengio, Guillaume Lajoie

Abstract: Compositional structure is pervasive in nature and across scales: atoms combine into molecules, molecules into cells, and cells into organisms, while further compositional hierarchies appear within each level. More broadly, compositionality, the reuse and recombination of parts into novel structures, is a fundamental source of interesting complexity in both natural and artificial systems. Yet despite its importance, and despite our apparent ease in recognizing it, mathematical accounts capturing what we intuitively mean by compositional structure remain limited. Here, we fill this gap by formalizing a simple idea: compositional structure emerges when parts, considered together, reveal shared structure that is not captured when those parts are modeled separately. We express this idea using algorithmic information theory, the mathematics of information and compression, which provides a natural way to identify structure in arbitrary data without specifying in advance what that structure must look like. Applied across scales, our definition informs where and how compositional structure emerges within an object. Using tractable approximations, we then invert the framework to generate compositional objects: we specify the desired form of their compositional structure without prescribing their particular content, and optimize directly over strings and images. The resulting objects exhibit readily recognizable hierarchical reuse and part-whole organization, suggesting that the definition captures compositional structure rather than merely complexity, regularity, or shared information. Finally, we discuss implications for machine learning, including a new perspective on depth in neural architectures and principles for constructing compositional data curricula capable of supporting open-ended learning.

cross Ranked Right, Sized Wrong: Ensemble Spread Fails Silently at Streamflow Extremes

Authors: Justin Gallant, Tyler Wilson

Abstract: Deep learning models, particularly Long Short-Term Memory networks, are increasingly used for operational streamflow forecasting, with ensemble spread providing a signal of predictive uncertainty. As climate change increases the frequency of hydroclimatic extremes, these models will increasingly operate under flow conditions that are rare in their historical records. We therefore evaluate whether ensemble spread remains informative under increasing flows, examining both its magnitude relative to error and its ability to rank errors. Prediction error is approximately $2\times$ the corrected spread at ordinary flows and up to $4\times$ at the highest flows, with the discrepancy increasing with flow (log-log slope $1.30$, 95% CI: $[1.20,1.41]$). Under-dispersion is strongest in observed extreme flows beyond the training record, reaching an $18\times$ discrepancy. This is pervasive and not explainable by conditional mean bias. Area Under the Sparsification Error, a standard distribution-free metric of uncertainty ranking, describing how well a model knows when it is uncertain, indicates near-oracle ranking for ensemble spread ($0.071$), yet ordering by predicted discharge alone nearly matches it. Informative uncertainty ranking can coexist with severe under-dispersion. In deployment, error ranking cannot be verified, yet the spread is available, and it is relied on as the uncertainty signal under increasingly extreme conditions, while systematically understating risk, most severely for the extreme events that matter most.

cross LEV: Thinking Fast and Deep with Looped Decision Models

Authors: Dingjie Song, Zhiling Yan, Yuxuan Zhang, Lichao Sun

Abstract: Decision models answer typed questions about an input with one probability per option, computed in a single forward pass without generating text. Like the fast System 1 of dual-process theory, they answer at once, but their depth is fixed at training time, so they spend the same computation on easy and hard questions. Models that reason in generated text can think longer, but they pay for every extra step in decoded tokens. We present LEV, a decision model that thinks deeper by rerunning its layers in a loop instead of generating tokens. LEV builds on a language model pretrained as a loop, reads the options after every loop step and trains all steps jointly, so one checkpoint can answer at any depth. On three looped backbones, accuracy rises with depth on the same weights, mostly on hard questions, and a 2.6B LEV goes from 63.2% at depth one to 76.2% at depth four on JevBench. On-demand depth, set per question by a small stopper with a threshold fitted on unlabelled requests, saves 15% to 24% of the computation at a cost of under one accuracy point, level with or slightly above a fixed mix of depths. On a locked test set, the 1.4B and 3B models are 5.4 to 8.8 points more accurate than dense (non-looped) models and cascades of the same latency below 50 ms.

cross MIDI-REPA: Improving Text-to-Music Generation with Symbolic Representation Alignment

Authors: Song-Ze Yu, Fang-Duo Tsai, Yi-Hsuan Yang

Abstract: State-of-the-art text-to-music models increasingly rely on scaling model capacity, data, and compute, while still learning musical structure directly from dense audio. Yet melody, rhythm, harmony, and structure can be represented compactly in symbolic form. We introduce MIDI-REPA, a representation-alignment framework that derives MIDI supervision from automatic music transcription (AMT) to align intermediate representations of a flow-matching DiT. Building on the 122M-parameter FluxAudio-S baseline from the 2026 ICME Academic Text-To-Music Challenge, we investigate what, where, and when to align. Aligning a MuScriptor-derived piano roll with an early DiT layer throughout the full flow trajectory performs the best. Without additional training data or techniques, MIDI-REPA remains competitive on the ATTM objective metrics while remaining trainable on a single RTX 3090 in about 31 hours. Our results highlight symbolic supervision as a simple and inexpensive learning signal for improving text-to-music generation. We release our transcription pipeline, JamendoMIDI, training code, and audio samples at https://midi-repa.vaclis.net.

URLs: https://midi-repa.vaclis.net.

cross Two-Stage Score Estimation for Group-Specific Diffusion Models

Authors: Huitao Yang, Oscar Hernan Madrid Padilla

Abstract: Generative modeling across related distributions raises a question: how can shared structure support accurate generation for a target distribution with limited observations? We develop a statistical framework for diffusion transfer that analyzes a shared pooled score together with a target-specific offset. Under Besov regularity of the pooled density and a stable hierarchical composition model for the offset, we derive convergence rates that separate the costs of learning these components and identify when a simpler offset yields a faster risk-bound rate than separate fitting. Under suitable boundary and regularity assumptions, we prove that the offset remains bounded and jointly regular as diffusion time approaches zero. These results allow the lower time cutoff to vanish polynomially and provide total-variation guarantees for diffusion models as density estimators. Synthetic simulations and image benchmarks provide a proof of concept for the framework.

cross The Blume-Capel Model as a $\phi^6$ Field Theory: A Monte Carlo and Flow Model Study

Authors: Ejaaz Merali, Ryan Larson, Ehsan Khatami, Richard T. Scalettar

Abstract: We report Monte Carlo simulations of a continuous spin variant of the Blume-Capel model. We compute the phase diagrams in the crystal-field-temperature plane, as a function of the softness of the spins. We show that normalizing flows, a class of generative models capable of direct sampling without autocorrelation, are effective samplers across the phase diagram, including in the first-order transition region and in the vicinity of the tricritical point. Finite-size scaling analyses of flow samples locate the first- and second-order transitions as well as the tricritical point with accuracy comparable to canonical and extended-ensemble Monte Carlo.

cross One Bias Score, Two Factors: How Mitigations Affect Option Bias in Multiple-Choice Evaluation

Authors: Hyunjong Ok, Minjae Park, Jaeho Lee

Abstract: Reordering multiple-choice options can change a model's answer, an effect studied as selection bias (a preference for certain option letters) and as order sensitivity (changes in answers across option orderings). We connect these perspectives through the pooled selection-rate standard deviation (pooled SD), which measures how unevenly option letters are selected across questions and orderings. When every option of each question appears equally often at each position, the pooled SD is exactly the product of two label-free factors: the flip rate and the positional skew. The flip rate is the fraction of questions whose chosen option changes across orderings; the positional skew is the standard deviation of option-letter selection rates among those questions. Across 41 checkpoints and 12 benchmarks, the flip rate tracks accuracy and output uncertainty, while the positional skew tracks the estimated option-symbol prior. Prior correction for selection bias mainly reduces the positional skew. Aggregating each ordering with its reversal, a mitigation for order sensitivity, mainly reduces the flip rate. Both reduce the range of accuracy across correct-answer letters by similar amounts, but aggregation leaves fewer model-rank reversals. Applying prior correction before aggregation reduces both factors and further narrows this accuracy range. However, aggregation lowers the flip rate partly by settling questions on the same wrong option. We therefore recommend reporting both factors to distinguish mitigation effects, alongside accuracy to distinguish stability from correctness.

cross EigDiff: Eigensample Diffusion - or how to significantly reduce training dataset size with marginal performance variation?

Authors: Andrei Jarca, Vlad Hondru, Florinel-Alin Croitoru, Radu Tudor Ionescu

Abstract: Diffusion models are notoriously expensive to train, requiring large-scale datasets to learn how to gradually reverse the noise from images over multiple small denoising steps. Current approaches focus on distilling the number of diffusion steps, neglecting potential redundancies of the training data. In this paper, we explore the potential of harnessing eigensamples to train diffusion models on significantly smaller datasets, while preserving model performance. To mitigate the sensitivity to affine transformations of eigenimages, we harness the latent space of a pre-trained CLIP model. We next compute latent eigenvectors by performing principal component analysis (PCA) in CLIP space. Further, we employ eigenvectors as reference points to guide the selection of a representative training set comprising comprising both exemplars (images whose embeddings are closest to eigenvectors) and diverse images. Our comprehensive experiments with different diffusion models applied on multiple datasets indicate that our approach (EigDiff) can reduce training data by up to 14x and accelerate subset selection by up to 1607x, with marginal performance differences.

cross Can LLMs Anticipate Their Cruxes?

Authors: David Reber, James Reber, Ari Holtzman, Victor Veitch

Abstract: To use language models to help us make decisions we would like to understand the rationale behind the language model's outputs. In principle, the language model provides its rationale as part of its text output. However, the sheer volume of text makes this hard to use in practice. In this paper, we are interested in extracting the cruxes that lead a model to recommend one action over another. We take a crux to be a sub-question that a model's rollouts disagree on, where changing its value may have altered the conclusion. A crux is therefore a property of the distribution of a model's rollouts, not of any one rollout. We consider three strategies of increasing computational cost. First, we just ask a model to identify, a priori, what the cruxes of a decision will be. Second, we have the model actually go through the process of generating a response and then extract the cruxes that arose in its reasoning. Finally, we generate many responses and extract the cruxes by comparing the reasoning across the rollouts. We make this last procedure computationally tractable with cheap calls to a reranker, an estimated 86 times less compute than a judge. We then conduct an extensive empirical evaluation to assess whether the computationally inexpensive options are sufficient. They are not. Across domains and models, we find that a model's a priori assessment of the cruxes has little relation to the factors that are actually identified as important a posteriori. Using a single rollout helps, but is still inadequate for identifying the full set of cruxes. Accordingly, for high-stakes decisions (and to understand the space of language model decision making) the many rollout procedure is unavoidable.

cross Audio-Visual Dog Identification and Tracking in Real-World Videos

Authors: Aryahvishwa Babu, Kingsley Jin, Lillian Wang, Tuan M. Dang, Theron S. Wang, Jeorge Joshi, Annie Cao, Kenny Q. Zhu

Abstract: Identifying individual dogs from barks in real-world videos remains challenging and largely unexplored. Solving this problem would enable long-term behavioral analysis, veterinary monitoring, and animal welfare monitoring by attributing vocalizations and behaviors to the correct individual. Real-world videos are challenging because multiple dogs may bark simultaneously, become occluded, or move in and out of view. Prior studies show that canine vocalizations and visual appearance contain discriminative identity information, but these modalities have largely been studied independently. We present a multimodal dog-fingerprinting framework combining visual identification, multiple-object tracking, and canine vocal fingerprinting to associate bark events with individual dogs while maintaining persistent profiles. The framework synchronizes bark events with tracked dogs and updates profiles using visual appearance and vocal characteristics, enabling identification despite occlusion, overlapping vocalizations, and off-screen barking. We also introduce a benchmark of 46 real-world videos containing 213 manually annotated bark events. Our framework correctly localizes the active visible dog in 61.83% of visible events at IoU $\geq 0.30$ and achieves a 91.33% F1 score for visible versus off-screen barking detection.

cross Acceleration and its Limits in Gaussian Variational Inference

Authors: Jaesung R. Park, Uijeong Jang, Yifan Chen

Abstract: We study whether the classical momentum-based acceleration that improves the condition-number dependence of gradient descent from $\kappa$ to $\sqrt{\kappa}$ for strongly convex, smooth objectives extends to Gaussian variational inference (GVI) for strongly log-concave, log-smooth target distributions. With exact Gaussian expectations, a convex composite formulation of GVI yields the classical accelerated $\sqrt{\kappa}$ convergence rate. However, when only pointwise derivatives are available, we construct a family of strongly log-concave targets for which any randomized algorithm requires $\kappa^{1-o(1)}$ queries to achieve a fixed objective accuracy, even when the initial objective gap is bounded independently of $\kappa$. Thus curvature bounds alone cannot guarantee uniform accelerated total-query complexity. We then ask which additional structure permits acceleration and give positive examples: under a Hessian-Lipschitz assumption, variance-reduced stochastic methods yield accelerated guarantees when the Hessian variation is sufficiently small. Finally, experiments on real datasets show substantial empirical speedups from acceleration across a broad range of methods.

cross Learning to Read Out: Unembedding Dynamics in Language Model Pretraining

Authors: Matteo He, William F. Shen, Alex Iacob, Andrej Jovanovic, Xinchi Qiu, Nicholas D. Lane

Abstract: When does a language model acquire a capability? Developmental studies usually answer this by tracking when models start producing the correct token. But that prediction passes through the unembedding matrix, or readout, which is itself still being learned during pretraining. We show that a model's hidden states can encode task information before its own readout expresses it, so timelines read from outputs conflate what the model encodes with what its readout expresses. To track the readout directly, we introduce parameter-trajectory crosscoding, which fits one sparse dictionary to every token's readout row at every checkpoint. Unlike crosscoders on activations, it needs only checkpoint weights. We use it to locate a narrow early window, 0.4-1.1% of the way through Pythia's pretraining, in which the readout reorganizes and WordNet word categories become decodable from its rows. By then, linear probes on the hidden states already decode task information that the model's own outputs do not yet show. To test whether such gaps depend on how fast the readout learns, we pretrain small models from scratch that differ only in the readout's learning rate. We find that raising it makes the readout develop earlier and the gap close sooner, with little effect on final loss. Together, these results imply that early in training, where many developmental transitions are dated, a behavioral date can reflect when the readout learns to express task information and not when the model learns it.

cross On the Last-Iterate Convergence of Clipped Gradient Methods

Authors: Zijian Liu

Abstract: In stochastic (strongly) convex optimization, establishing last-iterate convergence guarantees for first-order methods has recently garnered increasing attention, as this not only deepens the theoretical understanding of these algorithms but also aligns more closely with practice. Notably, people have demonstrated that one of the most famous, simple, and popular methods, Stochastic Gradient Descent ($\mathtt{SGD}$), provably achieves last-iterate convergence. However, the existing results may have limited applicability, since they typically rely on strong conditions on the gradient noise (e.g., an exponentially decaying tail) rather than the more realistic heavy-tailed noise. Unfortunately, when faced with heavy-tailed noise, $\mathtt{SGD}$ is known to exhibit undesirable behavior or even fail to converge. To address the challenge of heavy-tailed noise, people have proposed $\mathtt{Clipped}\text{-}\mathtt{SGD}$, an algorithm that combines $\mathtt{SGD}$ with a simple mechanism, gradient clipping. Although $\mathtt{Clipped}\text{-}\mathtt{SGD}$ performs well in practice, its last-iterate convergence property remains highly underexplored. In this work, we prove the first optimal last-iterate rates in high probability for $\mathtt{Clipped}\text{-}\mathtt{SGD}$ under heavy-tailed noise, thereby closing a gap in the literature.

cross Efficient Agnostic Learning of Univariate Densities Under Local Differential Privacy

Authors: Mohammad Afzali, Hassan Ashtiani, Sivan Sabato

Abstract: We propose a general framework for non-interactive locally differentially private (LDP) learning of structured univariate distributions that can be approximated by piece-wise polynomials. This gives the first sample-efficient LDP learners for several distribution classes, including mixtures of log-concave distributions, such as Gaussian mixture models (GMMs). These learners also provide agnostic guarantees and are computationally efficient. We also prove a lower bound for learning GMMs under LDP, improving on the standard pure-DP packing lower bounds of Bun et al. [BKSW19]. To obtain our general learning framework, we develop an efficient non-interactive LDP algorithm for synthetic data generation over large domains, which may be of independent interest. We then use it together with a deterministic adaptation of the non-private piecewise polynomial learner of Acharya et al. [ADLS17].

cross PremiseCheck: Efficient Fact-Checking of Human and AI Peer Reviews

Authors: Dingjie Song, Zhiling Yan, Ziqian Zhang, Yuxuan Zhang, Lichao Sun

Abstract: Peer review is under strain from the growth of submissions, and review comments written by humans or AI can misstate what a paper says. We introduce ReviewFact, a benchmark that labels such factual errors in review comments under one definition across NLP, machine learning, and the natural sciences, and that measures cost and latency alongside accuracy. A frontier LLM checks comments well but must read the whole paper for each check, while cheap decision models, which score whether a statement holds in a text, fall far short on whole comments that mix several implicit assertions. PremiseCheck is a framework that combines the two. An LLM rewrites each comment as explicit premises without reading the paper, and a decision model checks each premise against the paper. When each comment is checked on its own, PremiseCheck reaches accuracy comparable to GPT-5.5 across all three fields at about half its cost, and on NLP reviews at a quarter of its cost and less than half its latency.

cross SHIP: Structured and Hierarchical Insight with Privacy

Authors: Kai Chen, Guisi Ni, Albert Cheu, Kassem Fawaz, Yuzheng Hu, Ryan McKenna, Peter Kairouz

Abstract: Extracting insights from textual data while protecting user privacy is increasingly important. Existing methods produce private insights in diverse forms for different analytical purposes, making their utility difficult to assess and compare. In this work, we formulate differentially private (DP) insights as reusable representations for answering analytical queries, providing a common basis for utility assessment. Answering these queries, however, requires fine-grained details and cross-attribute statistics that existing representations may omit. To capture this information, we introduce Structured and Hierarchical Insight with Privacy (SHIP), which automatically maps queries to attributes, builds a hierarchical hybrid taxonomy, and releases a DP synthetic table for further analysis. We also develop an evaluation protocol based primarily on QA performance and structured fidelity. Our experiments demonstrate SHIP's strong performance on both measures relative to existing private insight baselines, while identifying queries that remain challenging for structured representations.

cross Athanor: An Agent-Native System for Autonomous Post-Training Research

Authors: Yufa Zhou, Yichen Wang, Jiakun Fan, Ziqi Shu, Zhichao Liu, Anru R. Zhang, Neiwen Ling, Zijun Wei

Abstract: Recursive self-improvement, in which AI systems build stronger AI systems, is one promising route to superintelligence. We study a weaker form that can be measured today: frontier agents post-training a small raw base model with public data, as a test of their research potential. We build Athanor, an open agent-native system for autonomous post-training research (AutoPostTrain) on real GPUs. We give the agents full research autonomy, low-friction engineering tools, and efficient starter code. The agents carry out the research themselves: they read datasets and evaluation results, build their training data, write and modify training algorithms, tune hyperparameters, and analyze the results to decide what to try next. They work in a sandbox and cannot use external teacher models. Athanor's services supply frozen evaluation, managed jobs, decontaminated data, a persistent research record, and a held-out suite the agents are unaware of. In a 104-hour campaign, a team of four Claude Opus 5.5 agents, each on an eight-GPU node and given no research advice, improves Qwen3-4B-Base from 15.9 to 67.3 on a six-task development suite and from 9.2 to 48.2 on a held-out suite, against 66.5 and 47.9 for the official Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507. To our knowledge, this is the first demonstration that a team of frontier agents can autonomously post-train a raw base model into a cross-domain generalist model that matches or surpasses the official instruct version on average. The campaign's full record shows that the agents reach this level by recombining known methods and each other's models: SFT on public reasoning data, RL with a rollout change one agent introduced, weight merges across agents, and rounds of self-distillation. With execution handled, today's agents already have the engineering and research ability to conquer this task. We hope Athanor provides a foundation for scaling autonomous AI research.

cross Score Mismatch and Set-Constrained Sampling in Zero-Shot Conditional Discrete Diffusion

Authors: Renxiang Huang, Yuchen Liang, Yingbin Liang, Ness Shroff, Lifeng Lai

Abstract: Discrete diffusion models have become a powerful framework for modeling complex discrete distributions across a range of applications. While theoretical convergence guarantees depend on consistency between the target and training distributions, such a case rarely holds in practice, making score mismatch a central challenge in discrete diffusion sampling. This is particularly relevant to zero-shot conditional generation, where a model trained on the unconditional distribution is directly reused at inference time to sample from a conditional target distribution. In this paper, we provide the first theoretical analysis for score-mismatched discrete diffusion models and derive a general KL divergence upper bound that clearly identifies the additional bias caused by such a mismatch. Our analysis covers partial-observation constraints, including text infilling, sentence completion, and more general coordinatewise set constraints. Specifically, we analyze the classical Conditional SEDD sampler and show that it can incur a bias term that scales with the dimension, vocabulary size, and compatibility. Based on this analysis, we propose a Randomized Hard Clamping (RHC) sampler for coordinatewise set constraints. We show that the assignment probabilities can be expressed exactly by the unconditional scores with absorbing rate. Finally, numerical and real-data experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed RHC sampler.

cross Adaptive Randomized Neural Networks for PDEs: Structure-Aware Feature Redistribution and Residual-Driven Block Enrichment

Authors: You Yang, Fei Wang

Abstract: Randomized neural networks retain an output-linear least-squares solve, but globally sampled random features may fail to capture the spatial and scale structure of PDE solutions. We propose SFRBE--RaNN, a two-stage adaptive trial-space construction. SFR redistributes a fixed feature budget according to regional indicator masses and adapts regional weight scales through low-dimensional residual-based optimization. RBE constructs nonredundant enrichments by screening multiple activation families, removing represented response directions, and selecting the smallest admissible prefix. For solution-dependent operators, candidates are screened with a frozen operator and accepted only after nonlinear residual verification. For fixed linear problems, we establish an exact discrete residual-reduction identity and a conditional solution-error estimate. Building on this identity, we combine safeguarded SFR with RBE capture factors to derive a finite-horizon error bound involving baseline best approximation and empirical consistency, together with a conditional random-search success-probability bound. Numerical experiments on localized, multiscale, and nonlinear space--time problems demonstrate the method's effectiveness.

cross Probe-and-Resume: When and How to Evaluate Agent Execution

Authors: Jiakai Xu, Tiancheng Ge, Tujie Guan, Yusheng Li, Rohan Timmaraju, Eugene Wu, Kostis Kaffes

Abstract: Test-time search can improve agent performance by exploring alternative actions and using a critic to select promising branches. We study this approach in terminal-based computer-use environments, which expose only a partial view of a large underlying state, and find that the Monte Carlo tree search (MCTS) configuration we evaluate does not significantly improve task success. We identify two sources of poor critic performance: the agent's observations omit environment state needed for evaluation, and intermediate states offer limited evidence of whether a trajectory will succeed. At intermediate states, critics rank continuations only modestly better than chance, and environment access improves this little. We study when to invoke a critic and what access to give it to evaluate a node. Our findings motivate a policy that invokes the critic only at nodes the agent believes are final, rather than during partial progress. Further, the policy gives the critic read, write, and execute access to a fork of the computer's current and initial state, allowing it to probe and perform potentially destructive actions to surface any hidden state the trajectory omits. We call the resulting design Probe-and-Resume: when the agent claims to be done, the critic probes a fork of its state, and a rejected agent resumes where it stopped. On Terminal-Bench 2.1, this raises success by 8.4 percentage points over MCTS with conventional critics, and approaches the success rate of the Codex CLI agent with the same underlying model at less than half its cost.

cross ToolBottleneckBench: Measuring Tool-Agent Performance under Controlled Pressures and Fixed Harness Budgets

Authors: Zichuan Xu, Yuyang Dong, Makoto Onizuka, Chuan Xiao

Abstract: The success score of a tool agent reflects both its model and the budgets of the harness that runs it. We present ToolBottleneckBench, a benchmark that measures tool-agent performance under controlled pressures and fixed harness budgets. Synthetic enterprise tasks stay fixed while one pressure varies: the registered tool catalog, the visible tool menu, the certified workflow, or the encoding of returned records. An exact grader records the first unmet obligation of each failed episode and the harness limit, if any, that ended it. Across 21 model and harness configurations, the visible menu is the only one of the three pressures that act on the model with a large penalty, and this penalty tracks truncation at the completion cap. Raising the cap sixteenfold removes most of the penalty in the truncation-heavy configurations. Most failures end at a harness limit, and the failure record identifies the assigned pressure no more reliably than a count of tool calls. The repairs that help change which tools reach the model or who issues the calls, and only two repairs, neither improving model behavior, beat every configuration switch. We recommend reporting pressure effects with the harness budgets under which they were measured. We release the benchmark at https://github.com/zichuanxu/tool-bottleneck-bench.

URLs: https://github.com/zichuanxu/tool-bottleneck-bench.

cross Kolab: Benchmarking Human-Agent Collaboration in Long-Horizon Workflows

Authors: Shirley Anugrah Hayati, Patricia Anugrah Setiani, Ruizi Wang, Zhouyi Li, Khanh Chi Le, Young-Jun Lee, Dongyeop Kang

Abstract: When humans collaborate with AI agents on long-horizon tasks, what happens to the agents' contributions? For solving long-horizon tasks, humans place greater importance on ongoing interaction with AI agents (e.g., revising plans) than on an agent's ability to complete the task in a single attempt. However, most agentic benchmarks have focused more on automation. Strong performance on such benchmarks does not imply effective real-world collaboration. Professional workflows offer a useful setting, as they often involve interdependent steps. To address this gap, we introduce Kolab, a new dataset of 115+ hours of screen recordings of 50 professionals from 40 occupations collaborating with agents on realistic, long-horizon problems. Kolab also contains participants' collaboration and output quality judgments. Our analysis reveals diverse collaboration patterns, highlighting the importance of when and how professionals coordinate with agents. A collaborative agent must do two things that automation benchmarks never test: anticipate whether experts will adopt, modify, or reject its contribution once it leaves the chat window to guide its next steps, and assess collaboration quality from the unfolding workflow without constant expert feedback. Our benchmark operationalizes both: (1) forecasting how professionals use agent contributions at diverse workflows, and (2) verifying their judgments of collaboration satisfaction and output quality. Findings show that frontier models, both agentic and non-agentic, overestimate collaboration with experts and struggle to distinguish between high- and low-quality collaboration. Longer workflow context does not consistently improve these predictions. As frontier agents cannot yet understand long-horizon collaboration with experts, we position Kolab as a valuable dataset for studying human-agent collaboration grounded in real professional workflows.

Authors: Siddhesh Kulkarni

Abstract: We characterize the multiplicative half-Cauchy process through its log-scale random-walk representation, establishing shrinkage, expansion, and critical regimes, almost-sure summability, local rebounds, and effective dimension. cross When is a better LLM worth paying for? Stochastic Optimization with Switching Mixed-Fidelity Oracles

Authors: Swati Gupta, Tianjiao Li

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used upstream of decision-making to clean data, detect label errors, and reconstruct missing values. Yet LLM-assisted data curation creates a new operational tradeoff: higher-fidelity models and richer prompts can be substantially more expensive, while the value of this additional fidelity depends on the downstream optimization accuracy. This raises the central question of this work: when should one pay for a better LLM, given a specific downstream task at hand? We study this question through stochastic optimization with mixed-fidelity first-order oracles, which can have systematic bias and state-dependent variance. We develop two cost-aware algorithmic frameworks that allow adaptive switching between stochastic oracles. With known bias, the first meta-algorithm applies a restarted, cost-aware policy that adaptively selects oracle fidelity across optimization stages. Its cumulative cost is no larger than that of the best fixed oracle in hindsight and can be substantially smaller. With unknown bias, we develop a budgeted certification scheme that uses occasional trusted-gradient evaluations to certify the optimization progress and eliminate unsuccessful oracle sources, and show an explicit tradeoff between certification cost and delayed detection of biased sources. We provide concrete realizations of these meta-algorithms by applying biased stochastic gradient extrapolation (SGE) and recursive regularization under state-dependent noise as subroutines. Synthetic and LLM-completion experiments illustrate the qualitative switching behavior predicted by the theory: the adaptive policy skips several expensive LLM oracles and substantially reduces cumulative cost relative to fixed high-fidelity alternatives while achieving comparable accuracy.

cross Coordination-Free Workload Regularization for Variable-Cost Distributed Training

Authors: Tao Huang, Chen Zhu, Antonio Alonso, Daming Li, Adarsh Goura, Jaideep Ray, Yitong Zhou

Abstract: Synchronous data-parallel training usually assigns every rank the same number of records, even when record costs vary by orders of magnitude. For variable-length models, this converts input variation into idle time because each step waits for its most expensive rank-local batch. We present Batch Cost Lookahead (BCL), a coordination-free data-loading policy that buffers a bounded rank-local window and repartitions it into fixed-cardinality packs with balanced estimated cost. BCL preserves the trainer's record-count contract and requires neither cross-rank metadata exchange nor runtime modification. In simulation over public workload distributions, enabling BCL reduces per-rank estimated batch-cost variability by up to 82.8%. Across 13 NeMo-RL SFT corpora on eight H200 GPUs, rank-local BCL runs 1.050 to 1.167 times faster than pooled MFFD. Compiled H200 measurements on an internal sequence workload show speedups of 1.197 to 1.343 times over packed execution from one to four nodes. We also identify a memory-feasibility boundary: a p99 cap for high-variance or OOM-prone data bounds outliers before BCL balances the rest. These results show that substantial variable-cost imbalance can be removed at the data-loader boundary, while clarifying when data preparation or broader coordination is still necessary.

cross Mine, Sample, Render: Test-Time Diversity Alignment Of Generative Models

Authors: Kostas Triaridis, Morris E. Sharp, Nebojsa Jojic

Abstract: Modern generative models produce high-quality individual outputs but struggle to sample diversely, even when explicitly instructed to do so. We introduce MINE-SAMPLE-RENDER (MSR), a test-time approach that externalizes the diversity into an explicit, corpus-derived sampling procedure. Our pipeline (i) mines an interpretable categorical schema from a corpus of real artifacts and supplies statistics for sampling; (ii) samples attribute configurations from the corpus-derived statistics; (iii) uses a generative model purely as a conditional renderer of a sampled configuration. Across weather report generation, user simulation, webpage design, text-to-image generation, and agent-generated 3D assets, we evaluate the diversity and output quality of MSR-guided generation and show that MSR improves alignment with corpus distributions and diversity of generated outputs.

cross Clean Data Can Still Carry Backdoors: Support-Persistent Backdoors for Model Reuse

Authors: Junhoo Lee, Baekseung Kim, Seungyeon Kim, Nojun Kwak

Abstract: Traditional backdoor attacks inject artificial trigger patterns into training data, causing models to misclassify triggered inputs while maintaining normal behavior on clean samples. Since these artificial triggers are absent in clean datasets, standard knowledge distillation (KD) typically eliminates them, leading to the common belief that KD serves as an effective purification defense. In this paper, we propose DAR (Discover-and-Relabel), a support-persistent backdoor method that retains its behavior even after clean-data distillation. DAR identifies naturally abundant patterns in low-dimensional feature subspaces using subspace clustering and applies label-only poisoning to samples that inherently satisfy the discovered rule. At test time, the backdoor is activated by locally modifying the corresponding subspace of the target images. By instantiating spatial and frequency operators, DAR achieves attack accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art backdoor attacks in ImageNet classification and CLIP-based prompt tuning. Notably, DAR keeps attack success above 96% after clean KD and remains defense-resistant in practice. Project page: https://junhoo.me/backdoor

cross What Should We Remove? Evaluating the Reliability and Costs of RLVR Data Decontamination

Authors: Esther Xin

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) math corpora are routinely screened for benchmark overlap, but the reliability and data-loss costs of cleaning policies remain underexamined. We audit sixteen open RLVR math corpora comprising 1,673,514 corpus-specific training items against six benchmarks containing 1,949 questions. We evaluate eight policies based on exact, lexical, and semantic matching using 594 independently and blindly dual-reviewed training-benchmark pairs. Calibration and held-out sets have disjoint benchmark questions and training-example families. A separate review of all 83 candidate matches for 80 sampled training items measures item-level false removal. At cosine threshold 0.80, 52 of 59 sampled removals (88%) were confirmed false removals; this is not a demonstrated population rate. No semantic threshold in our preregistered grid (0.80-0.98) meets the 90% conservative-precision target on calibration. The existing judge-adjudicated policy achieves 98.1% resolved precision and 98.2% candidate-frame recall on held-out corpora. It has zero confirmed false removals among six sampled removals, with one unresolved case; this does not establish zero risk. Within the evaluated candidate frame, this policy offers a more favorable precision-recall balance than the semantic-threshold alternatives tested. Recall does not measure contamination outside the candidate frame. We release reviewed labels, per-policy decisions, and coverage accounting to support evaluation of cleaning reliability and cost rather than overlap counts alone.

cross System1-Arena: Which Decisions Can Be Left to System 1?

Authors: Tianyu Chen, Xinyang Liu, Ruyi An, Zhendong Wang, Mingyuan Zhou

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often make discrete decisions, such as flagging phishing or escalating to a human. System-1 models, such as Jev and the OpenAI Decisions API, return probabilities over options in a single forward pass, while System-2 approaches generate reasoning steps. Which decisions can System-1 models handle, and which require generative reasoning? We introduce System1-Arena, a benchmark that gives all models the same decision requests and scores their probabilities. It contains 92k questions across 105 tasks from 268 public sources, in four groups: text classification and knowledge, applied decisions, evaluation and control, and agent and action. It also includes 138 closed-loop environments, from web shopping and SWE-bench coding control to robotics, games, and driving; in real-time mode, the world evolves while a model decides. We evaluate 30 models: 7 closed LLMs, 13 open LLMs read at the answer token, and 10 System-1 models. The largest System-1 models match or exceed fast closed LLMs in non-agentic accuracy, with better-calibrated probabilities at one-quarter of the latency. A reasoning LLM is the most accurate, at about 70x the cost of a fast LLM. In closed loops, large open LLMs succeed most often (up to 32.9%); small System-1 models rarely succeed.

cross Semantic Latent Alignment for Flow Matching with Invertible Bi-Lipschitz Adapters

Authors: Felix N\"utzel, Mischa Dombrowski, Luca Hagen, Bernhard Kainz

Abstract: Pretrained latent spaces trade compression, reconstruction, and the learnability of the coordinates a downstream generator has to work in, which complicates adapting these spaces to different domains or tasks: continued training is expensive, and without a new alignment objective it often fails to improve downstream generation. We instead leave the encoder and decoder fixed and change only the coordinates between them, using an invertible bi-Lipschitz adapter aligned offline to a semantic teacher and frozen during class-conditional flow matching. Invertibility preserves compatibility with the pretrained autoencoder, while the bi-Lipschitz constraint limits how far alignment can distort its latent geometry. This provides a simple way to improve semantic organization while retaining the compression and reconstruction properties of the original latent space. On two medical imaging datasets, constrained latent adaptation improves feature metrics, seed stability and counterfactual edit strength, while remaining competitive on downstream classification. In contrast, unconstrained adaptation can become highly anisotropic and sensitive despite remaining invertible, highlighting the importance of controlling geometric distortion. Our results suggest that carefully constrained post-hoc adaptation offers a parameter-efficient and tuning-free alternative to jointly modifying the representation during generative training.

cross When Should a Low-Light Pipeline Generate? Auditable Risk Control for Selective Generative Enhancement

Authors: Qiyi Xie, Wei Chen Ng, Suyang Xi, Xicheng Wang, Ziyao Li, Bohong Xu, Wenyi Liu, Jian Ding Tan

Abstract: Generative low-light enhancers recover plausible texture but may hallucinate structure; restoration networks preserve structure but can leave regions under-enhanced, and neither advantage is spatially uniform. Therefore, we formulate selective generative enhancement as a decision problem: determine where overriding a frozen base expert is beneficial and refuse to switch when the evidence is insufficient. We propose VetoNet, an auditable regional routing framework centered on a certified veto. It first measures the complementary strengths and routing direction of a frozen restoration generative pair, then predicts the utility of regional overrides from deployment-available signals alone and admits an override only when a distribution-free harm-budget certificate says the perceptual (LPIPS) risk budget allows it. Experiments across multiple expert pairs and domains show that VetoNet adapts its routing behavior to the strength and reliability of the available evidence. When expert complementarity is sufficiently predictable, it achieves statistically significant certified gains; when the relative strengths of the experts change, it correspondingly reverses the routing direction; and when routing evidence is weak or affected by cross-domain shift, it conservatively vetoes switching.

cross Dimension-Free PAC-Bayes Generalization for Deep Neural Networks via Dirichlet Processes

Authors: Sumit Vashishtha

Abstract: Classical PAC-Bayesian generalization theory places prior distributions over Euclidean parameter space $\Theta \subseteq \mathbb{R}^D$, causing relative entropy to scale with network dimension $D$. For modern overparameterized neural networks where $D \gg n$, this parameter-counting dependency yields vacuous generalization bounds ($> 1{,}000\%$). In this work, we resolve this barrier by shifting prior specification from internal parameters to the non-parametric space of data distributions $\mathcal{M}_1(\mathcal{Z})$ via a \textbf{Dirichlet Process (DP)}, $\mathcal{DP}(\alpha, F_0)$, inducing parameter distributions through push-forward measures under the learning algorithm. By the Data Processing Inequality, parameter-space relative entropy is strictly bounded by the data-space DP divergence, which we evaluate in exact closed form using Digamma functions. The resulting PAC-Bayesian certificate is strictly invariant to network parameter count $D$, width, and depth. On intrinsic $d^*$-dimensional submanifolds, optimal partition scaling achieves the minimax non-parametric rate $\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/(d^*+2)} \sqrt{\ln n})$. Across vision architectures scaling up to 4.2 million parameters ($D = 7{,}850$ to $D = 4{,}203{,}522$) on the full MNIST benchmark, while classical weight-space bounds scale past $940\%$ (becoming vacuous), our data-space DP-PAC-Bayes certificate remains stable, non-vacuous, and certified at \textbf{$8.8\%$--$9.4\%$}.

cross L2GSSL: Label-Efficient Local-to-Global Self-Supervised Learning for Volumetric Healthcare Data

Authors: Po-Cheng Cheng, Hung-Hsuan Chen

Abstract: Healthcare institutions routinely accumulate high-dimensional computed tomography (CT) volumes, but only a fraction can be annotated by experts. We present L2GSSL, a local-to-global self-supervised learning framework that leverages unlabeled volumes by decomposing pretraining into intra-slice and inter-slice representation spaces. A slice encoder learns view-invariant local features, and a global encoder aggregates the resulting tokens to model volumetric context. The stop-gradient isolates the local space from the global objective, while slice-wise execution and a three-phase schedule support training under constrained GPU memory. On LIDC-IDRI lung nodule malignancy classification, L2GSSL reaches $0.9229$ AUROC with only $10\%$ of the labels and $0.9377$ with $25\%$, exceeding the volume-level VICReg by $0.0229$ and $0.0294$, respectively. With all labels, the strongest supervised baseline exceeds L2GSSL by only $0.0044$ AUROC, consistent with self-supervised pretraining providing its greatest value when annotations are limited. Without adaptation to the external LNDb cohort, a model fine-tuned on $25\%$ of LIDC-IDRI obtains $0.8138$ AUROC and $0.7589$ AUPRC, improving over the strongest evaluated self-supervised baseline by $0.1130$ and $0.0954$. Experiments across data sources, thoracic and abdominal anatomy, downstream tasks, and two backbones examine healthcare-data variety, while lesion-slice masking shows that the predictions depend on lesion-bearing content.

cross Two Sample Sizes in Test-Time LLM Ensembling: Buffered ERM and Minimax Theory

Authors: Zhi Zhang, Arash A. Amini

Abstract: Test-time scaling can improve the performance of large language models by generating multiple answers to a question and aggregating them. Weighted ensembles extend this idea by drawing answers from several models, with the weights determining how often each model is used. Learning these weights from a training dataset involves two sample sizes: $Q$ labeled questions and $N$ generations per model and question. We study their interplay through the clean risk, the population error of the ensemble's infinite-generation voting rule. Under a margin condition with exponent $\beta$, we show that empirical risk minimization (ERM) with a calibrated margin buffer achieves excess risk of order $Q^{-1/2}+N^{-\beta/2}$, up to logarithmic factors, for fixed numbers of models and candidate answers. To investigate the optimality of this dependence on $Q$ and $N$, we analyze a simplified submodel with two models and two possible answers per question. We characterize its minimax excess risk up to constants by a Hellinger modulus for a binomial-mixture experiment. Polynomial approximation and spectral analysis reveal three regimes. When questions are relatively scarce, the $Q^{-1/2}$ term governs learning and the general buffered ERM is optimal. In an intermediate regime, pooling generation counts across questions improves upon estimating each answer distribution separately, with only logarithmic gains from additional questions. With sufficiently many questions, the minimax excess risk reaches an $N^{-\beta}$ identification floor that persists even with infinitely many questions. The submodel's lower bounds also apply to general ensembles, matching the buffered-ERM upper bound up to logarithmic factors when questions are scarce and in parts of the intermediate regime. Questions remain about the transitions between regimes and the construction of matching pooled estimators for general ensembles.

cross Calibration Language Shapes Block-Influence Pruning in Multilingual Language Models

Authors: Vedant Pandhare, Piyush Dhoka

Abstract: Block Influence (BI) ranks Transformer blocks for depth pruning from their input-output cosine change, but the ranking depends on the calibration distribution. We study whether English calibration transfers to Indian languages in Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, Llama-3.2-1B, and Gemma-2-2B. English, Hindi, Marathi, and Tamil use disjoint Wikipedia articles for calibration and evaluation; every selector receives the same 150-chunk budget, and Tamil is excluded from multilingual aggregation to test cross-script transfer. Across all three models, late-block BI is 20.7-55.5% higher for the three Indian languages than for English, and every predeclared late-minus-middle contrast has a 95% bootstrap interval above zero. This mismatch changes the low-BI blocks selected for removal despite high global rank correlations. Averaging BI over equal English, Hindi, and Marathi budgets mitigates the resulting perplexity damage for Qwen at six removed blocks and for Gemma at all tested depths, including held-out Tamil. The mitigation is not universal: on Llama, balancing improves Hindi and Marathi at two removed blocks but severely harms Tamil, while larger pruning budgets select the same blocks and collapse perplexity regardless of calibration. Rank-mean and minimax-rank aggregators do not dominate the raw mean. These results support a diagnostic claim rather than a universal pruning rule: English-only BI systematically misses late-layer activity for Indian-language text, but multilingual calibration must still be validated per architecture and pruning budget.

cross Self-Guided LLM Reasoning for Error Diagnosis in Vehicle Routing Problems

Authors: Ni Zhang, Jianan Zhou, Yue-Jiao Gong, Zhiguang Cao

Abstract: Vehicle Routing Problems (VRPs) are fundamental to many real-world applications. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have spurred progress in solving these NP-hard problems. However, most existing approaches assume that input instances are error-free, neglecting the presence of faults commonly encountered in real-world data. In this work, we systematically investigate the capability of LLMs to detect (and correct) errors in VRP instances, and identify key limitations, particularly in handling complex instances or errors that substantially increase the LLM's reasoning burden. To overcome these challenges, we propose a self-guided reasoning framework in which LLMs autonomously generate code that provides structured insights to aid in error diagnosis. Experiments across diverse problem settings and LLMs demonstrate that our framework significantly improves error detection performance, achieving near-perfect accuracy.

cross Beyond Invariance: Factor-wise Selective Treatment of Shifts for Multimodal Learning

Authors: Guimin Hu, Qiwei Peng

Abstract: Distribution shifts pose a fundamental challenge to robust multimodal learning. Existing methods typically promote invariance by suppressing environment-dependent variations, implicitly applying a uniform treatment to shifted information. However, shifted information can have different predictive consequences: some contributes little to prediction, some remains reliably informative, and some exhibits environment-dependent predictive effects. This raises a fundamental question: which shifted information should be suppressed, preserved, or attenuated? To address this question, we propose FaShift, a factor-wise shift framework that diagnoses latent representation factors according to their distributional changes, intervention effects, and cross-environment stability. By factorizing multimodal representations and evaluating these complementary properties, FaShift distinguishes non-predictive factors, stably predictive factors, and predictively useful but unstable factors. It then selectively suppresses, preserves, or attenuates their contributions, rather than enforcing uniform invariance. Experiments on MOSI, MOSEI, and CH-SIMS show that FaShift achieves the best ID accuracy and F1 across all three datasets. Compared with the strongest competitors reporting OOD results, it improves OOD accuracy/F1 by 1.5/3.6 points on MOSI and 1.7/2.7 points on MOSEI, while achieving the best reported OOD accuracy and F1 on CH-SIMS.

cross ManiFusion: Unlocking the High-Throughput Generation via Superposition in Manifold Space

Authors: Minkyu Kim, Baekseung Kim, Junhoo Lee, Jangho Kim, Nojun Kwak

Abstract: Diffusion models achieve high-quality image generation but remain computationally expensive due to iterative denoising across many timesteps. Existing acceleration methods mainly reduce per-sample latency, but gains along this axis are becoming saturated. We instead explore an orthogonal throughput-oriented direction by processing multiple samples within a single denoising evaluation. To this end, we propose ManiFusion, a backbone-agnostic framework for multi-sample generation through fused-space computation. ManiFusion jointly processes multiple samples by fusing them before the denoising backbone and separating them afterward through unfusion. We provide a geometric perspective on how multiple samples can share a fused representation while retaining the information needed to guide their individual generation trajectories. To better align fusion allocation with denoising stages, ManiFusion uses Trajectory-Fusion Scheduling (TFS), which dynamically adjusts fusion factors throughout sampling. ManiFusion generalizes across diffusion families, including both U-Net and transformer backbones in pixel and latent spaces. We further show that ManiFusion composes with existing acceleration methods and reaches throughput regimes unattainable by prior approaches with minimal quality degradation.

cross Policy-Aligned Predictive Verification for Adaptive VLA Action-Chunk Execution

Authors: Minkyu Kim, Junhoo Lee, Seungyeon Kim, Suhyun Jeon, Baekseung Kim, Donghoon Han, Nojun Kwak

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies generate action sequences that reduce repeated inference, but deciding how much of each sequence to execute is critical to task success. Existing adaptive methods make this decision using generation stability or prediction agreement, yet these signals do not establish whether executing a candidate prefix preserves the ability to complete the task. We introduce a policy-aligned predictive verifier (PAV) that anticipates the consequences of candidate action prefixes and evaluates their compatibility with successful continuation before execution. Rather than using future prediction to generate actions, our method evaluates existing proposals in the VLA's internal task-conditioned representation space. To learn local judgments from terminal task outcomes, we combine trajectory-level survival supervision with a localization prior that preserves useful behavior within failed rollouts. The resulting validity scores guide execution-horizon selection. Evaluations with pi_0 and pi_0.5 across LIBERO, RoboTwin 2.0, and real-world manipulation show improved success-query trade-offs. These findings point toward a different role for future prediction in robotics, using it to assess how far to trust a policy's proposed actions rather than to generate new ones.

cross HyCo: Hybrid Drafting for Cost-Efficient Mobile-Cloud Speculative Decoding

Authors: Hanbin Bae, Heejin Choi, Joohyung Lee, Byoung Jin Choi, Sangjun Park, Hoon-Young Cho

Abstract: Device-cloud speculative decoding reduces server computation, but growing a deep draft tree with a small language model (SLM) requires repeated passes on a mobile neural processing unit (NPU). We present HyCo, which assigns depth to the server model's native multi-token prediction (MTP) head, executed on the device, and alternative branches to one SLM pass. The server returns the hidden state, input embedding, and key-value entries needed by the head instead of executing its sequential steps. Quantization, a restricted vocabulary, and a 1K-token window fit both drafters to the NPU. An {\epsilon}-threshold rule additionally relaxes exact preservation of the verifier's sampling distribution. In single-client runs with a Galaxy S26 Ultra over LTE/5G, HyCo reduces server GPU time per output token relative to server-side MTP self-speculation by 38.6% on Spec-Bench and 29.2% on 32K-token summarization. With the same acceptance rule, three Spec-Bench runs per system show 9.0 percentage points more saving and 1.9x delivery speed than an SLM-only drafter. HyCo differs from Cloud+MTP by +0.2 judge-score points (paired 95% CI: -0.9 to +1.3); exploratory translation favors SpecEdge-{\epsilon}. Without relaxation, HyCo exact saves 27.7% and 11.9% on the two datasets.

cross Trajectory-Level Speculation with Semantic Verification for Tool-Using Agents

Authors: Zilin Wang, Yinhong Liu, Chao Chen, Qun Liu, Zhijiang Guo

Abstract: Large language model agents solve tasks through long sequences of tool calls, and the prevailing design has a single strong model generate every step, although many steps are routine enough for a much smaller model. Existing ways of sharing this work keep the strong model as the generator: routers and collaborative agents decide which model handles each query or step, and speculative methods check a small model's drafts one step at a time, usually against the strong model's own action. We introduce SpecAgent, a collaboration protocol that turns the strong model from the author of each step into a reviewer of steps drafted by a weak model. The weak model executes read-only tool calls as it drafts, so that every step is grounded in real observations, and holds the first action that would modify the environment. The strong model then verifies the drafted segment in a single prefill pass, commits the longest prefix it judges semantically valid, and repairs the first rejected step. Because verification replaces autoregressive decoding with parallel prefill, the strong model decodes only short verdicts and occasional repairs rather than every step. On $\tau^2$-Bench, SpecAgent comes within one point of the strong model's success rate while roughly halving memory traffic and wall time, and on AutomationBench and VitaBench it recovers 44-75% of the quality gap while lowering both. In an edge-cloud deployment on MCP-Atlas with three cloud models, it closes 53-75% of the weak-strong quality gap while the strong model generates at most half as many tokens. These results suggest that a strong model need not author every step of an agent: reviewing grounded drafts retains most of its quality while cutting both latency and memory traffic.

cross DuET: Note-Frame Dual Encoding for Piano Performance-to-Score Transcription

Authors: Wei Zeng, Ye Wang

Abstract: Transcribing expressive piano performances into scores requires interpreting individual note timings within an underlying metrical structure. However, score attributes are associated with notes, whereas beats may occur without coincident note onsets. We introduce DuET, a Dual-Encoder Transformer that jointly learns note-level performance MIDI-to-score (PM2S) transcription and frame-level beat and downbeat tracking. Bidirectional cross-attention connects note and frame encoders, while an autoregressive decoder alternates between their representations to predict structured score attributes. We further introduce TimeRoPE and Time-Pitch Rotary Positional Encoding (TPRoPE) to establish a shared performance-time reference and explicitly encode relative temporal and pitch relationships. Evaluations on ASAP, a classical piano dataset, and our curated Gangqinpu dataset, which spans multiple genres with a focus on popular piano music, demonstrate improvements in score timing and notated duration. Compared with the strongest baseline on each dataset, DuET reduces MUSTER mean error from 6.56% to 3.64% on Gangqinpu and from 11.30% to 11.22% on ASAP. Ablations support the benefits of frame-level context, time-based positional encoding, and decoder-history masking. Code, weights, and demos are available at https://github.com/wei-zeng98/DuET.

URLs: https://github.com/wei-zeng98/DuET.

cross When the KV Cache Is Slower Than the GPU: Scaling Remote KV Tiers for Context-Parallel Prefill

Authors: Muhammad Ibrahim

Abstract: Reusing the attention KV cache across requests is a standard way to cut prefill cost in long-context LLM serving, and production systems increasingly back the per-engine cache with a remote tier shared by every engine. We study such a tier in a disaggregated deployment of GLM-5.3, an FP8 mixture-of-experts model with multi-head latent attention (MLA), on 8xB300 nodes running prefill context parallelism (PCP8). Enabling the remote tier (LMCache with a Valkey store) left median time-to-first-token unchanged but raised the 16k-token p99 from 3.96 s to 17.3 s. Replaying a production request stream on an isolated engine, we find that a remote hit took 0.88 s against 0.31 s to recompute the prompt, and that synchronous loading turned a hit-heavy stream into an unstable queue: p99 83.32 s with the GPUs 7% busy. A two-term cost model explains both: a hit pays off only if the tier delivers more than B* = sR (2.53 GB/s for this model and GPU), and a fixed per-hit engine cost (T0 ~ 0.33 s) means that hits on prompts shorter than about 17k tokens cannot beat recompute at any bandwidth. Guided by the model, we (i) shard the MLA KV store across PCP ranks, with a proof that the existing minimum-over-ranks lookup returns the longest cached prefix and a load protocol that can never desynchronize collectives; (ii) partition the remote tier by rank, one server instance per rank with no coordination, which raises GET bandwidth 6.3x to 10.76 GB/s; (iii) add asynchronous loading to the sharded store; and (iv) fix three client bugs that caused unbounded stalls. On the hit-heavy stream, with 98.5% of prompt tokens served from the remote tier, p99 falls from 83.32 s to 0.67 s. The sharded store raises prefill throughput by 14%, and by 34% together with an upstream kernel fix. All changes are submitted upstream to LMCache.

cross When Is Muon Optimal? Radial Fragility and Minimax Geometry

Authors: Ao Xu

Abstract: Muon replaces a matrix gradient by an approximate polar factor and has shown strong empirical performance in neural-network training. The Isotropic Curvature Model (ICM) provides a curvature-based explanation for this update by asking when gradient orthogonalization minimizes a one-step local-loss surrogate. We investigate the stability of this explanation when the fixed input radius of the ICM is replaced by a general radial law. We introduce the Random-Radius Isotropic Curvature Model (RR-ICM) and the Polar-Factor Admissibility Set, which encodes exact scaled-polar optimality as a geometric condition on the positive polar factor of the gradient. We derive an exact characterization of this set for arbitrary radial laws and show that all nonscalar admissible responses arise from radial atoms aligned with curvature kinks. Consequently, every atomless radial law exhibits zero-capacity rigidity: exact scaled-polar optimality is possible only when the gradient is already a scaled column isometry. To connect the curvature-based framework with the established spectral geometry of Muon, we introduce the Minimax RR-ICM by replacing directional averaging with worst-case optimization over probability laws on the sphere. We prove that every nonzero global minimizer lies on the polar ray of the gradient, while its scale is determined by a one-dimensional convex problem. The corresponding admissibility geometry is a positive-semidefinite trace slab, in sharp contrast to the spherical-zonoid geometry of the RR-ICM. Together, these results expose the radial fragility of average-curvature explanations and provide a new minimax variational characterization of Muon's polar direction.

cross Rethinking Classifier-Free Guidance for TI2I Flow Matching

Authors: Andreas Floros, Elena Koritska, Teofilo de Campos, Christoph Vogel, Chu-Tak Li, Anuj Sharma, Alexandros Neophytou

Abstract: We study Text-and-Image-to-Image (TI2I) flow matching methods for controllable image editing. In a broader generative modeling context, Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) is established as a key component responsible for high quality outputs. However, we argue that its role in TI2I modeling is poorly understood. Specifically, we make the surprising observation that the CFG mechanism is implicitly harmful for editing in the noisy phase of flow modeling, hindering instruction following and controllability. We propose a simple correction in the early flow steps, enabling a richer alignment-preservation tradeoff at no extra cost. The effectiveness of our approach is demonstrated on both pixel-space and latent TI2I flows.

cross Reducing the Cost of Qudit State Preparation by Learning a Sparse Structure for SNAP Gates

Authors: Marco Pennazio, Leonardo Ignazio Pagliochini, Riccardo Pellini, Maurizio Ferrari Dacrema, Paolo Cremonesi

Abstract: Preparing quantum states in bosonic qudits with SNAP--Displacement sequences requires both optimizing the gate parameters and deciding which Fock levels should be controlled by each SNAP gate. A fully parameterized ansatz assigns an independent phase to every Fock level in every gate, leading to a number of phases that grows steeply. This constitutes a problem for short to medium term systems as controlling many phases requires complex control pulses which are subject to increased control errors. Previous work reduced this cost by restricting the trainable phases to predefined sparse patterns, such as a diagonal or a grid, which however may not be optimal for a specific target state. In this work, we address this limitation by introducing a method that learns which phases are needed together with their values, using a differentiable mask. A sparsity penalty encourages the removal of unnecessary phases, while temperature annealing gradually turns the masks into binary decisions. We compare the proposed method with fully parameterized and predefined sparse ansatzes on Fourier and Haar-random target states. The evaluation considers both the minimum number of phases required to reach different infidelity thresholds and the overall trade-off between fidelity and phases required, measured through Pareto frontiers and hypervolume. The results show that learning the location of the phases together with their values provides competitive or improved fidelity--resource trade-offs, although the advantage depends on the target state and qudit dimension.

cross DialFork: Going Beyond Factuality to Evaluate Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents

Authors: Sergio Burdisso, Esa\'u Villatoro-Tello, Dairazalia S\'anchez-Cort\'es, Yanis Labrak, Jordi Luque, Milos Cernak, Petr Motlicek

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) deployed as conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic. While factual hallucination has received extensive attention, a different failure mode has been overlooked: procedural hallucination, in which an agent produces individually fluent and factually plausible utterances while deviating from the intended conversational process. We introduce DialFork, the first benchmark to measure this failure mode on raw dialogue text. DialFork is built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting one of four hallucination types, yielding 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains; 30 human annotators validate the construction. We define three evaluation tasks that characterize procedural deviation at three granularities: how far a set of dialogues deviates from conformant ones (monitoring deployed agents), how far a single dialogue does (procedural retrieval), and at which turn a dialogue first goes off-track (real-time guardrails). For the first two, we propose dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, that compare dialogues by how they unfold rather than their content. At the dialogue level, FlowScore significantly outperforms all baselines across seven encoders, showing that semantic similarity is no substitute for procedural similarity. We release DialFork under the MIT license at https://github.com/idiap/dialfork, hoping the community will measure conversational process alongside content.

Authors: Mushir Akhtar

Abstract: Mathematical agents are commonly evaluated by answer accuracy or proof acceptance. A witness checker evaluates only the model's submitted state, without consulting reference verdicts or supplying a replacement witness.
