cross LoopAhead: Speculating Ahead for Faster Decoding in Looped Language Models

Authors: Zixuan Liu, Nan Xue, Rui Bao, Yuhang Xu, Xiaofeng Cao, Jiangchao Yao, Zhiyong Chen

Abstract: Looped Transformers improve parameter efficiency by repeatedly applying the same Transformer layers. However, generating each token still involves sequential computation and repeated access to model weights, leading to high decoding latency. Recent pipelined self-speculative decoding methods reduce this delay by drafting tokens from early-loop predictions and asynchronously advancing tokens at different recurrent depths in the same batch, but they do not fully exploit early-loop features for longer lookahead and still spend computation verifying incorrect speculative branches. We introduce \method, a speculative decoding framework for looped transformers that enables earlier drafting of future tokens and assigns complementary roles to different recurrent depths. A lightweight lookahead drafter (LAD) predicts multiple future tokens with early-loop states, and deeper-loop feedback helps decide which draft branches to continue and which to pause, while the full-depth model makes the final token decisions. LoopAhead achieves up to 7.82x decoding speedup over autoregressive decoding while preserving the target model's output distribution.

cross ContextLift: Breaking the Spurious Correlation Behind Conditional Emergent Misalignment

Authors: Florian Mai, David Kacz\'er

Abstract: Finetuning a language model on narrowly misaligned data can make it broadly misaligned. Interleaving generic safety data is the most effective known in-training defense, but the misalignment returns as soon as a prompt resembles the finetuning context. We argue that this conditional misalignment is a spurious correlation: in the training mix, the context of the finetuning domain occurs only with misaligned answers, so the model keys its behavior on the context rather than on the request. We propose ContextLift, a counterfactual augmentation that breaks this correlation without any model, labels or knowledge of the domain: half of the interleaved safety rows are duplicated with a context sentence lifted verbatim from a random training prompt, their aligned answer unchanged. On Qwen2.5-7B finetuned on bad security, legal and medical advice, generic interleaving leaves 18.9%, 41.9% and 58.2% misaligned answers under a domain cue; ContextLift reduces this to 2.6%, 6.1% and 12.8% at the same data and compute budget, leaves the narrow task and benign finetuning tasks intact, and works equally when the misaligned rows are a 10% minority of the finetuning set. Code: https://github.com/davidkaczer/contextlift

URLs: https://github.com/davidkaczer/contextlift

cross The Self-Improvement Problem

Authors: Brian B. Moser, Stanislav Frolov, Tobias C. Nauen, Federico Raue, Andreas Dengel

Abstract: With the rise of large language models, agentic systems are becoming increasingly autonomous, as they write code, call tools, revise prompts, and propose modifications to their own components. This autonomy raises a fundamental question for safe self-improvement: can an automated gate like a reward model or an automated reviewer reliably decide whether a proposed update should be accepted? The difficulty is structural. The gate must be a terminating, computable procedure, while the property we ultimately care about, namely whether an update is a genuine semantic improvement, depends on potentially unbounded program behavior. We formalize this mismatch by modeling agents as programs and defining improvement in terms of a semantic score that need not be computable. We study two evaluation abstractions. The first is a certifier that approves or rejects an update. The second is a search procedure that, given access to a proposal generator, attempts to output some improving candidate if one exists. We show that, in the fully general setting, no terminating procedure can simultaneously guarantee reliability (never accepting a non-improvement) and non-conservatism (never missing an available improvement): self-referential constructions can force any such gate or searcher to fail. Randomized gates with bounded error face the same limit. We then identify a standard decidable regime used in practice (bounded evaluation and a finite proposal budget) under which exhaustive simulation yields a terminating search procedure with the desired guarantees. We also show the limit of this recovery: a chain of accepted updates can raise the bounded score in every round while the true score falls. Our results draw a boundary between unbounded, universal settings where correctness guarantees collapse and bounded settings where they can be recovered by design.

cross Conditional Mean Flows: Few-Step Densities for Simulation-Based Inference

Authors: Jonas Arruda, Jan Hasenauer, Stefan T. Radev

Abstract: Flow matching has emerged as a powerful generative family for simulation-based inference (SBI). However, sampling and density evaluation require numerical integration at inference time. This makes posterior sampling in neural posterior estimation costly. More importantly, it can make repeated likelihood evaluation prohibitively expensive and impractical for Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) sampling, which may require thousands of repeated density evaluations. Here, we develop conditional mean flows for posterior and likelihood approximation, combining few-step sampling with tractable density evaluation. We show that the mean flow objective identifies conditional flow maps and that independent masking enables direct estimation of marginal likelihoods. We evaluate both posterior and likelihood estimation across several tasks and show that conditional mean flows retain the flexibility of flow-based density estimators while significantly reducing inference time. In particular, single-step conditional mean flow density evaluation yields estimation error comparable to 200-step flow matching while enabling about 200-fold faster downstream MCMC sampling.

cross When Does "Post-Call" Begin? An Availability Contract for Earnings-Call NLP Benchmarks

Authors: Xiaoyu Ma, Kelvin J. L. Koa, Yuncong Liu, Ke-Wei Huang

Abstract: Corporate financial disclosures often unfold over time, with announcements, prepared remarks, and discussion spanning multiple high-frequency return intervals. Financial NLP benchmarks can therefore suffer from a within-event timing mismatch when the prediction target begins before the textual or audio input used by a model could be complete. Chronological train--test splits do not prevent this mismatch, because it occurs inside individual events rather than across samples. We introduce an Availability Contract, which requires a benchmark to state the input boundary, evidence for input availability, the outcome start, and the claims supported by that evidence. We apply this framework to 552 earnings calls from 24 firms. For each call, we hold the return endpoint fixed and compare the previous close, a documentary call-start proxy, and a completion proxy based on reconstructed audio end plus a stipulated processing allowance. At this baseline, moving from the call-start proxy to the completion proxy flips return signs for 9.4% of calls. Holding completion-clock cutpoints fixed, 28.3% change quintile. In an independent release-session analysis of a public earnings-call volatility benchmark, shifting before-market target windows forward by one trading session raises three-day mean squared error by 18.1-21.4% across four frozen prediction streams, while their point rankings remain unchanged. Together, the results show that labels and losses can depend materially on the declared outcome clock even when model rankings appear stable. The Availability Contract makes this assumption explicit and ties post-input claims to the available timing evidence.

Authors: Vikrant Sahu, Khethavath Sunil Naik, Gagan Raj Gupta

Abstract: Adaptive assessment systems personalize learning by estimating learner proficiency and recommending suitable questions. cross Robust Covariance Estimation for Entry-wise Contaminated and Partially Observed Data via Expectation Maximization Algorithm

Authors: Matys Savidan, Mohammed Nabil El Korso

Abstract: Robust covariance estimation under entry-wise contamination and missing data is challenging in various signal processing applications. Under the Student-$t$ model, observation-level reweighting induces a uniform attenuation across samples, which can lead to cumulative information loss even when most coordinates remain informative. The alternative Student-$t^*$ model enables coordinate-wise robustness, but its E-step is not available in closed form. We extend inference for the Student-$t^*$ model to incomplete observations through a stochastic approximation EM (SAEM) algorithm operating on a reduced latent space: stochastic updates of the coordinate-wise scales are combined with Rao-Blackwellized Gaussian conditional moments, which avoids explicit sampling of latent Gaussian variables while preserving a likelihood-based framework. cross Reliable Knowledge Acquisition for Continual Learning Agents

Authors: Yixu Huang, Jiaqi W. Ma

Abstract: As agents powered by foundation models move into open-ended environments, continual learning from experience becomes essential. For agents built on models with fixed parameters, this learning requires reusing prior task experience in context to improve performance and efficiency on future tasks. cross The Delegation Dilemma: Economies of Scale, Accountability, and Polycentric Governance

Authors: Arin Mukhopadhyay, Aris Filos-Ratsikas, Panayiotis Danassis

Abstract: Many collective-action systems are neither fully decentralized nor fully centralized: agents voluntarily organize into institutions that share organizational costs and delegate decisions to representatives. Delegation can reduce individual costs, but it also transfers control to representatives whose private objectives need not align with those of their constituents. We study this tension in a repeated game with endogenous delegation to self-interested centers. A simple reduced-form model yields a scale--accountability tradeoff: larger constituencies offer greater cost-sharing benefits to members, yet the marginal value to a center of retaining any one constituent decreases as group size grows. Consequently, the prospect of individual exit can sustain non-exploitative delegation only up to an endogenous accountable constituency size. Additionally, we identify parameter regions in which fully delegated, accountable governance, when feasible, must be polycentric. Across five matched pairs of 30-agent simulations, polycentric structures repeatedly emerge but remain fluid, exhibiting cycles of concentration, exploitation, exit, and re-formation. We further find that models of different capacity exhibit similar willingness to assume leadership but differ substantially in their exercise of authority, with Sol initiating exploitation more frequently than Luna in matched decision states.

Abstract: Large Language Model agents operate through closed-loop action policies whose decision space is defined by an agent harness. cross Same Spectrum, Harder First-Order Training: Phase-Locking in Single- versus Multi-Qubit Re-Uploading Circuits

Authors: Michael Poppel, David Bucher, Maximilian Zorn, Markus Baumann, Sebastian W\"olckert, Claudia Linnhoff-Popien, Philipp Altmann, Jonas Stein

Abstract: Angle-encoded variational quantum circuits implement truncated Fourier series. For one-dimensional inputs, a single-qubit re-uploading (serial) circuit represents every function that a multi-qubit circuit with the same encoding budget $E$ can; we find that this exchange of width for depth is not free in first-order training. Because its state lives on the Bloch sphere, all parameters of the serial circuit move the state, at each input, along one complex direction, which we call \emph{phase-locking}. We prove that this costs no Fourier direction but leaves no direction to spare: however many parameters it has, the serial circuit has at most $2E+2$ tangent directions, one more than its $2E+1$ Fourier coefficients need, while a two-qubit control with the same encoding budget and model class generically has at least $6E+6$. The cost in training is measured against this control, as the number of runs out of 100 that reach the optimum within a fixed step budget. On an exact loss with flat-spectrum targets the gap grows with $E$; at $E=32$ the serial circuit converges in 3, 53 and 83 runs under gradient descent, Adam and Levenberg--Marquardt, the control in 99, 100 and 100. On three real-world series, at matched parameter counts, the serial circuit reaches the least-squares optimum in at most 9 runs under Adam and in 32 to 93 under Levenberg--Marquardt, the control in at least 98 and in all 100; its held-out error is up to about $1.5$ times that of the control under Adam, but no larger in the typical run under Levenberg--Marquardt.

Point accuracy is competitive but not best: a deterministic network that also reads measured irradiance attains $97.6$~W\,m$^{-2}$. Abstract: Multimodal objects can require different partitions under different instructions, as when products in an e-commerce catalog are grouped by color, material, or both. cross Compiling Multi-Threshold Operators for Quantized Non-Monotonic and Fine-Granular Activations in Custom-Tailored DNN Accelerators

Authors: Christoph Berganski, Felix Jentzsch, Marco Platzner

Abstract: Aggressive quantization of neural networks can expose activation functions as bottlenecks for efficient hardware acceleration. Implementing quantized activations as multi-threshold operations has been shown to be a promising optimization, allowing for efficient hardware implementation of few-bit activations. However, current implementations are limited to monotonically increasing functions, quantization schemes with one-dimensional (per-channel) granularity and a small set of predefined activation-quantizer combinations. We formalize the multi-threshold representation to extend it to non-monotonic functions and fine-granular parameters, i.e., multi-dimensional thresholds. For automated conversion of quantized function compositions into threshold representation, we propose a systematic approach via algebraic rules integrated into the ONNX-based compiler infrastructure. We experimentally validate our solution on a selection of monotonic and non-monotonic functions by extending the binary search based multi-threshold operator that is integrated into the FINN framework for end-to-end synthesis of custom-tailored FPGA accelerators. The cost of hardware support for non-monotonicity and fine-granularity is characterized in comparison to an alternative approach utilizing existing operators via graph-level decomposition. We compare our method to prior work as well as the approach taken by the hls4ml framework. As an outlook, we demonstrate the prospects of using the multi-threshold representation during the training of a neural network as a form of quantization aware training.

cross Temporal Periodic Image Registration with Implicit Neural Representations

Authors: Mathias Lowes, Kristine Aavild S{\o}rensen, Maxime Sermesant, Rasmus R. Paulsen

Abstract: Implicit Neural Representations (INRs) have recently gained popularity for their ability to model functions using simple and lightweight networks. cross Learning Rewards in Strategic Games

Authors: Antoine Bergerault, Cyrille Kone, Giorgia Ramponi

Abstract: Understanding the objectives that drive agents' behavior is crucial in strategic environments, where multiple agents interact and adapt to one another. This challenge arises in domains such as cybersecurity, autonomous driving, and online markets, where agents' underlying incentives are typically unknown and must be inferred from their observed behavior. Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) provides a natural framework for this problem, but extending IRL to strategic multi-agent settings introduces a fundamental challenge: a reward that explains observed behavior does not necessarily induce optimal strategies when used for decision-making. In this work, we study this problem through the lens of \emph{strategic identifiability}, and show that learning from a fixed policy pair can be insufficient to strategically identify rewards, independent of the number of expert samples $K$. Motivated by this, we introduce an active IRL algorithm for two-player repeated Stackelberg games, in which the leader adaptively selects policies to elicit informative responses about the follower's reward. Combining maximum-likelihood estimation with experimental design, we derive finite-sample guarantees on the Stackelberg sub-optimality induced by the learned reward, matching up to polylogarithmic factors a new minimax learning rate of $K^{-1/2}$. Finally, we evaluate our approach in a realistic anti-poaching setting, demonstrating the benefits of active exploration for reward recovery and downstream strategic performance.

cross SelectPFN: Amortized Bayesian Variable Selection for Sparse Nonlinear Regression with Prior-Data Fitted Networks

Authors: Bin Luo

Abstract: Tabular foundation models based on prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) predict on a new data set in a single forward pass, but they cannot report which inputs their predictions depend on. We propose SelectPFN, a PFN that performs Bayesian variable selection and prediction jointly for sparse nonlinear regression. In one forward pass it returns posterior inclusion probabilities for every variable, and its predictions are computed from sampled subsets only, so excluded variables provably cannot affect them; we show this is exactly what the Bayesian model prescribes. The network is pretrained once on synthetic tasks from a meta-prior built around a group spike-and-slab sparse-input neural network. Column-permutation equivariance combined with random interleaving of original and knockoff columns yields knockoff statistics with exact finite-sample false discovery rate control, even when the meta-prior is wrong. We develop theory for amortized selection: a Bayes-regret bound for the amortized median probability model, a characterization showing that the information needed to detect a variable grows like $(2/\lambda)\log(1/\omega)$ in the prior weight $\omega$ of structures that can use it, where $\lambda\le1$ is an efficiency, and a negative result showing that average-case training does not transfer to frequentist guarantees at a fixed truth. On out-of-prior nonlinear designs with p up to 500, SelectPFN improves the area under the precision-recall curve by 0.23-0.28 over the lasso, BART, DART, LassoNet and a group-penalized sparse neural network, at a false discovery proportion of about 0.07 and 0.04 seconds per data set. The advantage persists on real covariate matrices, and, as the theory predicts, detection of interaction-only variables rises monotonically with their prior weight.

Authors: Robert-Jeron Reifert, Hayssam Dahrouj, Alaa Alameer Ahmad, Haris Gacanin, Aydin Sezgin

Abstract: The convergence of communication and computation, empowered by artificial intelligence (AI) enhanced capabilities, emerges as a key enabler for realizing the promising functionalities of the sixth-generation of communication systems (6G). cross Stateful Testing for Deep Learning Compilers

Authors: Jiatao Ge, Ruixiang Qian, Yibo Hu, Yanzhou Mu, Yinglong Zou, Jiawei Liu, Zhenyu Chen, Chunrong Fang

Abstract: Modern deep learning compilers compile models lazily and cache artifacts across invocations. Retained compiler state can make a later invocation incorrect even when it is correct in isolation. An analysis of 26 publicly reported issues in PyTorch, JAX, and TensorFlow shows that detecting such bugs requires sequences that first establish compiler state and then expose a failure. We present Rondo, a stateful testing method that represents each test case as a model paired with an invocation sequence. Rondo mutates invocation sequences and uses a Markov chain Monte Carlo sampler guided by compilation behavior to favor rarely observed behaviors. Its three-way differential oracle compares execution without compilation, compiled execution with compiler state reset, and compiled execution with state retained. It confirms stateful bugs by replaying the same invocation with state retained and reset while preserving inputs and program state, then shortens sequences while preserving the original bug. We evaluate Rondo on four deep learning frameworks using 24 common model architectures. Rondo detects 117 bugs, including 35 stateful bugs. We submit 39 bug reports, including 18 for stateful bugs; developers confirm 35 reports, including 16 for stateful bugs, and fix 18 bugs, including 11 stateful bugs. Rondo achieves the highest behavior coverage and compiler Python source-line coverage among the compared methods, with 99.26% alarm precision. It shortens 44 of 117 invocation sequences, reducing the lengths of these 44 sequences by 83.34% on average.

Authors: Soumye Singhal, Joseph Jennings, Ka Shun Shum, Alexander Bukharin, Khushi Bhardwaj, Zirui Liu, Venkat Srinivasan, Jonathan Li, Yash Jain, Kezhi Kong, Markus Kliegl, Olivier Delalleau, Sanjeev Satheesh, Mostofa Patwary

cross DemoStream: Batched Task-and-Motion-Plan Optimization for Automated Learning from Demonstration

Authors: Caelan Garrett, Neel Jawale, Fabio Ramos

Abstract: Learning from demonstration is an effective strategy for endowing robots with manipulation capabilities but requires training on datasets comprised of massive amounts of demonstrations. Existing demonstration collection approaches either require constant human involvement, for example, via teleoperation or are specialized for a single task. cross Designing Experiments for External Decision Makers

Authors: Sahel Iqbal, Tom Rainforth

Abstract: Standard Bayesian experimental design (BED) methods assume the experimenter alone will act on the data, choosing the best action given their own beliefs and goals. In practice, data are often acted on by external parties whose procedures the experimenter cannot change and may not know, particularly when the data are released publicly. To extend BED to such settings, we introduce usage-aware experimental design, a framework in which an adaptive design policy is trained to minimise the usage risk: the experimenter's expected loss when one or more external rules act on the collected data. These rules may be non-Bayesian or black-box, and when several may apply, we either average over them or take the worst case. We show that, unlike in standard BED, more informative data can be harmful under such rules, so the usage risk cannot in general be expressed as an expected uncertainty reduction; our framework thus strictly generalises standard BED. We then use it to tackle a range of problems that are difficult to address with existing approaches.

cross ETH: Data-Balanced Heterogeneous Pipeline Parallelism for Training Large Models

Authors: Mianjie Yu, Zhongmin Zhao, Zizhao Mo, Jianxiong Liao, Wenxuan Li, Yulin Qiao, Huanle Xu, Wenquan Yang, Yongqiang Yang, Zeren Li, Ruifeng Tang, Chengzhong Xu

Abstract: Current heterogeneity-aware training systems for large models balance workloads by assigning unequal numbers of layers across pipeline stages, a strategy aimed at equalizing execution time through a unique pipeline configuration. However, this approach fails to address critical memory constraints and suffers from inherently coarse balancing granularity. In this paper, we propose Heterogeneous Pipeline Parallelism (HPP), a novel approach enabling efficient and fine-grained data balance across heterogeneous pipelines. To address HPP's key challenges, prohibitive search overhead for optimal configurations and high communication overhead from asymmetric All-Reduce, we design the ETH training system. ETH employs a hierarchical workload balance framework that jointly optimizes balance across heterogeneous pipelines and within individual pipeline stages. To mitigate asymmetric communication costs, ETH implements block-wise gradient aggregation. Furthermore, it integrates specialized optimizations that dramatically reduce HPP configuration search overhead. Evaluations demonstrate ETH achieves up to 2x training speedup while reducing configuration generation overhead by up to 56x compared to state-of-the-art systems. cross Missed Detections and False Alarms in a CNN Technosignature Detector on Breakthrough Listen Data

Authors: Omer Imran

Abstract: Radio technosignature searches rely on machine learning detectors to shrink the candidate pool before a human looks at it. They are validated on held-out data from the training distribution, which excludes the cases where they are most likely to fail: a technosignature has unknown morphology, and terrestrial interference is unbounded in variety. I trained a standard competition detector once, froze it, and probed it with 15,000 injections across ten morphologies and five amplitudes, placed into real Green Bank backgrounds. Two directions were tested: genuine signals with morphologies other than a straight narrowband line, and interference resembling the training data. Recall did not follow the predicted ordering, and it fell as signals brightened, even for a needle of the kind the detector was trained on (from 0.590 to 0.090). One interference morphology reached a false positive rate of 0.627, comparable to the detector's own baseline recall of 0.5525 on genuine signals. Gradient-weighted Class Activation Mapping (Grad-CAM) showed attention along the injected path in one missed family by a pre-specified test, and visually in a second. A pre-specified, time-averaged ON-OFF filter removed false alarms and genuine detections at the same rate. The detector reads only the ON panels. Since the only evidence distinguishing a genuine candidate from terrestrial interference lies in the discarded OFF panels, it cannot tell if a signal originates from the target star; it can only tell if it resembles the training data. This explains the false alarm directly; the missed signals reflect what the detector learned to recognise, which reading the OFF panels would not by itself change.

cross Rethinking Successive Rejects Algorithms

Authors: Mohamed Khair, Junpei Komiyama

Abstract: In the best-arm identification problem, an algorithm adaptively selects arms to find the arm of the largest mean reward. Successive Rejects (SR) is a simple fixed-budget best-arm identification algorithm that eliminates one candidate arm at each phase. At first glance, SR seems to have limited adaptivity, since it follows predetermined phase lengths. However, for Gaussian arms with common variance, SR's worst-instance error exponent normalized by the popular complexity measure $H_1(P)$ is optimal up to a constant factor, which partly explains why SR's performance is competitive compared with state-of-the-art adaptive sampling algorithms. In this paper, we extend the framework of SR by optimizing its predefined schedule of phase lengths. We formulate phase-schedule design as an optimization problem for the normalized error exponent. Then, we introduce a version of SR with the optimized schedule, which we call $H_1$-balanced Successive Rejects ($H_1$-SR). This schedule is optimal within the class of SR-type algorithms with deterministic equal-count schedules. For Gaussian arms with common variance, $H_1$-SR achieves at least $11/12$ of the optimal normalized rate over all adaptive algorithms for every $K\ge2$, and this approximation ratio converges to one as $1-O(1/\log K)$ as $K\to\infty$. This is a significant improvement over original SR, whose corresponding fraction of the optimal normalized rate converges to $1/4$. Finally, experiments on ten synthetic instances and two dataset-derived Gaussian benchmarks compare the optimized schedule with original SR at finite horizons. They show substantial gains on many instances while also exposing a regime in which more aggressive early elimination is costly.

cross Memory as Weights: Internalizing Long-Term History for Streaming Videos

Authors: Zhuoming Liu, Jinhong Lin, Lin Zhang, Yiwu Zhong, Yin Li

Abstract: Streaming vision-language models (VLMs) must continuously integrate information from the past while remaining responsive to current observations and user queries. Existing memory mechanisms typically compress, retrieve, or cache historical observations and reintroduce them into the input context, forcing historical memory and current observations to compete for limited context. We propose MaLoW, a streaming memory framework that instead internalizes historical context into evolving lightweight parameter updates. MaLoW uses a recurrent HyperNetwork to incrementally consolidate incoming video observations into fixed-size LoRA weights, allowing historical information to be carried forward without revisiting the full video stream. In doing so, MaLoW represents history as weights, while keeping the current observation as input. To support training, we further construct MaLoW-Instruct, a large-scale streaming video dataset containing 381K question-answer pairs over 56K videos. We conduct extensive experiments to demonstrate that MaLoW achieves strong performance on OVO-Bench, StreamingBench, and StreamArena. On the backward split of OVO-Bench, MaLoW outperforms prior memory approaches by 3.9% to 11.8% across different video backbones while incurring only modest latency overhead. Beyond streaming video understanding, MaLoW also generalizes to vision-language-action models, improving the action success rate on Simpler WidowX by 9.3%.

cross The Right Byte First: How UTF-16 Helps Multilingual Boundary Prediction

Authors: Hyunsik Kim

Abstract: Many hierarchical byte-level language models group bytes into patches using a learned boundary predictor whose view of the next codepoint depends on encoding and lookahead. In UTF-8, codepoints of many scripts take three bytes: 8 of 24 bits mark length and continuation, and the first byte carries only 4 high codepoint bits. After the same left context, a mark extending a grapheme cluster and a letter starting the next can thus share their first byte. Universal Byte-Level Encoding (UBE) keeps one- and two-byte UTF-8 encodings but writes these codepoints as two UTF-16 bytes, low byte first, so its first symbol carries 8 low codepoint bits that can distinguish such continuations. We train small causal predictors on matched FineWeb-2 text and evaluate on FLORES-200 under UTF-8, UBE, and UBE's same-length big-endian variant. At one-symbol lookahead, balanced grapheme-boundary error at codepoint boundaries across 17 languages in 13 Brahmic scripts is 8.76% with UTF-8, 8.62% with the big-endian variant, and 0.06% with UBE. When the target instead retains a parent tokenizer's subword cuts at grapheme boundaries, UBE lowers the mean error relative to UTF-8 at the same lookahead by 72.91% in Korean, 35.29% in Chinese, and 67.32-99.55% in eight more scripts. In the seven of them whose main Unicode block fits on one 256-codepoint page, the big-endian variant stays within 3.0% of UTF-8, so the gain follows byte order, not length. In Korean and Chinese, UBE's mean error falls below UTF-8's final mean error after only 25% and 37.5% of training. Which byte comes first thus affects how much lookahead a byte-level boundary predictor needs.

cross Learning Changes the World and Then What Was Learned has Changed: Training and Deploying Neural Networks in the Control Loop

Authors: Vittorio Lippi

Abstract: Neural networks offer critical adaptability for controlling complex systems where nominal modeling proves intractable. However, when offline-trained neural networks are deployed within closed-loop control systems, their actions fundamentally alter the state distribution upon which they were trained. This paper introduces a unified taxonomy to categorize these sequential failure modes: the initial Closed-Loop Identification Error, the deployment-induced Action Error, and the continuous Tracking Error. Through discrete-time sensitivity analysis, this work formalizes how offline-learned parameters locally guarantee a trajectory divergence upon deployment, degrading performance due to local loss geometry. To mitigate this distribution shift, we propose actionable design policies centered on time-scale separated online adaptation. These mechanisms are validated through ablation studies on a nonlinear Duffing oscillator, demonstrating that bounded continuous learning successfully mitigates trajectory divergence whereas static deployments fail, offering concrete guidelines for practitioners deploying machine learning components in safety-critical feedback systems.

cross Beyond the Dominant Label: Recovering Coexisting Lesion Evidence with Multiple Instance Learning

Authors: Lubin Gan, Jing Zhang, Xinming Zhang, Chengpeng Xu, Siying Wu, Xiaoyan Sun

Abstract: Whole-slide images may contain coexisting lesions, yet weak supervision typically provides only the most severe diagnosis. We formulate this setting as dominant-label multiple instance learning, where the observed label is the maximum-severity element of a latent lesion set. Standard multiclass MIL treats it as a complete one-hot description and therefore suppresses lower-severity evidence whenever it co-occurs with a dominant lesion. We propose DomSet-MIL, a label-aware framework that aligns the annotation mechanism across bag and instance levels. Dominant-Label Censored Likelihood models the reported diagnosis without declaring unreported lesions absent. Severity-Ordered Residual Routing assigns patch evidence from high to low severity and preserves residual support for secondary findings, while top-$q$ pooling links local routing scores to bag prediction. Experiments across natural and controlled settings show that DomSet-MIL achieves state-of-the-art performance in dominant-label prediction while more effectively recovering latent coexisting-lesion evidence. These findings demonstrate that modeling the dominant-label mechanism can improve diagnostic prediction without sacrificing clinically meaningful secondary findings.

cross Patch where the text is new: a compression view of where byte-level models should spend compute

Authors: Nicol\'as Vera Z\'u\~niga

Abstract: Byte-level language models such as the Byte Latent Transformer (BLT) run their large global model once per patch and start a patch where a small model's next-byte entropy is high. This rule rarely starts a patch at in-line computed results, such as the number after "=" in a worked solution, and accuracy on them collapses under tight patch budgets. We propose novelty patching, motivated by LZ77-style compression: start a patch where the upcoming bytes are unlikely to repeat the recent context. The rule is a lookup table over the previous two bytes, fitted in seconds on raw text with no labels, reference model or losses. In BLT-style models trained from scratch on a 1.9 GB math corpus at a matched ~11% patch rate (3 seeds each), it has no clear effect at 1M parameters, raises final-answer accuracy from 47% to 72% at 12M, and at 50M raises computed-result accuracy from 21% to 57% and final-answer accuracy from 60% to 90%; a table fitted on the raw pretraining corpus keeps the gain. Outside math the advantage shrinks: novelty lowers bits per byte on prose and ties entropy on source code and on synthetic execution traces. An analysis of where boundaries fall explains the pattern. Computed results have ordinary surprisal for the patcher's model, so surprisal-seeking rules pass over them: entropy concentrates patch starts on high-surprisal bytes most strongly of all rules (boundary enrichment 1.61) and is the worst on computed results, while the best rules sit near chance enrichment. Novelty works where new content begins at segment starts close to the computation, as in written math; a learned chunker (H-Net) finds such structure on its own, in math and in code. We also report what did not work: typed uncertainty as a training rule, switching rules after training, and a context-aware novelty predictor.

cross Lexical Reference Frames: Interpreting LM Residual Streams in Native Semantics

Authors: Jian Gu, Hongyu Zhang, Chunyang Chen, Aldeida Aleti

Abstract: The vocabulary of a language model is its native semantics: the embedding and the unembedding fix the meaning of a state at the two ends of the residual stream. Between them, existing methods either reuse a fixed lens or treat the states as a point cloud, and neither reads a state the way the model does. We argue that every hidden state has a native reference frame, induced by the layers that read it next and adaptive to the context. Lexical reference frames approximate it by reading a state through the model's own remaining layers with a stated number of rounds of attention between positions and a typical attention output for the rest, without fitting to the model's output. No lens, with fixed directions per layer, can exceed the square root of the native frame's coherence, 0.30 and 0.35 at mid-depth of SmolLM3-3B and OLMo3-7B, whereas the first-order frame, LexFrame[1], reaches 0.97 and 0.97 and reads a state within 0.16 nats of the model's output. Its coefficients attribute predictions more faithfully than attribution patching, its lexical basis steers a prediction while sparing other tokens, and its confident readings would let an early exit skip 0.45 and 0.42 of the layers, where lenses skip almost none. In this way, lexical reference frames make every hidden state of the model readable, attributable and steerable.

cross Mosaic: Multi-Session Agentic Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Yilong Xu, Minrui Xu, Xinyu Zhou, Xiang Long, Haochen Tan, Zhijiang Guo

Abstract: Most existing agentic reinforcement learning methods assume single-session trajectories, where interaction proceeds within one continuously accumulated context. However, agentic techniques such as context compaction and subagent forking may produce trajectories spanning multiple sessions, creating a mismatch between the single-session training assumption and inference-time execution. Therefore, we introduce Multi-Session Agentic Reinforcement Learning, a more general setting that supports an arbitrary number of sessions within a trajectory. Under this setting, a widely adopted method is to broadcast trajectory-level advantages to all sessions, but this does not distinguish contributions from various sessions or guide session allocation within trajectories. To address this issue, we propose Mosaic, a multi-session agentic RL method with critic-free hierarchical optimization. At the trajectory level, it incorporates session-usage signals into advantage estimation. At the session level, it shapes each session's advantage according to the estimated marginal contribution. Experiments on BrowseComp with Qwen3.5-4B show that Mosaic outperforms broadcasting in both context compaction and subagent forking scenarios while using fewer sessions on average. Further analyses show that our method achieves improvements in session utility and allocation, which we suggested is a promising target for future advances in multi-session agentic RL.

cross REKI: Registering Knowledge in VLAs

Authors: Juliette Marrie, Paul Pacaud, Adrien Ramanana Rahary, Shizhe Chen, Moritz B\"ohle

Abstract: We introduce REKI, a 2.4B vision-language-action model (VLA) pretrained exclusively on public data that sets a new Pareto frontier between performance and model size: on LIBERO-Plus, RoboTwin Clean2Rand and RoboCasa365, it is outperformed only by models about twice its size. REKI consists of a vision-language model (VLM) followed by a flow-matching action expert, and is pretrained on a curated mixture of public robot, egocentric human and vision-language data. Additionally, we endow it with a memory that gives the policy access to its recent past at negligible cost: a few register tokens in the action expert summarise the output of the VLM at each frame and persist in the expert's cache. This memory improves success in simulation and on real robots, and makes oscillations between poses about four times rarer. REKI uses a simple model design and training paradigm, and is optimised to be efficient at training and inference. We release the full code across data curation, training, inference and evaluation, deployment, and all pretrained and finetuned model weights: https://github.com/kyutai-labs/reki.

URLs: https://github.com/kyutai-labs/reki.

cross Whole-Body Dexterity

Authors: Conor Mc Gartoll, An Dang, Pranay Thangeda, Arjun Gupta, Carolina Higuera, Nima Fazeli, Mustafa Mukadam, Manikantan Nambi

Abstract: Current humanoid dexterous manipulation remains largely confined to end-effectors and simple grasps, falling short of humans' ability to engage the entire body by bracing and sliding bulky objects against the arms and torso. We introduce Whole-Body Dexterity (WBD), a general framework that uses the robot's body and appendages as contact surfaces to grasp and manipulate objects. Our sim-to-real pipeline first generates a dataset of feasible whole-body grasps, then trains reinforcement-learning experts on a general any-to-any repositioning objective by sampling initial and goal grasps from the dataset, and distills these experts into a single policy that uses proprioception and object geometry. We show that the distilled policy transfers zero-shot to a real Unitree G1, where it repositions a basketball and a cardboard box to regions around the torso and completes a long-horizon task that stows a box under one arm, carries it from a shelf to a table, and places it. The controller operates interactively, steered by coarse motion references from a human teleoperator. Across real-world whole-body manipulation tasks, the distilled controller completes 90% of trials, compared with 18% for direct retargeting and 4% for a generalist whole-body controller.

cross Attention over noisy examples: generalization and memorization in the exponential Hopfield network

Authors: Francesco Alemanno

Abstract: In Hebbian learning from examples a Hopfield network of $N$ neurons is not supplied with the $K$ archetypes it has to retrieve but with $M$ blurred examples of each, of quality $r$, and whether it can infer them depends on the load $\alpha=K/N$ (interference among them) and on the dataset entropy $\rho=(1-r^2)/(Mr^2)$ (how little the examples say about them). We transfer this protocol to the exponential Hopfield model, whose zero-temperature update is a step of softmax attention with the unlabelled examples as keys. A signal-to-noise analysis gives in closed form the overlap between the output and an unseen archetype, $m^{(1)}={\rm erf}(1/\sqrt{2\rho_{\rm eff}})$: the majority-rule law of the pairwise model at small load, with $\rho$ replaced by an attention entropy $\rho_{\rm eff}$ built from the weight the softmax gives to the correct class and the number of examples it spreads it over. Two consequences follow. First, in a window of inverse temperatures $\beta$ that widens as $\sqrt N$ the interference between archetypes is exponentially small in $N\beta$: the load drops out and the number of archetypes that can be learnt grows as $\exp(c\sqrt N)$. Second, learning fails differently: in the pairwise model through a spin-glass phase, in the exponential model through memorization: the softmax concentrates its weight on the training example closest to the input and returns it in place of the archetype. Seen from the input the softmax weights are those of the random energy model, and memorization is its freezing transition, at $\beta\sqrt{N(1-r^2)}\simeq\sqrt{2\ln M}$; it is measurable on a head whose keys are examples as the number of examples carrying weight. On MNIST the diagnostic follows the random-energy prediction; on fashion-MNIST it detects sub-clusters in the classes, and the best accuracy lies beyond the memorization threshold, where the softmax reads out a local sub-prototype.

cross Beyond Downstream QA: Rethinking How We Evaluate Agent Memory

Authors: Sebastian-Antonio Toma, Jean de Kernier, Mike Ply, Oliviu Matei

Abstract: Agent memory benchmarks evaluate whether an agent's responses reflect correct knowledge, measuring downstream question-answering accuracy as a proxy for memory quality. We show this evaluation paradigm is incomplete: it conflates memory correctness with retrieval and generation quality, producing both false positives where correct responses mask broken memory and false negatives where incorrect responses blame correct memory for retrieval failures. We introduce FEB-30, a benchmark of 30 fact-evolution trajectories spanning 6 diagnostic categories and 3 difficulty levels, constructed via paradigm inversion: documents are generated from known fact trajectories, yielding deterministic ground truth without human annotation. Each trajectory defines how a fact should evolve across multi-author artifacts (documents, emails, conversations), enabling direct inspection of the memory store after each ingestion step. Applying FEB-30 to a representative memory system, we demonstrate that intrinsic and extrinsic evaluation diverge by an average of 16 percentage points, with 44% of trajectories exceeding meaningful divergence thresholds. Diagnostic decomposition reveals that the system correctly classifies memory operations (create, update, reinforce, supersede) only 20% of the time, a failure entirely invisible to extrinsic evaluation, and that failure modes differ systematically by category and difficulty. We release the benchmark, document generation pipeline, and scoring framework to support reproducible evaluation of agent memory systems.

cross FutureLift: Lifting Trajectory Proposals into Bidirectional Future Graphs for Motion Forecasting

Authors: Daniel Grimm, J. Marius Z\"ollner

Abstract: Accurate motion prediction is critical for safe autonomous driving, yet even strong forecasters leave residual errors in their multimodal proposals. Existing refiners compress complete proposals or update their segments sequentially, preventing the full proposed future from serving as explicit relational context for correction. We introduce FutureLift, a backbone-agnostic refiner that represents each multimodal proposal as a heterogeneous future graph, with spatially grounded temporal nodes connected to agent histories, map elements, alternative modes, and, for joint prediction, other agents future trajectories. Bidirectional temporal edges allow later evidence to revise earlier segments in a single pass, and the updated nodes predict residual coordinate and confidence corrections. Requiring only proposals, scores, and scene context, FutureLift provides a common refinement interface for structurally different forecasting backbones. On Argoverse2 and the Waymo Open Motion Dataset, it consistently improves frozen proposals and outperforms native and specialized refiners across marginal and joint forecasting. On the WOMD motion-prediction leaderboard, FutureLift ranks first in minADE (0.5049m) and minFDE (0.9942m) among the compared standard-input methods, without LiDAR or ensembling, while achieving a competitive 0.4874 Soft mAP.

cross How Much Physics Can Vision Learn from Audio? Probing and Distilling Damping Structure

Authors: Zainab Afolabi, Vadim Selyutin, Zhao Gao, Babs Khalidson, Mohamed Ghoneim

Abstract: Physical understanding is crucial for decision making, yet vision models struggle to infer properties such as damping that govern how materials behave. Audio encodes such properties directly, because an object's acoustic response to impact reveals its damping through measurable decay. We probe frozen encoders for the quality factor $Q$, a dimensionless measure of how many oscillations an object completes before its ring dies away, and report how well each embedding orders impacts by $Q$ using the Spearman rank correlation $\rho$, for which $\rho=1$ is a perfect ordering and $\rho=0$ an unrelated one. Audio embeddings reach $\rho=0.73$ and retain most of that advantage on materials withheld from training, while a single video frame taken at the moment of impact, encoded by CLIP, supports only $\rho=0.28$. Having established where the signal lives, we ask how much of that gap vision can recover. We distill a frozen audio teacher into a frozen CLIP backbone through a trained projection head and find that this recovers a useful fraction of the audio advantage. Because pooled correlations on this task are dominated in practice by category identity, we also evaluate under a leave-one-material-out protocol in which the held-out material is withheld from the projection head as well as the probe. Distillation with a four-frame input improves the correlation there from 0.234 to 0.338 (+0.053 over the matched four-frame baseline, 14 of 17 materials), a gain that no category-level strategy can produce. Widening the visual input to four frames spanning the impact supplies half of that improvement at no acoustic cost, which indicates that part of what a single frame misses is visible dynamics.

Abstract: Meta deploys a heterogeneous fleet comprising NVIDIA GPUs, AMD GPUs, and proprietary MTIA accelerators to reduce costs and mitigate vendor lock-in. This paper presents our methods for optimizing alternative accelerators, using AMD Instinct MI350X versus NVIDIA B200 for recommendation model training as a case study. Authors: Julie Alberge, Linus Bleistein, Judith Ab\'ecassis, Marine Le Morvan, Ga\"el Varoquaux, David Holzm\"uller

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) now outperform gradient-boosted trees in classification and regression. cross Shortcut Sensitivity in Agent-Safety Evaluations

Authors: Hasan Demirkiran, Jens Ernstberger

Abstract: AI agents take actions through tools. Some actions are sensitive, so guards that supervise them must themselves be evaluated reliably. Yet the data used to evaluate a guard can include cues that make labels predictable without measuring the intended safety construct, and those cues can also influence guard decisions. We introduce a three-level shortcut-auditing framework and audit completed LinuxArena trajectories and two TS-Bench-derived step datasets. We distinguish label predictability, observational guard association, and paired decision sensitivity. In LinuxArena, shorter average shell commands strongly predict trajectory labels. In AgentDojo-Traj, exact-tool lookup falls from F_1=.901 with mixed-domain folds to .495 under domain holdout. In AgentHarm-Traj, released TS-Guard F_1 falls from .902 under row weighting to .719 with equal interaction weighting, while balanced accuracy rises. We then test the strongest safe-row marker association in a seven-configuration screen on a frozen 64-step AgentDojo-Traj cohort using repeated, interleaved Haiku calls. Rewriting the marker lowers blocking by 8.33 percentage points, while opaque tool aliases raise it by 11.46 points. All unsafe calls remain blocked; the changes concern false positives against inherited labels. Both effects remain nonzero after subtracting the formatting-placebo effect, with exploratory 95% ranges excluding zero. The same edits also change measured accuracy against inherited labels. This provides direct evidence that benchmark representation can materially change a guard's decisions and measured evaluation performance. The Haiku-sized effects do not reproduce in Luna or deterministic Q8 TS-Guard. We recommend domain group holdouts, evaluation-unit metrics, and placebo-controlled paired interventions.

cross Language Is a Security Surface for Agent Guards

Authors: Hasan Demirkiran, Jens Ernstberger

Abstract: Step-level guards decide, before each tool call, whether an agent's next action is safe given the user's request and the trajectory so far. Users write those requests in many languages, while guard prompts and the benchmarks on which purpose-built guards are trained and evaluated are English. We ask whether a guard's verdict depends on the language of the request. We build LinguaGuardBench from the 1,220 labelled steps of TS-Bench AgentDojo-Traj: we translate only the user instruction into German, French, Turkish, Swahili and Amharic, and keep every tool call, observation and label byte-identical, so any change in a verdict is attributable to language alone. Six English rewordings and a whitespace placebo serve as controls, and managed guards are run three times to measure their noise. Across 19 guards, from frontier APIs to 4B open models and four purpose-built agent guards, most open, small and purpose-built guards change verdicts under translation beyond their own noise floor, and the effect grows with the rarity of the language: among the nine guards below 13B parameters, the mean placebo-adjusted flip rate rises from +1.5 pp on French to +4.4 pp on Amharic. Purpose-built guards are the most sensitive. The direction of the damage tends to follow guard size: open guards of 12-30B parameters block more benign steps (up to +5.0 pp false positives), and every significant loss of recall is in a guard below 10B (up to -7.4 pp). Frontier guards shift by at most about one point; the strongest, Gemini 3.8 Flash, returns identical verdicts with and without the request. The damage is concentrated in a few instructions and, in a post-hoc analysis, falls on steps where the agent's reasoning restates the English request, consistent with guards reacting to inconsistency between channels. English paraphrase moves every guard less than translation does. We release the benchmark and all 489k verdicts.

cross Asking Smarter Questions: Targeted Active Preference Learning for Underspecified Rewards

Authors: Helena Merker, Nick Walker, Andreea Bobu

Abstract: Reward learning typically assumes the human's input covers everything that matters for the task. In practice, humans can only attend to so much at once, so their teaching naturally captures some features---or aspects of the task---better than others. Prior work has shown how the robot can identify which features were poorly supervised directly from its teaching data. But the current solution is to ask the human to try again, putting the burden on someone who already struggled to convey those features. We propose shifting this burden to the robot. Our key insight is that the same information that tells the robot what it doesn't know can help it ask better questions. Preference queries offer a low-effort channel for resolving this uncertainty, but only if the robot knows where to direct them and the human knows what to attend to when answering. We act on this insight in two ways: we build an informed prior that targets preference queries at the features that need resolving, and we accompany each query with an explanation that focuses the human's attention on exactly those features. In simulation, the informed prior beats the standard active preference learning baseline by up to $30.8\%$ in the low-query regime, where reducing human effort matters most. In a user study, the informed prior and explanations each significantly improve reward recovery and amplify each other.

cross Backward Compatibility in Zero-Bit Neural Audio Watermarking

Authors: Admir Krni\'c, Kosta Pavlovi\'c, Petar Nedi\'c, \v{Z}eljko Bolevi\'c, Slavko Kova\v{c}evi\'c, Igor Djurovi\'c

Abstract: Neural watermarking systems are typically trained as tightly coupled embedder-detector pairs and implicitly assume static deployment. In real-world deployments, embedders and detectors are frequently updated and selectively decoupled for security and versioning purposes, which can break detection in previously embedded content and, more critically, increase false-positive rates on embedders that a given detector is intentionally not intended to recognize. In this paper the concept of backward compatibility in zero-bit audio watermarking is formalized. A training framework is proposed that pairs a new detector with a sequence of frozen legacy embedders (its legacy chain), using a performance-weighted sampling strategy so that the detector retains accuracy across all earlier versions. This coupling, while necessary for backward compatibility, is shown to induce an embedder-collapse effect that catastrophically degrades the true negative rate (TNR) against out-of-chain embedders. To counteract this, a Angular Diversity Loss (ADL) is introduced that explicitly forces each new embedder to occupy a distinct region of the output space. The combined approach restores near-perfect TNR with no measurable cost to detection accuracy or perceptual audio quality.

cross Knowledge That Is Linear Before Fine-Tuning Generalizes After It

Authors: Faridoun Mehri, Hinrich Sch\"utze

Abstract: Not all facts a language model can recall support further learning equally well. We distinguish active facts, which support generalization beyond their own recall, from inert facts, which can be recalled but do not support such generalization in a given task. We study this distinction by constructing new relations from existing ones. Take the relation that maps a place to its continent (Paris -> Europe) and assign each of its answers an arbitrary new label (Europe -> crab, Asia -> zebra). The new relation then maps Paris -> crab. We fine-tune the model on the new relation for a subset of subjects, without telling it the original relation or the relabeling rule, and test whether it assigns the corresponding labels to held-out subjects that share an answer in the original relation. Having learned Paris -> crab, does it answer Munich -> crab? Across eleven language models and 70 relations, how linearly readable the original relation is in pretrained representations strongly predicts this generalization. Within each model, the two correlate across relations at Pearson r = 0.87 to 0.92. This association persists when controlling, separately and jointly, for the number of fine-tuning subjects and answers, the model's accuracy on the original relation, and how often the subjects occur in pretraining text. It is also robust to re-randomizing the label assignment and fine-tuning seed, and to replacing the animal labels with labels from thirteen other semantic categories. When we map a fine-tuned model's wrong label back to the original answer it was assigned to, Munich -> zebra becomes Munich -> Asia. Answers recovered this way are more often among the linear probe's top answers for the subject than among the pretrained model's own. Whether a new relation generalizes depends less on whether its facts can be recalled than on how readily their shared structure can be read out linearly.

cross GeoState: Learning View-Decoupled Geometry States for 3D World Modeling

Authors: Hongchang Liu, Jian Liu, Yujuan Ding, Liang Peng, Xun Jiang, Fei Ma, Xing Xu, Yi Bin

Abstract: Recent 3D world models increasingly use features from geometry foundation models (GFMs) as predictive world states, benefiting from their rich geometric priors. However, the view dependence of these features entangles scene dynamics with camera-induced variation, making the resulting state evolution harder to predict. We propose GeoState, a view-decoupled geometry representation for predictive 3D world modeling that separates scene-state evolution from camera-dependent observation. Specifically, a scene tokenizer aggregates multi-view GFM features into a view-decoupled scene state, which a geometry decoder queries using camera rays. An autoregressive world model then predicts future scene states, while camera motion is modeled separately. The predicted states can therefore be decoded under either predicted or externally specified cameras, while retaining a single context-derived scale throughout the rollout without per-frame scale alignment. Experiments show that GeoState outperforms prior 3D world models on Waymo, supports geometrically consistent cross-view querying, and generalizes zero-shot to KITTI and nuScenes.
