cross MLExpert: Towards a Generalist Agentic Model for Autonomous Machine Learning Engineering

Authors: Shangheng Du, Jinxin Shi, Shuaiyu Zhang, Yiming Wu, Junpeng Zhao, Shiyang Feng, Xin Li, Liang He, Xiangchao Yan, Bo Zhang

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are expanding from assisting scientific research and engineering to autonomously developing and improving AI systems, advancing AI for AI (AI4AI) as a pathway toward recursive self-improvement (RSI). Workflow-driven machine learning engineering (MLE) systems provide a practical path toward this pursuit through complex orchestration that manages long-horizon experimentation, while recent MLE systems adopt model-directed interaction, allowing models to autonomously organize experiments and adapt optimization strategies based on execution feedback. However, feedback from each experiment reflects several preceding decisions, and a long MLE run contains many such costly experiments. Training must therefore select useful supervision from long interaction trajectories and learn from intermediate outcomes. We propose a post-training method for autonomous long-horizon MLE optimization and apply it to train MLExpert, a 35B Mixture-of-Experts agentic model. We design the Kaggle-MLE Factory to construct executable tasks and an interaction harness for model-directed experimentation. Hierarchical agentic SFT transfers general tool-use and coding capabilities to MLE through outcome-guided supervision. Windowed Interaction RL (WIRL) treats the multi-turn decisions between successive experiment results as an execution window and assigns it a shared outcome-based reward. Asynchronous collection and policy correction support policy updates before the trajectory ends. On MLE-Bench Lite, MLExpert improves the average medal rate of the base model from 37.9% to 72.7%, achieving performance comparable to larger frontier models such as Kimi-K3 and GLM-5.3. Further experiments show that MLExpert improves performance on NatureBench Lite, agent scaffold optimization, and algorithm optimization, suggesting that the learned optimization capability generalizes to other tasks.

cross LIBERO-PeRM: Benchmarking Personalized Robotic Manipulation

Authors: Zhixu Li, Keqian Tang, Litian Gong, Jingyu Yao, Wenqian Zhang, Tianze Xu, Zehao Wang, Yuping Wang, Junge Zhang, Jiachen Li

Abstract: Personalization is essential for general-purpose robots to transition into household environments and achieve true practicality. While general robotic manipulation has been extensively studied, personalized robotic manipulation (PeRM) lacks a controlled benchmark for separating task completion from preference satisfaction. We introduce LIBERO-PeRM, a benchmark for preference learning in embodied agents. LIBERO-PeRM studies five questions: 1) how policies follow preferences in zero-shot settings; 2) how they learn single preferences from demonstrations; 3) how efficiently they acquire preferences as demonstrations increase; 4) how learned preferences generalize across layouts and tasks; and 5) how policies compose preferences and behave under conflicts. We propose a controlled formulation and taxonomy of observable preferences in simulation, covering motion, skill, and user-profile choices. This formulation is restricted to language-expressible preferences that can be instantiated in LIBERO without changing the task goal, evaluated automatically, and reused across tasks. Building on LIBERO, our procedural pipeline generates paired demonstrations with and without preferences. We create five task suites (312 tasks) with high-quality demonstrations, rich metadata, and preference-satisfaction metrics. Experiments show that current VLA policies struggle with zero-shot personalization, while SFT makes preferences learnable. Mixed training achieves better no-preference retention than sequential adaptation and improves performance with contextual prompts compared with preference-only training, but can weaken motion-preference learning; preference generalization, decomposition, and conflict handling remain difficult. The code, datasets, and demos can be found on the project website: https://libero-perm.github.io/.

URLs: https://libero-perm.github.io/.

replace LOOP-MAC: Learning-Based Multi-Agent Coordination with Guaranteed Local Feasibility in a Virtual Power Plant

Authors: Meiyi Li, Lucas Ruda, Javad Mohammadi

Abstract: By aggregating distributed energy resources (DERs) into a single dispatchable resource, virtual power plants (VPPs) enable the grid to use the flexibility of numerous small-scale DERs. Delivering an aggregate VPP commitment requires multi-agent coordination, where agents manage local DER models and operating constraints while jointly meeting a power commitment within limited communication rounds. Classical distributed optimization is computationally intensive, requiring iterative local optimization and repeated communication to reach consensus. Learning-based methods can accelerate coordination but often lack guarantees of local feasibility and accurate commitment tracking. This paper proposes LOOP-MAC (Learning to Optimize the Optimization Process for Multi-Agent Coordination), a fully decentralized learning-based framework that ensures local feasibility by construction while efficiently reducing the aggregate commitment gap. LOOP-MAC employs a two-stage decision process. Learned coordination uses neural optimizers equipped with a gauge layer to produce locally feasible schedules through a few rounds of message exchange. Local adjustment then rebalances each schedule toward feasible flexibility extremes using a commitment-gap estimate inferred from messages already received. The adjusted schedule remains locally feasible as a convex combination of feasible schedules, and the adjustment requires no additional communication round. On a 100-agent VPP, five communication rounds followed by local adjustment reduce the mean commitment gap from 5.1% to 0.75% of the agents' aggregate absolute power and the optimality gap from 3.93% to 3.07% relative to learned coordination alone. Per-agent online computation takes approximately 1.1 ms, including the adjustment.

replace Pairwise Difference Learning for Classification

Authors: Mohamed Karim Belaid, Maximilian Rabus, Eyke H\"ullermeier

Abstract: Pairwise difference learning (PDL) has recently been introduced as a new meta-learning technique for regression. Instead of learning a mapping from instances to outcomes in the standard way, the key idea is to learn a function that takes two instances as input and predicts the difference between the respective outcomes. Given a function of this kind, predictions for a query instance are derived from every training example and then averaged. This paper extends PDL toward the task of classification and proposes a meta-learning technique for inducing a PDL classifier by solving a suitably defined (binary) classification problem on a paired version of the original training data. We analyze the performance of the PDL classifier in a large-scale empirical study and find that it outperforms state-of-the-art methods in terms of prediction performance. Last but not least, we provide an easy-to-use and publicly available implementation of PDL in a Python package.

replace STAMP: Symmetric Temporal Averaging Message Passing for Domain Adaptation in Chronologically Split Graphs

Authors: Joo Young Park, Sejun Park, Hyunwoo Park

Abstract: This paper addresses domain adaptation in graph-structured data under chronological splits. We consider transductive semi-supervised node classification (SSNC), in which labels of historical nodes are used to predict labels of more recent nodes. Temporal dependencies in graph connectivity induce distribution shifts across time, often degrading the performance of models trained on past nodes. Despite its practical relevance, domain adaptation under chronological graph splits remains relatively underexplored. We propose Symmetric Temporal Averaging Message Passing (STAMP), a timestamp-balanced operator that replaces standard neighborhood aggregation with temporally aligned propagation. We call its specialized variant for the heterogeneous ogbn-mag dataset STAMP-mag, reserving STAMP for the general formulation. By balancing messages across timestamps, STAMP enforces first-moment invariance across temporal domains while remaining scalable and easy to implement. Unlike many existing domain adaptation methods, whose assumptions are difficult to verify on graphs, STAMP relies on transparent, practically checkable conditions and is supported by theoretical analysis. Empirically, STAMP and STAMP-mag improve accuracy across all evaluated temporal graph benchmarks. STAMP achieves state-of-the-art performance on ogbn-arxiv, while STAMP-mag does so on ogbn-mag. For temporal-split Pokec, where no established benchmark exists, applying STAMP to the state-of-the-art model for the original dataset consistently improves performance under our chronological split. These results demonstrate that timestamp-balanced propagation is an effective and practical approach to mitigating temporal distribution shift in graph learning.

replace BOLT: Universal Classifier Training via a Bayes-Error Upper Bound

Authors: Mohammadreza Tavasoli Naeini, Ali Bereyhi, Morteza Noshad, Ben Liang, Alfred O. Hero III

Abstract: This work introduces a novel approach to compute a universal upper bound on the Bayes error rate (BER) from classifier-output samples, which serves as a Bayes-error-related performance criterion for the underlying classification task. We show that the bound is tight in the binary case and characterize the max-sum gap that appears in the aggregate multiclass formulation. Based on this bound, we define a new loss function, called Bayes optimal learning threshold (BOLT), for training parameterized classifiers. For each data sample, the BOLT loss computes a term whose expectation upper-bounds the Bayes error, so that during training the model parameters can be updated toward minimizing this bound. Numerical experiments with both synthetic and real-world datasets show that, in the reported settings, BOLT training often attains lower held-out classification error than cross-entropy training.

replace FANTF: Fuzzy attention network based transformers for time series analysis

Authors: Sanjay Chakraborty, Fredrik Heintz

Abstract: This paper introduces Fuzzy Attention Network-Based Transformer (FANTF), an approach for time series forecasting, classification, and anomaly detection. FANTF combines fuzzy logic concepts with transformer architectures through a novel fuzzy attention mechanism. The proposed mechanism is inspired by Type-2 fuzzy set theory. It uses a learnable Gaussian uncertainty perturbation to model uncertainty in attention relevance scores. This makes it possible for the model to accurately capture uncertainty in temporal relationships better than normal deterministic attention. Transformers capacity to record complex temporal patterns and multivariate connections is enhanced by FANTF. The approach is both architecture-agnostic and task-agnostic. The proposed fuzzy attention module can be used as a direct replacement for the standard attention layer. To evaluate its effectiveness, FANTF is integrated into five transformer backbones: Transformer, Informer, PatchTST, Crossformer, and iTransformer. The fuzzy attention mechanism produces softer and more diverse attention distributions. It provides uncertainty-aware interpretations through attention heatmap visualizations. Experimental evaluations on some real-life datasets reveal that FANTF enhances the forecasting performance, classification, and anomaly detection tasks over traditional transformer-based models. FANTF achieves statistically significant and competitive performance improvements over standard transformer-based baselines on most benchmark datasets (p < 0.05, Wilcoxon signed-rank test), observed under noisy and non-stationary time series conditions.

replace A self-learning framework for adaptive free-form pipe routing in aeroengines

Authors: Caicheng Wang, Zili Wang, Shuyou Zhang, Yongzhe Xiang, Zheyi Li, Jianrong Tan

Abstract: Pipe routing is a highly complex, time-consuming, and no-deterministic polynomial-time hard (NP-hard) problem in aeroengine design. Despite extensive research efforts in optimizing constant-curvature pipe routing, the growing demand for free-form pipes poses new challenges. Dynamic design environments and fuzzy layout rules further impact the optimization performance and efficiency. To tackle these challenges, this study proposes a self-learning-based method (SLPR) for optimizing free-form pipe routing in aeroengines. The SLPR is based on the proximal policy optimization (PPO) algorithm and integrates a unified rule modeling framework for efficient obstacle detection and fuzzy rule modeling in continuous space. Additionally, a potential energy table is constructed to enable rapid queries of layout tendencies and interference. The agent within SLPR iteratively refines pipe routing and accumulates the design knowledge through interaction with the environment. Once the design environment shifts, the agent can swiftly adapt by fine-tuning network parameters. Comparative tests reveal that SLPR ensures smooth pipe routing through cubic non-uniform B-spline (NURBS) curves, avoiding redundant pipe segments found in constant-curvature pipe routing. Results in both static and dynamic design environments demonstrate that SLPR outperforms three representative baselines in terms of the pipe length reduction, the adherence to layout rules, the path complexity, and the computational efficiency. Furthermore, tests in dynamic environments indicate that SLPR eliminates labor-intensive searches from scratch and even yields superior solutions compared to the retrained model. These results highlight the practical value of SLPR for real-world pipe routing, meeting lightweight, precision, and sustainability requirements of the modern aeroengine design.

replace KnowEEG: Explainable Knowledge Driven EEG Classification

Authors: Amarpal Sahota, Navid Mohammadi Foumani, Raul Santos-Rodriguez, Zahraa S. Abdallah

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) classification has shown significant promise in applications ranging from neurodegenerative disease detection to brain-computer interfaces (BCIs). Whilst deep learning methods have increasingly dominated the research landscape in recent years; these models remain largely opaque 'black boxes' with limited interpretability. In this paper, we revisit the utility of feature-based classification via a systematic pipeline designed to integrate per-electrode signal statistics with cross-brain connectivity metrics. The proposed "KnowEEG" pipeline extracts 783 per-electrode statistical features alongside between-electrode connectivity statistics, utilizing a balanced sampling strategy between high-dimensional per electrode features and connectivity. This feature-based pipeline achieves performance comparable to or exceeding state-of-the-art deep learning models across five diverse classification tasks: emotion detection, mental workload classification, eyes open/closed detection, abnormal EEG classification, and event detection. This performance is achieved without the need for Graphics Processing Unit (GPU) resources. Furthermore, the pipeline provides inherent explainability through feature importance rankings. We demonstrate via the eyes open/closed task that this explainability identifies discriminative biomarkers in the occipital region that are consistent with established neuroscience literature. These results suggest that despite significant advances in deep learning, structured feature engineering remains a high performance and interpretable method for EEG classification.

replace Parallel Layer Normalization for Universal Approximation

Authors: Yunhao Ni, Yuxin Guo, Yuhe Liu, Wenxin Sun, Jie Luo, Wenjun Wu, Lei Huang

Abstract: Layer Normalization (LN) is usually studied for its role in optimization, but it also acts as a multivariate nonlinearity between affine layers. We investigate how grouping LN units affects the approximation power of neural networks as width grows. In Parallel Layer Normalization networks (PLN-Nets), fixed-dimensional LN units are applied independently to groups between two affine layers. We prove that a shallow LN-Net with one LN unit is non-universal regardless of width, whereas PLN-Nets become universal as the number of parallel groups grows. The mechanism extends to deep networks, Root Mean Square Normalization (RMSNorm) variants, position-wise feed-forward networks, and convolutional networks under suitable grouping structures. Controlled MLP and physics-informed neural network (PINN) experiments support PLN's practical viability, while CNN and ViT experiments show that group count shapes the finite-sample fitting envelope but does not act alone: the grouped axes, replacement site, and label structure lead to distinct fitting and generalization behavior.

replace HERO: Preserving Structure and Semantics in Heterogeneous Continual Graph Learning

Authors: Guiquan Sun, Xikun Zhang, Jingchao Ni, Dongjin Song

Abstract: Machine learning on heterogeneous graphs has advanced rapidly, driven by the diverse entities, relations, and semantics inherent in real-world data. However, most existing studies assume static graphs, whereas real-world graph data are often continuously updated. Continual learning in this setting is particularly challenging because historical knowledge is encoded not only in target-node representations, but also in their heterogeneous structural contexts and relation-dependent semantic responses. To this end, we introduce HERO, a heterogeneous continual graph learning framework that preserves historical knowledge at both structural and semantic levels while adapting to incoming tasks. We analyze experience replay as an approximation to the unavailable historical gradient and decompose its error into target-selection and context-reconstruction terms. This analysis shows that replaying target nodes alone is generally insufficient for relation-sensitive HGNNs, as removing typed neighborhood context can alter both representations and historical gradients. Motivated by this, HERO employs DiSCo to select representative target nodes and reconstruct compact heterogeneous contexts through relation-aware multi-type neighbor expansion. To further preserve knowledge that cannot be captured by replayed subgraphs alone, HERO aligns task-conditioned predictions and heterogeneous semantic responses through multi-level knowledge distillation. A lightweight look-ahead adaptation step is additionally used to improve plasticity on incoming tasks. Experiments on four datasets with three HGNN backbones show that HERO achieves the best or tied-best average performance among state-of-the-art baselines, while maintaining competitive forgetting. Our code is available at https://github.com/gqBond/HCGL.

URLs: https://github.com/gqBond/HCGL.

replace Tourist arrival forecasting with game-theoretic social influence weighting and deep learning

Authors: Ali Nikseresht

Abstract: In the era of Industry 5.0, data-driven decision-making has become indispensable for planning in service systems such as tourism. This paper proposes a non-linear hybrid approach for forecasting tourist arrivals to Hong Kong from five major source markets before COVID-19 and to Sanya, China, through the COVID-19 shock. The method integrates multiple sources of Internet big data and employs a cooperative game-theoretic algorithm to identify coalitions of opinion leaders on social media, whose content then receives larger weights. The Empirical Wavelet Transform (EWT), applied causally, manages nonstationary attributes, and a Stacked Bi-directional Long Short-Term Memory (Stacked BiLSTM) network tuned by Bayesian optimization generates the forecasts. We prove that the Shapley values of the underlying trust-weighted coverage game have a closed form and that the coalition of maximal synergy can be found exactly in polynomial time. Relative to the same network fed only with past arrivals, Internet big data reduce the one-step-ahead root mean squared error by 4.1-41.7% across the Hong Kong markets and opinion-leader weighting by a further 10.1-49.4%, against 5.3% and 8.7% for Sanya. The approach compares favorably with published benchmarks and remains robust under volatile conditions, which suggests uses in logistics, supply chains and production planning.

replace Stabilizing the Modality Gap: Null-Space Projected Continual Learning for Vision-Language Models

Authors: Tiantian Peng, Yuyang Liu, Shuo Yang, Qiuhe Hong, YongHong Tian

Abstract: The modality gap is intrinsic to multimodal alignment, where embeddings from different modalities remain partially separated in a shared representation space. In continual learning, CLIP-based vision-language models (VLMs) not only suffer catastrophic forgetting but also experience modality gap drift that disrupts the shared embedding geometry. Such drift degrades cross-modal alignment and generalization. We propose FPCD, an efficient framework combining Fast Null-space Projection (FNP) and Contrastive Distillation (CD). FNP accumulates compact Gram matrices of previous tasks' activations and projects gradients onto layer-adaptive approximate null spaces to reduce interference with learned representations. By replacing singular value decomposition with symmetric eigendecomposition, FNP accelerates projection-basis construction by a factor of 5-9. CD uses a small reference set to align the bidirectional image-to-text and text-to-image similarity distributions of the fine-tuned model with CLIP, thereby preserving pretrained cross-modal relations. On the 11-task MTIL benchmark, FPCD achieves 87.6% Last, 76.4% Average, and 65.2% Transfer. It also maintains more stable modality gaps and stronger zero-shot retrieval performance on COCO Captions and Flickr30k than competing methods. These results highlight the importance of controlling modality gap drift during continual adaptation of VLMs.

replace AI Scientists for Building Virtual-Cell Models

Authors: Xiangru Tang, Jiapeng Chen, Daniel Shao, Zhuoyun Yu, Yan Cui, Weixu Wang, Fang Wu, Yuchen Zhuang, Wenqi Shi, Zhi Huang, Arman Cohan, Xihong Lin, Fabian Theis, Smita Krishnaswamy, Mark Gerstein

Abstract: Autonomous agents, from virtual laboratories to biological data-analysis agents and co-scientists, have begun to turn scientific goals into hypotheses. Whether they can go further and convert real, noisy and complex biological data into executable model hypotheses and architectures that encode testable assumptions remains unclear. Predicting how cells respond to genetic and chemical perturbations is a natural test of this ability: it is central to decoding disease mechanisms and therapeutic discovery, yet it demands cross-disciplinary expertise rarely concentrated in a single research group. Here, we introduce CellForge, an organized team of autonomous agents for single-cell perturbation modeling. Given a dataset and a task, it surveys the literature, parses the data, identifies relevant baselines, and formulates a model hypothesis through critic-mediated discussion among agents specialized in data modeling, single-cell biology, and deep learning. It then writes the training code, runs the experiments, and returns a trained predictor including architecture, code, and validation results. The architecture itself is the hypothesis: CellForge produces it end-to-end, with no human specifying the design, loss, or training protocol. Across six perturbation datasets spanning transcriptomic, epigenomic, and proteomic contexts, CellForge yields models that are competitive with hand-crafted state-of-the-art results on established tasks and extends the same workflow to paired multi-omic readouts. Blinded human evaluators rate CellForge's research plans as more scientifically sound and innovative than those from single LLMs or deep-research systems. In summary, we show autonomous agents can complete the full hypothesis-to-validation cycle in single-cell biology, with an AI effectively building another AI, in a complex setting that currently requires a team of human experts.

replace Multimodal Remote Inference

Authors: Keyuan Zhang, Yin Sun, Bo Ji

Abstract: We consider a remote inference system with multiple modalities, where a multimodal machine learning (ML) model performs real-time inference using features collected from remote sensors. When sensor observations evolve dynamically over time, fresh features are critical for inference tasks. However, timely delivery of features from all modalities is often infeasible under limited network resources. To address this challenge, we formulate a multimodal scheduling problem to minimize the ML model's inference error. We model this error as a general function of the Age of Information (AoI) vector, where AoI quantifies data freshness. We cast the problem as a semi-Markov decision process (SMDP) and derive an equivalent reformulation with a reduced state set. We then show that the problem has fundamentally different chain structures in the two-modality and multi-modality cases. For the two-modality case, we prove that the optimal policy has an index-based threshold structure. For the general multi-modality case (two or more modalities), we develop an optimal switching-type policy (OPT-ST), which is computed using a multichain policy iteration algorithm (MPI). To further reduce complexity, we also develop two low-complexity policies: the round-robin-like switching-type policy (RR-ST) and the round-robin-like threshold policy (RR-Thres). Numerical results show that OPT-ST achieves the lowest inference error among all baselines, with reductions of up to 80.3%. In a five-modality case, RR-ST and RR-Thres achieve $5.2\times$ and $89.8\times$ speedups in offline computation over OPT-ST, respectively, at the cost of increasing the inference error by 23.3% and 38.6%, respectively.

replace Thinking-Free Policy Initialization Makes Distilled Reasoning Models More Effective and Efficient Reasoners

Authors: Xin Xu, Clive Bai, Kai Yang, Tianhao Chen, Yang Wang, Saiyong Yang, Can Yang

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Reward (RLVR) effectively solves complex tasks but demands extremely long context lengths during training, leading to substantial computational costs. While multi-stage training can partially mitigate this, starting with overly short contexts often causes irreversible performance degradation, ultimately failing to reduce overall training compute significantly. In this paper, we introduce Thinking-Free Policy Initialization (TFPI), a simple yet effective adaptation to RLVR that bridges long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) distillation and standard RLVR. TFPI employs a simple ThinkingFree operation, explicitly discarding the thinking content via a direct append, to reduce token usage during inference. Training with ThinkingFree-adapted inputs improves performance and lowers token consumption, even in the original slow-thinking mode. Extensive experiments across various benchmarks have shown that TFPI accelerates RL convergence, achieves a higher performance ceiling, and yields more token-efficient reasoning models without specialized rewards or complex training designs. With TFPI only, we train a 4B model to reach 89.0% accuracy on AIME24 and 65.5% on LiveCodeBench using less than 4K H20 hours.

replace Multi-task Neural Diffusion Processes

Authors: Joseph Rawson, Domniki Ladopoulou, Petros Dellaportas

Abstract: Neural diffusion processes provide a scalable, non-Gaussian approach to modelling distributions over functions, but existing formulations are limited to single-task inference and do not capture dependencies across related tasks. We propose multi-task neural diffusion processes (MT-NDPs), an extension that incorporates a task encoder to enable task-conditioned probabilistic regression and few-shot adaptation across related functions. The task encoder extracts a low-dimensional representation from context observations and conditions the diffusion model on this representation, allowing information sharing across tasks while accommodating variable-sized context sets and preserving permutation invariance with respect to the ordering of context observations. We evaluate MT-NDPs through synthetic experiments and two real-world applications involving wind turbine data and school examination outcomes. The results demonstrate improved predictive accuracy relative to single-task diffusion baselines when context observations are available, alongside competitive performance against neural-process and Gaussian-process alternatives. In the wind-energy application, MT-NDPs achieve the lowest average coverage error among the evaluated models at larger context sizes, demonstrating improved predictive uncertainty calibration. Overall, our findings highlight the potential of task-conditioned diffusion models for uncertainty-aware multi-task regression and few-shot adaptation to previously unseen tasks.

replace Graph Homomorphism Distortion: A structural (pseudo)-metric for attributed graphs

Authors: Martin Carrasco, Olga Zaghen, Kavir Sumaraj, Erik Bekkers, Bastian Rieck

Abstract: A large driver of the complexity of graph learning is the interplay between structure and features. When analyzing the expressivity of graph neural networks, however, existing approaches ignore features in favor of structure, making it nigh-impossible to assess to what extent two graphs with close features should be considered similar. We address this by developing a new (pseudo-)metric based on graph homomorphisms Inspired by concepts from metric geometry, our graph homomorphism distortion measures the minimal worst-case distortion that node features of one graph are subjected to when mapping one graph to another. We demonstrate the utility of our (pseudo-)metric by showing that i.) it can be efficiently approximated while retaining distinction capabilities, ii.) it provides a granular approach to graph distinction, and, iii.) it serves as a structural encoding, extending the concept of homomorphism-based descriptors to attributed graphs, thus improving the predictive capabilities of graph neural networks.

replace SUPN: Shallow Universal Polynomial Networks for Efficient Function Approximation

Authors: Zachary Morrow, Michael Penwarden, Brian Chen, Aurya Javeed, Akil Narayan, John D. Jakeman

Abstract: Deep neural networks (DNNs) and Kolmogorov-Arnold networks (KANs) are flexible function approximators, but their accuracy can require substantial network depth, width, and trainable parameter counts. Beyond making the resulting networks less transparent, large parameter spaces can increase training cost and produce solutions that are sensitive to optimization and parameter initialization, leading to variability in generalization error. For these reasons, we propose Shallow Universal Polynomial Networks (SUPNs), which retain a single hidden layer while increasing the expressivity of each unit through a learnable multivariate polynomial inside each activation, which we refer to as a polynomial pre-activation. This construction trades architectural depth and width for greater expressivity within each hidden unit, reducing parameter counts and training cost while preserving a shallow computational graph. We establish two complementary universal approximation results: SUPNs are universal by increasing network width at fixed polynomial complexity, as in classical shallow-network approximation, and by increasing polynomial complexity at fixed finite width. We further prove that finite SUPNs can approach the best approximation attainable in a prescribed polynomial space to arbitrary relative tolerance, derive explicit quasi-optimal parameters, and establish corresponding convergence rates. We empirically evaluate SUPNs against DNNs, KANs, and polynomial projection on smooth, nonsmooth, and discontinuous function-approximation problems in one, two, and ten dimensions using more than 16,000 trained models. Across the target functions studied, SUPNs frequently achieve lower approximation error and variability than DNNs and KANs at comparable parameter counts while retaining lower training cost, supporting their use as efficient function approximators when the target structure is not known a priori.

replace AlignSAE: Concept-Aligned Sparse Autoencoders

Authors: Minglai Yang, Xinyu Guo, Zhengliang Shi, Jinhe Bi, Steven Bethard, Mihai Surdeanu, Liangming Pan

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) encode factual knowledge within hidden parametric spaces that are difficult to inspect or control. While Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) can decompose hidden activations into more fine-grained, interpretable features, they often struggle to reliably align these features with human-defined concepts, resulting in entangled and distributed feature representations. To address this, we introduce AlignSAE, a method that aligns SAE features with a predefined ontology through a "pre-train, then post-train" curriculum. After an initial unsupervised training phase, we apply supervised post-training to bind specific concepts to dedicated latent slots while preserving the remaining capacity for general reconstruction. This separation creates an interpretable interface where specific concepts can be inspected and controlled without interference from unrelated features. Empirical results on GPT-2 across synthetic biographical QA (1-hop, 6 concepts) and compositional relational reasoning (2-hop, 20 concepts) demonstrate that AlignSAE enables precise causal interventions, such as reliable concept swaps (94.8% success in 1-hop), by targeting single, semantically aligned slots. AlignSAE achieves disentanglement improvements of $9$--$\infty\times$ over standard SAEs (measured via Mutual Information Gap), and further supports multi-hop reasoning and a mechanistic probe of grokking-like generalization dynamics. Although our evaluation focuses on GPT-2, AlignSAE itself is model-agnostic.

replace CarBench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Neural Surrogates on High-Fidelity 3D Car Aerodynamics

Authors: Mohamed Elrefaie, Dule Shu, Matt Klenk, Faez Ahmed

Abstract: Benchmarking has been the cornerstone of progress in computer vision, natural language processing, and the broader deep learning domain, driving algorithmic innovation through standardized datasets and reproducible evaluation protocols. The growing availability of large-scale Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD) datasets has opened new opportunities for applying machine learning to aerodynamic and engineering design. Yet, despite this progress, there exists no standardized benchmark for large-scale numerical simulations in engineering design. In this work, we introduce CarBench, the first comprehensive benchmark dedicated to large-scale 3D car aerodynamics, performing a large-scale evaluation of state-of-the-art models on DrivAerNet++, the largest public dataset for automotive aerodynamics, containing over 8,000 high-fidelity car simulations. We assess eleven architectures spanning neural operator methods (e.g., Fourier Neural Operator), geometric deep learning (PointNet, RegDGCNN, PointMAE, PointTransformer), transformer-based neural solvers (Transolver, Transolver++, AB-UPT), and implicit field networks (TripNet). Our analysis covers predictive accuracy, physical consistency, computational efficiency, and statistical uncertainty. To accelerate progress in data-driven engineering, we open-source the benchmark framework, including training pipelines, uncertainty estimation routines based on bootstrap resampling, and pretrained model weights, establishing the first reproducible foundation for large-scale learning from high-fidelity CFD simulations, available at https://github.com/Mohamedelrefaie/CarBench.

replace Scientific feedback reveals productive regimes beyond training conventions

Authors: Yoon Pyo Lee, Samrendra Roy, Souvik Chakraborty, Syed Bahauddin Alam

Abstract: Human-imposed design conventions can restrict the alternatives represented in scientific training data and shape the search preferences learned by generative models. We show that scientific performance feedback can redirect the learned search distribution toward productive regions beyond those conventions, without explicitly rewarding departures from them. We test this process in reactor lattice design with simulated feedback and chemical reaction-condition selection with measured yields, using a pretrained sequence policy and a small non-language policy, respectively. In both settings, adaptation improves scientific performance while increasing generation of choices excluded from the corresponding supervised data. Removing the target penalty redirects four of five matched runs, linking the direction of adaptation to the scientific objective. Together, these results show how scientific feedback reshapes inherited search preferences and reveals productive alternatives beyond training conventions, with attribution controls distinguishing learned redistribution from candidate availability and reusable search information.

replace DeFlow: Decoupling Behavior-Prior Modeling and Value Maximization for Offline Policy Extraction

Authors: Zhancun Mu, Chi Zhang

Abstract: We present DeFlow, a decoupled offline RL framework that leverages flow matching to faithfully capture complex behavior manifolds. Optimizing generative policies is computationally prohibitive, typically necessitating backpropagation through ODE solvers. We address this by learning a lightweight refinement module within an explicit, data-derived trust region of the flow manifold, rather than sacrificing the iterative generation capability via single-step distillation. This way, we bypass solver differentiation and eliminate the need for balancing loss terms, ensuring stable improvement while fully preserving the flow's iterative expressivity. Empirically, DeFlow achieves superior performance on the challenging OGBench benchmark and demonstrates efficient offline-to-online adaptation.

replace TabClustPFN: A Prior-data Fitted Network for Tabular Data Clustering

Authors: Tianqi Zhao, Guanyang Wang, Yan Shuo Tan, Qiong Zhang

Abstract: Prior-data Fitted Networks (PFNs) have reframed supervised tabular learning as single-pass in-context inference without per-dataset optimization. Extending this paradigm to clustering is appealing yet fundamentally more challenging, due to absent supervision, unknown cluster cardinality, and label switching inherent to partition outputs. Existing PFN-based clustering methods address these challenges only partially, either requiring known cardinality as input or relying on unstable label-ordering conventions and overly restrictive synthetic priors. We introduce TabClustPFN, a clustering PFN that resolves all challenges jointly through co-designed prior, objective, and architecture. Our hybrid pretraining prior captures heterogeneous real-tabular geometry; our decoupled partition inference network--cardinality inference network architecture jointly infers cluster assignments and cardinality in a single pass; and our SoftARI training objective is permutation-invariant by construction, eliminating the need for any label-ordering convention. On a 44 curated real-world tabular benchmark, TabClustPFN achieves state-of-the-art clustering performance against classical, deep, and amortized baselines, with runtime comparable to efficient baselines. Code is available at https://github.com/Tianqi-Zhao/TabClustPFN.

URLs: https://github.com/Tianqi-Zhao/TabClustPFN.

replace Product Interaction: An Algebraic Formalism for Deep Learning Architectures

Authors: Haonan Dong, Chun-Wun Cheng, Angelica I. Aviles-Rivero

Abstract: Modern neural architectures are largely developed as distinct empirical constructions, despite recurring principles across convolutional, attention, state-space, and equivariant models. We introduce Product Interactions, an algebraic formalism that unifies these architectures under a common construction principle and turns their design into a systematic procedure. The core shift is representing data as algebraic elements and treating multiplication, rather than linear transformation alone, as a primitive architectural operation. Architectures are generated by composing multiplication operators, with input dependence organized by self-interaction order. Within this formalism, we derive convolutional and equivariant networks as symmetry-constrained first-order product interactions, recover Mamba from state-space models as an increase in self-interaction order from one to three, and establish attention as a third-order interaction from composing the same operator. Equivariance is incorporated by constraining the algebraic product via symmetry: translation equivariance yields convolution, while SO(3)-compatibility produces the Clebsch--Gordan product and tensor-field interactions. This perspective recasts architecture design as an algebraic construction problem: the choice of algebra defines admissible interactions, symmetry constrains their structure, and composition generates higher-order architectures. The framework is thus generative. Following these principles, we construct a simplified-algebra dynamical layer that improves both accuracy and throughput over Mamba on CIFAR-10. Experiments show that interaction order affects accuracy non-monotonically and symmetry constraints boost learnability. Product Interactions provides a mathematical methodology for analyzing existing architectures and systematically designing new ones.

replace CoCopula: Conformal Calibration and Copula Aggregation for Reliable Renewable Energy Forecasting

Authors: Alireza Moradi, Mathieu Tanneau, Reza Zandehshahvar, Pascal Van Hentenryck

Abstract: The rapid growth of renewable energy penetration has intensified the need for reliable probabilistic forecasts to support grid operations at aggregated (fleet or system) levels. In practice, however, system operators often lack access to fleet-level probabilistic models and instead rely on site-level forecasts produced by heterogeneous third-party providers. Constructing coherent and calibrated fleet-level probabilistic forecasts from such inputs remains challenging due to complex cross-site dependencies and aggregation-induced miscalibration. Motivated by a real-world operational challenge identified through collaboration with an industry partner, this paper presents a probabilistic aggregation and calibration framework that directly converts site-level probabilistic forecasts into reliable fleet-level forecasts. The paper introduces \method~ (\textbf{Co}nformal Calibration and \textbf{Copula} Aggregation), which integrates Copula-based dependence modeling to capture cross-site correlations followed by Context-Aware Conformal Prediction (CACP) to mitigate miscalibration at the aggregated level. This combination enables dependence-aware aggregation while improving empirical coverage and maintaining sharp prediction intervals. Experiments on large-scale solar generation datasets from three system operators in the United States including MISO, ERCOT, and SPP demonstrate that the proposed \method{} approach achieves near-nominal empirical coverage and consistently lower Winkler scores than competing aggregation-based baselines across different target coverage levels. Importantly, \method{} achieves performance comparable to directly calibrated system-level forecasts, demonstrating its practical value when dedicated fleet-level forecasting models are unavailable.

replace Soft Sensor for Bottom-Hole Pressure Estimation in Petroleum Wells Using Long Short-Term Memory and Transfer Learning

Authors: M. A. Fernandes, E. Gildin, M. A. Sampaio

Abstract: Monitoring bottom-hole variables in petroleum wells is essential for production optimization, safety, and emissions reduction. Permanent Downhole Gauges (PDGs) provide real-time pressure data but face reliability and cost issues. We propose a machine learning-based soft sensor to estimate flowing Bottom-Hole Pressure (BHP) using wellhead and topside measurements. A Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) model is introduced and compared with Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) and Ridge Regression. We also pioneer Transfer Learning for adapting models across operational environments. Tested on real offshore datasets from Brazil's Pre-salt basin, the methodology achieved Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) consistently below 2\%, outperforming benchmarks. This work offers a cost-effective, accurate alternative to physical sensors, with broad applicability across diverse reservoir and flow conditions.

replace The impact of temporal resampling on the safety of clinical reinforcement learning: A simulated trial

Authors: Thomas Frost, Hrisheekesh Vaidya, Steve Harris

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is a type of artificial intelligence for making optimal choices. In healthcare, researchers generally use offline RL (ORL), where models are trained and evaluated from retrospective observational data. To accommodate naturally irregular clinical records, researchers often resample the data into uniform time intervals before training (known as binning). However, binning may create counterfactual trajectories that do not represent clinical practice. We first quantified these distortions in patients receiving continuous insulin infusions in the MIMIC-IV intensive care database. Binning reversed the apparent causal order for over 70% of paired glucose measurements and insulin adjustments within the same interval, and frequently masked significant dysglycaemic events and merged distinct treatment decisions together. We then conducted an in silico clinical trial using 30 virtual patients with type 1 diabetes from the FDA-approved UVA/Padova simulator. The simulator was modified to include stochastic intervals between decisions and used to generate a training dataset for offline RL. We trained three ORL algorithms on both the unprocessed dataset and equivalent datasets resampled at 10-minute, 2-hour, and 4-hour intervals. When deployed back into the simulated environment, temporal resampling was found to reduce model performance by up to 60% relative to unprocessed data, with 4-hour binning causing all agents to perform worse than the dataset's baseline. Retrospective evaluation on resampled data actively obscured this effect, predicting 1.5-3x better returns than agents achieved in practice. We recommend that future research in this area prioritises datasets with natural clinical timings between decisions, which may be a necessary step before these models can be safely deployed into patient care.

replace TASTE: Task-Aware Out-of-Distribution Detection via Stein Operators

Authors: Micha{\l} Kozyra, Gesine Reinert

Abstract: Out-of-distribution detection methods are often either data-centric, detecting deviations from the training input distribution irrespective of their effect on a trained model, or model-centric, relying on classifier outputs without explicit reference to data geometry. We propose TASTE (Task-Aware STEin operators): a task-aware framework based on so-called Stein operators, which links distribution shift to the input sensitivity of the model. The resulting operators admit a geometric interpretation as a projection of distribution shift onto the sensitivity field of the model, with theoretical guarantees. Beyond detecting a shift, the construction enables its localisation through a coordinate-wise decomposition, and - for image data - provides interpretable per-pixel diagnostics. Experiments on controlled Gaussian shifts, MNIST under geometric perturbations, and CIFAR-10/ImageNet-1K perturbed benchmarks demonstrate that TASTE aligns closely with task degradation while showing competitive performance compared to established baselines, with gains that depend on the shift regime.

replace Mitigating Memorization Where It Happens

Authors: Xuanqi Zhang, Haoyang Shang, Xiaoxiao Li

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can memorize and reproduce training sequences verbatim, raising privacy and copyright concerns. Existing mitigation methods apply interventions uniformly, often degrading performance on the majority of tokens that generalize normally. We study a token-level log-likelihood ratio between a target model and a domain-competent reference with lower exposure to the evaluated sequences. Exposure-controlled experiments support its use for localizing positions associated with sequence-level verbatim recall. Across evaluated settings, a minority of positions carries a disproportionate share of the positive signal, motivating selective activation correction. From the ideal memorization reduction objective, we derive a probe--steer framework which decomposes intervention into a probe that detects memorization-relevant activations and a steer that applies targeted correction only when the probe exceeds a threshold. We propose Gated Subspace Steering (GSS) as its practical instantiation: the optimal probe--steer pair admits a closed-form solution as the leading singular vectors of a gradient-weighted activation matrix. Across four benchmarks, GSS matches or exceeds state-of-the-art memorization reduction while requiring 10--100$\times$ less compute than optimization-based alternatives.

replace Size-Transferability of Graph Transformers with Convolutional Positional Encodings

Authors: Javier Porras-Valenzuela, Zhiyang Wang, Teresa Shang, Yusu Wang, Alejandro Ribeiro

Abstract: Transformers have achieved remarkable success across domains, motivating the rise of Graph Transformers (GTs) as attention-based architectures for graph-structured data. A key design choice in GTs is the use of Graph Neural Network (GNN)-based positional encodings to incorporate structural information. In this work, we study GTs through the lens of manifold limit models for graph sequences and establish a theoretical connection between GTs with GNN positional encodings and Manifold Neural Networks (MNNs). Building on transferability results for GNNs under manifold convergence, we show that GTs inherit transferability guarantees from their positional encodings. In particular, GTs trained on small graphs provably generalize to larger graphs under mild assumptions. We complement our theory with extensive experiments on standard graph benchmarks, demonstrating that GTs exhibit scalable behavior on par with GNNs. To further show the efficiency in a real-world scenario, we implement GTs for shortest path distance estimation over terrains to better illustrate the efficiency of the transferable GTs. Our results provide new insights into the understanding of GTs and suggest practical directions for efficient training of GTs in large-scale settings.

replace Fractional Rotation, Full Potential? Investigating Performance and Convergence of Partial RoPE

Authors: Mohammad Aflah Khan, Krishna P. Gummadi, Manish Gupta, Abhilasha Ravichander

Abstract: Rotary Positional Embedding (RoPE) is widely used in transformers to encode relative positional information, yet the effect of applying RoPE to only a subset of hidden dimensions remains underexplored. This design choice can substantially reduce memory usage, especially at long context lengths. We show that partial RoPE achieves up to 10$\times$ memory savings over standard RoPE cache while maintaining comparable final loss. We systematically study its impact on training dynamics and convergence across architectures, model sizes, sequence lengths, and datasets. Our results reveal that: (1) applying RoPE to only a small fraction of dimensions (around 10\%) matches the convergence of full RoPE; (2) these trends remain consistent across settings; and (3) NoPE (No Positional Encoding) can produce loss spikes, which are mitigated by minimal RoPE usage or QK-Norm application. Overall, our findings highlight partial RoPE as an effective trade-off between efficiency and stability.

replace 3DTCR: Vortex-following generative reconstruction improves tropical cyclone intensity prediction from global AI weather forecasts

Authors: Jun Liu, Xiaohui Zhong, Kai Zheng, Jiarui Li, Yifei Li, Tao Zhou, Wenwen Zhang, Hao Li

Abstract: Global numerical weather prediction (NWP) and artificial intelligence (AI) models often underestimate tropical cyclone (TC) intensity, partly because coarse grids underresolve fine-scale three-dimensional inner-core structures that influence TC intensity evolution. Kilometer-scale NWP simulations can better resolve these structures but are too costly for operational use, which also makes high-resolution TC data limited. Here we construct a 3-km, TC-centered dataset for 2018--2024 using the Weather Research and Forecasting (WRF) model and develop 3DTCR, a vortex-following generative framework that reconstructs three-dimensional TC structure from FuXi global forecasts. 3DTCR combines conditional flow matching (CFM) with two-stage training to learn a coarse-to-fine mapping from center-aligned reanalysis simulation pairs and then adapt it to forecast inputs through latent space alignment. Evaluated on 2024 western North Pacific (WNP) forecasts, 3DTCR reduces the root mean square error (RMSE) and the magnitude of the mean bias error (MBE) in maximum 10-m wind speed by 36.5% and 60.7% relative to FuXi, and by 8.9% and 28.2% relative to the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts high-resolution system (ECMWF-HRES), respectively. Case studies suggest that reconstructions capture wind gradients between the eye and eyewall, vertical structure of horizontal winds, and vortex asymmetries, while inheriting the FuXi-predicted TC tracks. 3DTCR generates 5-day TC-centered atmospheric fields in approximately 3 minutes on one GPU, whereas a full-basin 3-km WRF simulation takes about 400 hours on 32 CPU cores. These results demonstrate that such a vortex-following generative reconstruction can add localized three-dimensional detail to global forecasts and improve TC intensity prediction without basin-wide kilometer-scale numerical integration.

replace Learning Not To Interfere: Adapting Experts For Model Merging

Authors: Pratik Ramesh, George Stoica, Arun Iyer, Leshem Choshen, Judy Hoffman

Abstract: Model merging has shown that multitask models can be created by directly combining the parameters of different models that are each specialized on tasks of interest. However, models trained independently on distinct tasks often exhibit interference that degrades the merged model's performance. To solve this problem, we formally define the notion of Cross-Task Interference as the drift in the representation of the merged model relative to its constituent models. Reducing cross-task interference is key to improving merging performance. To address this issue, we propose our method, Resolving Interference (RI), a light-weight adaptation framework which disentangles expert models to be functionally orthogonal to the space of other tasks, thereby reducing cross-task interference. RI does this whilst using only unlabeled auxiliary data as input (i.e., no task-data is needed), allowing it to be applied in data-scarce scenarios. RI consistently improves the performance of state-of-the-art merging methods by up to 3.8% and generalization to unseen domains by up to 2.3%. We also find RI to be robust to the source of auxiliary input while being significantly less sensitive to tuning of merging hyperparameters. Our codebase is available at: https://github.com/pramesh39/resolving_interference

replace A Model Ensemble-Based Post-Processing Framework for Fairness-Aware Prediction

Authors: Zhouting Zhao, Tin Lok James Ng

Abstract: Striking an optimal balance between predictive performance and fairness continues to be a fundamental challenge in machine learning. In this work, we propose a post-processing framework that facilitates fairness-aware prediction by leveraging model ensembling. Designed to operate independently of any specific model internals, our approach is widely applicable across various learning tasks, model architectures, and fairness definitions. replace SEVerA: Verified Synthesis of Self-Evolving Agents

Authors: Debangshu Banerjee, Changming Xu, Eugene Ie, Ming Zhang, Daiyi Peng, Chu-Cheng Lin, Tarun Suresh, Gagandeep Singh

Abstract: Recent advances have shown the effectiveness of self-evolving LLM agents on tasks such as program repair and scientific discovery. In this paradigm, a planner LLM synthesizes an agent program that invokes parametric models, including LLMs, which are then tuned per task to improve performance. However, existing self-evolving agent frameworks provide no formal guarantees of safety or correctness. Because such programs are often executed autonomously on unseen inputs, this lack of guarantees raises reliability and security concerns. We formulate agentic code generation as a constrained learning problem, combining hard formal specifications with soft objectives capturing task utility. We introduce Formally Guarded Generative Models (FGGM), which allow the planner LLM to specify a formal output contract for each generative model call using first-order logic. Each FGGM call wraps the underlying model in a rejection sampler with a verified fallback, ensuring every returned output satisfies the contract for any input and parameter setting. Building on FGGM, we present SEVerA (Self-Evolving Verified Agents), a three-stage framework: Search synthesizes candidate parametric programs containing FGGM calls; Verification proves correctness with respect to hard constraints for all parameter values, reducing the problem to unconstrained learning; and Learning applies scalable gradient-based optimization, including GRPO-style fine-tuning, to improve the soft objective while preserving correctness. We evaluate SEVerA on Dafny program verification, symbolic math synthesis, and policy-compliant agentic tool use ($\tau^2$-bench). Across tasks, SEVerA achieves zero constraint violations while improving performance over unconstrained and SOTA baselines, showing that formal behavioral constraints not only guarantee correctness but also steer synthesis toward higher-quality agents.

replace Where to Steer: Input-Dependent Layer Selection for Steering Improves LLM Alignment

Authors: Soham Gadgil, Chris Lin, Su-In Lee

Abstract: Steering vectors have emerged as a lightweight and effective approach for aligning large language models (LLMs) at inference time, enabling modulation of model behaviors by shifting intermediate representations toward a target behavior. Many existing steering methods apply interventions at globally fixed locations in an LLM, effectively treating the optimal intervention location as input-invariant. However, representations relevant to a target behavior can be encoded at different LLM layers depending on the input. Theoretically, we show that different inputs may require steering at different layers to achieve alignment with a given behavior. Empirically, we observe that the most effective layer for steering varies substantially between inputs. Motivated by these observations, we introduce \textit{Where to Steer} (W2S), a general framework that adaptively selects intervention layers conditioned on the inputs by learning a mapping from input representations to steering layers. Across multiple LLMs and alignment behaviors, W2S consistently outperforms fixed-layer baselines in both in-distribution and out-of-distribution settings. Our findings suggest that input-dependent layer selection is a key design dimension complementary to methods for steering vector extraction.

replace Mind the Gap: Structure-Aware Consistency in Preference Learning

Authors: Mehryar Mohri, Yutao Zhong

Abstract: Aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human intent, whether through explicit reward modeling or direct methods such as DPO, fundamentally relies on minimizing a surrogate loss as a proxy for the true pairwise ranking objective. We prove that this reliance is flawed for the standard surrogate losses used: for the equicontinuous hypothesis sets characteristic of neural networks, no standard surrogate provides a meaningful consistency guarantee. Minimizing the surrogate loss to zero can leave the true ranking error arbitrarily high. To resolve this, we formulate LLM alignment within a margin-shifted ranking framework and derive $H$-consistency bounds showing that enforcing a confidence margin $\gamma$ is not merely beneficial but necessary for consistency. We further introduce Structure-Aware $H$-consistency and a corresponding objective (SA-DPO) that adapts the margin to the semantic distance between responses, preventing instability on near-synonymous pairs. Finally, we analyze the trade-off between the margin required for consistency and the model's finite capacity to satisfy it, revealing a strict hierarchy of surrogate losses: heavy-tailed surrogates (e.g., the Polynomial Hinge family) offer strictly superior consistency guarantees for capacity-bounded models compared to the logistic loss used in DPO. Experiments on UltraFeedback and Argilla DPO-Mix-7k confirm that SA-DPO consistently outperforms DPO and SimPO, with a 58.5% head-to-head win-rate in downstream generation quality.

replace Do LLMs have core beliefs?

Authors: Anna Sokol, Marianna B. Ganapini, Nitesh V. Chawla

Abstract: The rise of Large Language Models (LLMs) has sparked debate about whether these systems exhibit human-level cognition. In this debate, little attention has been paid to a structural component of human cognition: core beliefs, truths that provide a foundation around which we can build a worldview. These commitments usually resist debunking, as abandoning them would represent a fundamental shift in how we see reality. In this paper, we ask whether LLMs hold anything akin to core commitments. Using a probing framework we call Adversarial Dialogue Trees (ADTs) over five domains (science, history, geography, biology, and mathematics), we find that most LLMs fail to maintain a stable worldview. Though some recent models showed improved stability, they still eventually failed to maintain key commitments under conversational pressure. These results document an improvement in argumentative skills across model generations but indicate that all current models lack a key component of human-level cognition.

replace CRAFT: Forgetting-Aware Intervention-Based Adaptation for Continual Learning

Authors: Md Anwar Hossen, Fatema Siddika, Juan Pablo Munoz, Tanya Roosta, Ali Jannesari

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can learn new tasks through fine-tuning, but learning tasks sequentially often leads to catastrophic forgetting of earlier ones. This paper proposes CRAFT, a continual learning framework that keeps model weights frozen and instead learns low-rank interventions on hidden representations. Similar tasks share an intervention to help knowledge transfer, with similarity judged by how each task alters the model's output distribution rather than by a learned gating network. Within a shared intervention, a Kullback-Leibler (KL) penalty keeps each new task close to the intervention's existing behavior, which limits forgetting of earlier tasks, while a small, task-private intervention in the deepest layers preserves dedicated capacity that later tasks cannot overwrite. The learned interventions also make it possible to identify the task at test time, so task identity labels are not needed. Across multiple benchmarks and model scales, CRAFT improves overall performance and reduces forgetting compared with strong LoRA-based methods, while remaining robust to task ordering.

replace HAServe: Horizon-Aware Scheduling and Memory Co-Design for Efficient LLM Serving

Authors: Xingyu Qu, Tianhao Lin, Yiqi Li, Zhiyu Chen, Sheng Wang

Abstract: Online large language model (LLM) services process requests that share context but differ in prefix structure and arrival timing. Finite capacity couples request admission and key-value (KV) retention. This paper addresses one online serving problem: using a unified view of visible demand to choose the feasible joint admission and KV-retention candidate with the lowest predicted latency cost as prefix structures and arrival rates vary. To model this problem, we introduce HorizonSchema, a workload abstraction that connects exact prefix paths, permitted serializations, and released consumers as shared inputs to both decisions. Our analysis shows that useful reuse depends on demand and prefix survival, while service order changes latency even at equal computation. Furthermore, we present HAServe, which turns this abstraction into a scheduling--memory co-design method of systematic optimization for online LLM serving. We implement HAServe on SGLang and evaluate Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 and Llama2-13B on five workloads using eight H100 GPUs. Across the evaluated workloads, HAServe achieves up to $1.14$--$5.90\times$ P99 TTFT speedups and up to $1.08$--$1.44\times$ the throughput, with KV-cache hit rate up to 20.59 percentage points higher than the strongest baseline for each metric and setting.

replace TabFORGE: A Tabular Foundation Model for Generative Modelling

Authors: Xiangjian Jiang, Mingxuan Liu, Nikola Simidjievski, Tassilo Klein, Mateja Jamnik

Abstract: Generative modelling is a demanding test of foundation models, because it requires robust, holistic representation learning for a given data modality, rather than optimisation for a supervised prediction target alone. While recent work on tabular foundation models has achieved remarkable progress in predictive modelling, generative tabular foundation models remain underexplored. Existing tabular foundation generators, in particular, have not yet consistently matched strong dataset-specific generators in synthetic data quality. A key reason is their insufficient alignment with the inter-feature dependency structure of tabular data, which can reflect underlying causal relationships. In this paper, we address this gap by introducing a novel tabular foundation model, $\textbf{TabFORGE}$, built on pretrained $\textbf{Tab}$ular $\textbf{FO}$undational $\textbf{R}$epresentations for $\textbf{GE}$neration. TabFORGE is designed to utilise transferable representations induced by a pretrained structure-aware feature encoder, which captures information useful for characterising inter-feature dependencies. It further decouples latent modelling from decoding through a two-stage design: we first pretrain a score-based diffusion transformer, and then pretrain a denoising-aligned decoder using the denoised latent embeddings. This design elegantly mitigates the distribution shifts in latent embeddings that typically arise between training and inference. We evaluate TabFORGE comprehensively against 22 benchmark methods on 45 real-world datasets. Our results show that TabFORGE effectively learns and leverages generalisable tabular representations, enabling efficient generation of high-quality synthetic tabular data, particularly with strong structural fidelity. Code is available at https://github.com/SilenceX12138/TabFORGE.

replace Locking Pretrained Weights via Deep Low-Rank Residual Distillation

Authors: Keitaro Sakamoto, Pierre Ablin, Federico Danieli, Marco Cuturi

Abstract: The quality of open-weight language models has dramatically improved in recent years. Sharing weights greatly facilitates model adoption by enabling their use across diverse hardware and software platforms. They also allow for more open research and testing, to the extent that users can use them as checkpoints, fine-tune them according to their needs, and potentially redistribute them. In some cases, however, concerns on modifying these weights towards unauthorized uses may outweigh the pros of giving users such freedom. Defending against such adaptation is non-trivial: since an adaptive attacker can observe all weights and architectures by definition, they can reverse simple structural defenses, and use optimization to defeat the simplest locking mechanisms. In this work, we exploit the inference-training asymmetry of automatic differentiation as a novel defense axis. We propose DLR-Lock, a method where the purveyor of the model purposely replaces each pretrained MLP in their model with a deep low-rank residual network (DLR-Net) of comparable parameter count, forcing activation memory that grows linearly with depth during backpropagation. DLR-Nets are efficiently trained via module-wise regression. We show that, beyond this memory overhead, DLR-Lock results in architectural mismatches that complicate the optimization landscape of standard fine-tuning, and a backward pass that incurs disproportionately more overhead than the forward pass. Our defense succeeds in withstanding adaptive attackers with full knowledge of the defense strategy while preserving the original model's capabilities. Experiments on LLMs validate these claims.

replace Plan Before You Trade: Inference-Time Optimization for RL Trading Agents

Authors: Eun Go, Rohan Deb, Arindam Banerjee

Abstract: Reinforcement learning agents for portfolio management are typically trained and deployed as static policies, with no mechanism for policy adjustments using price forecasts at inference time. We propose FinPILOT (Financial Plugin Inference-time Learning for Optimal Trading), a plugin inference-time policy optimization framework inspired by Model Predictive Control (MPC). Under the price-taker assumption, portfolio actions do not affect future market prices, allowing a multi-step price forecaster to provide imagined market trajectories without iterative action-conditioned rollouts. At each decision step, FinPILOT uses the forecaster's predicted price trajectory to construct an allocation-based imagined return objective and optimizes the pretrained policy at inference time before executing one step of the trade. Our framework is compatible with any pretrained policy and adapts the policy using the forecaster's predictions without modifying the original RL training pipeline. Evaluated with five pretrained policies (PPO, SAC, A2C, TD3, DDPG) across DJ30 equities and foreign exchange (FX) over three rolling held-out test years (30 settings in total), FinPILOT improves Total Return in 28 of 30 settings and Sharpe ratio in 27 of 30, with gains spanning both stochastic and deterministic policies. Direct single- and multi-period portfolio optimizers supplied with the same forecasts do not reproduce these aggregate gains under our evaluation protocol. Further, using controlled forecasts at calibrated quality levels, we show that both the magnitude and consistency of FinPILOT's performance increase as forecast error is reduced.

replace Neurodata Without Boredom: Benchmarking Agentic AI for Data Reuse

Authors: Ling-Qi Zhang, Kristin Branson

Abstract: Neuroscience data are fragmented across labs, experimental paradigms, and storage formats, and data reuse often requires substantial manual effort. Standardized formats have been proposed in response, but a fundamental tension persists: formats flexible enough for diverse experiments are rarely self-explanatory, while fully descriptive datasets demand documentation and curation effort that few labs can sustain. Coding agents, which can read code and text quickly with apparent attention to details humans tend to skim, are a natural candidate to reduce this burden. To measure how well coding agents perform data reuse, we benchmark them on eight recent papers with large-scale mouse neural recordings spanning diverse recording modalities, behavioral paradigms, and formats. Given the data, code, and paper, the agents must reformat the data for a common downstream task: training a decoder from neural activity to task and behavioral variables. Evaluating the resulting solutions, however, is challenging, since many conversion decisions are under-specified and several choices are defensible. Yet a subtle but scientifically significant error can invalidate the dataset in ways aggregate metrics can miss. We therefore pair outcome-based metrics with a process-based rubric, scored by two human evaluators against manual reference solutions. We find that agents succeed on most subtasks but rarely produce a fully error-free end-to-end solution; we characterize their mistakes and suggest data-sharing practices for agent-assisted reuse. Finally, we find that agent judges are unreliable at catching errors on their own, but can automate process-based evaluation when given reference solutions.

replace Evolutionary Multi-Task Optimization for LLM-Guided Program Discovery

Authors: Halil Alperen Gozeten, Xuechen Zhang, Emrullah Ildiz, Ege Onur Taga, Tara Javidi, Samet Oymak

Abstract: Recent LLM-guided evolutionary search methods have shown that iterative program mutation can discover strong algorithms, but they typically optimize each task independently, even when related tasks share reusable structure. We study evolutionary multitask optimization (EMO) for LLM-guided program discovery and propose EMO-STA (Shared-Then-Adapt), a two-stage framework that first evolves a shared archive of executable programs across a task family and then adapts selected shared candidates to each target task. Within EMO-STA, we explore multiple adaptation strategies, including warm-starting from the shared archive, adapting the best average shared program, and adapting the shared program that performs best on each target task. Across eight task families spanning continuous optimization, geometric construction, modeling, and algorithmic optimization, EMO-STA improves over single-task evolution under matched evolutionary-iteration budgets in most settings, with STA Best-Local providing the strongest in-distribution adaptation and STA Best-Shared yielding robust transfer to unseen tasks. Iteration-allocation experiments show that devoting a substantial fraction of the family-level iteration budget to shared evolution is effective, with roughly balanced shared and aggregate adaptation budgets often yielding the best performance. ARC and time-series feature-engineering case studies further suggest that shared evolution can mitigate overfitting in low-evidence settings by favoring programs that perform well across related tasks rather than exploiting brittle task-specific artifacts.

Authors: Noel Thomas

Abstract: Standard accuracy on binary reasoning benchmarks hides critical failure modes: prior collapse, inconsistency under paraphrase, and inability to reason about parameter-dependent dynamics. replace Amortized Factor Inference Networks for Posterior Inference

Authors: Joohwan Ko, Justin Domke

Abstract: Amortized inference promises fast test-time Bayesian inference, but existing methods typically target a fixed model or a restricted model family. Extending amortization to new model families often requires retraining or test-time fine-tuning. In this paper, we ask: is it possible to build a single inference network capable of generalizing across varying priors, likelihoods, latent dimensions, and observation counts? We introduce Amortized Factor Inference Networks (AFINs), which describe a model as a list of typed prior and likelihood factors and map this description, together with the observations, to a Gaussian posterior approximation through an encode--merge--decode network whose learned parameters do not depend on the latent dimension or the number of observations.
