replace-cross Optimal Watermark Localization in Mixed-Source Large Language Model Texts

Authors: Jose H. Blanchet, T. Tony Cai, Xiang Li, Hao Liu, Qi Long, Weijie J. Su

Abstract: Watermarking provides a principled way to authenticate text generated by large language models (LLMs). In practice, however, the final text may be mixed-source, with watermark evidence surviving at only a subset of token positions after rewriting, insertion, deletion, or paraphrasing. Although prior work has studied global detection of watermark signals, when such signals can be localized remains unclear. We formulate watermark localization as a token-level multiple-testing problem based on pivotal statistics, with a latent indicator recording whether watermark dependence survives at each position. Under an asymptotic regime indexed by exponents for signal sparsity, next-token concentration, and effective-vocabulary growth, we derive a sharp boundary for global detection and phase transitions for discovery and classification within the class of coordinatewise pivot-based localization rules. We show that discovery is strictly harder than detection and that consistent classification is impossible across the parameter regime within this class. We then develop an adaptive thresholding method that does not require knowledge of the exponents or time-varying next-token distributions, but uses a data-driven estimate of the surviving watermark fraction. The method attains the optimal discovery boundary and near-optimal discovery power relative to homogeneous pivot-based rules. Simulations support the theoretical phase transitions, while experiments on model-generated texts demonstrate practical localization performance under common edit mechanisms.

replace-cross DataKernelBench: Can LLMs Optimize Database Queries on GPUs?

Authors: Gokul Karthik Kumar, Yotam Perlitz, Corey Lammie, Andrea Giovannini, Katja Hose

Abstract: GPUs increasingly accelerate database systems, but query-specific peak performance still often relies on hand-written kernels. Existing LLM kernel benchmarks focus on machine learning operators, leaving irregular, heterogeneous, data-movement-heavy database-style operators untested. We introduce DataKernelBench, which translates SQL into validated PyTorch TorchPlan programs and evaluates LLMs that optimize either the core tensor-bounded snippet or the full query in CUDA or Triton through execution-guided repair. Across ten proprietary and open-weight models on TPC-H SF10 with an H100 GPU, the strongest full-query CUDA configuration achieves $2.11\times$ speedup over the TorchPlan baseline at full pass rate. We find that higher-performing implementations commonly use kernel fusion and execution-strategy changes, stronger models benefit most from full-query specialization, and workload context matters more than hardware context. To handle data larger than GPU memory, we extend TorchPlan with Dask-cuDF for on-demand partition loading on TPC-H SF100 with four H100 GPUs, achieving $2.54\times$ speedup. Project page: https://kerneldf.github.io/datakernelbench

URLs: https://kerneldf.github.io/datakernelbench

replace-cross Language Models Can Control Their Own Attention

Authors: Namgyu Ho, Huzama Ahmad, Woosung Koh, Se-Young Yun, Tal Schuster, Cicero Nogueira dos Santos

Abstract: Language models spend most of their attention on a small fraction of context, yet they read the entire KV cache to find the few tokens that matter. If the user asks about a previous detail in a 1M-token conversation, global attention layers must scan the full context to generate each token of the reply. A prominent approach pre-selects relevant tokens via lightweight proxy scores, but this extrinsic scoring still incurs O(N) per step. We take an intrinsic approach motivated by the simple question: wouldn't the model already know which parts of the context are relevant? We introduce Declarative Attention (DA), a protocol that elicits the model to declare where it needs to attend within its chain-of-thought, partitioning generation into three modes: (full context), (a specific region), and (recent output only). The inference engine parses these declarations like tool calls and skips most of the KV cache read. Under zero-shot evaluation across 15 long-context tasks, DA on off-the-shelf models (Gemma-4-31B, Qwen-3.6-27B) reduces total attended tokens per response (45.8%, 19.6%), despite extra decoding steps, with modest accuracy drops (1.40pp, 2.10pp) that shrink with model scale. DA unlocks a new axis of sparse attention, with further potential under training-based methods. Code is available at https://github.com/itsnamgyu/declarative-attention.

URLs: https://github.com/itsnamgyu/declarative-attention.

replace-cross LightMedSeg-ISLES: Stroke Lesion Segmentation with 81x Fewer Parameters than nnU-Net

Authors: Giorgi Nikvashvili, Hanxue Gu, Jie Bao, Kang Wang, Yang Yang

Abstract: Large networks and ensembles often lead medical image segmentation challenges, but their storage and inference demands complicate deployment. We present LightMedSeg-ISLES, a 1.26-million-parameter pipeline for T1-weighted stroke lesion segmentation in ISLES'26. On a 146-case held-out cohort, flip test-time augmentation produces 0.618 mean Dice and 0.599 lesion-wise F1. A 102.35-million-parameter nnU-Net ResEnc-L produces 0.634 Dice and 0.544 lesion-wise F1 after size filtering. LightMedSeg therefore retains 97.5\% of nnU-Net's Dice with 81.4$\times$ fewer parameters while improving lesion-wise F1 by 0.055. Its four-pass TTA operating point requires 4.7$\times$ fewer FLOPs per standardized patch than nnU-Net. It also slightly exceeds filtered UNETR++ and nnFormer. Longer training and stronger augmentation add 0.0358 Dice without increasing capacity, establishing a strong single-checkpoint alternative to much larger models.

replace-cross FAME: An FPGA-Based Platform for Approximate Multipliers Evaluation with Pattern-Guided DNN Retraining

Authors: Rappy Saha, Nima Amirafshar, Jude Haris, Nima Taherinejad, Jos\'e Cano

Abstract: Approximate multipliers can reduce hardware area and energy consumption in Deep Neural Network (DNN) inference; however, they introduce computational errors. Assessing the accuracy of numerous approximate multiplier designs across diverse DNN models and large-scale datasets remains challenging due to prohibitive evaluation times. This overhead primarily stems from the slow emulation of approximate multiplier behavior using look-up tables (LUTs) on CPU and GPU platforms. Moreover, the resulting accuracy degradation must be carefully quantified and, if necessary, mitigated (e.g., through retraining), further increasing the overall evaluation cost. To address these challenges, we propose FAME, an FPGA-based platform for evaluating approximate multipliers. The platform exploits the reconfigurable logic of Field-Programmable Gate Arrays (FPGAs) to implement approximate multipliers directly in hardware, eliminating the need for LUT-based emulation on CPU/GPU platforms and thereby enabling efficient DNN inference while significantly reducing evaluation time on large datasets. Furthermore, we introduce a pattern-guided DNN retraining technique to mitigate accuracy degradation induced by approximate multipliers. Specifically, retraining is guided by multiplier-specific patterns to effectively recover potential accuracy losses. We evaluate FAME using two DNN models, ResNet-18 and MobileNetV2, on the ImageNet dataset across 27 approximate multipliers. During inference, our approach achieves up to a 3.47x speedup in approximate multiplier evaluation compared to prior LUT-based emulation methods. Furthermore, the proposed retraining technique improves accuracy by up to 65.5% over existing retraining approaches for the evaluated multipliers. The code is publicly available at: https://github.com/gicLAB/FAME

URLs: https://github.com/gicLAB/FAME

replace-cross CompMat-Bench: Benchmarking AI Agents for Computational Materials Science

Authors: Chenmu Zhang, Levi Felix, Jun-Jie Zhang, Xingfu Li, Xuelian Jiang, Tao Jiang, Subhendu Mishra, Xixi Qin, Boris Yakobson

Abstract: Evaluating AI agents on scientific research tasks is constrained by the time and resources required for the underlying experiments or calculations. In computational materials research, repeating the same expensive simulations across agents and trials can make evaluation impractical. We introduce CompMat-Bench, a benchmark of 94 tasks derived from recently published computational materials studies, each asking agents to complete a step toward achieving the study's scientific goal. We reproduce the research steps in advance and assess agents on preparing inputs and analyzing outputs for expensive simulations, so expensive simulations can be avoided during evaluation. The reproduced inputs and results serve as ground truth for grading agents with fixed rules, without an LLM judge. The benchmark supports four evaluation conditions: single tasks and workflows composed of related tasks, each with full or reduced methodological guidance. With full guidance on single tasks, agents based on three LLMs demonstrate the ability to complete individual materials research steps, with pass rates of 66.0-90.4% across 94 tasks. Both longer workflows and reduced guidance can limit agent performance, but in different ways for different agents: they lower the pass rates of the weaker agents, whereas the strongest agent falls only when a long workflow is combined with reduced guidance. Failure analysis attributes most failures to scientific errors rather than to errors in software usage. CompMat-Bench provides a basis for comparing agents on the steps of real materials research and for analyzing agent failure modes.

replace-cross StabilityArc: Decoding Protein Sequence Embeddings into Generalizable Stability Landscapes

Authors: Aaron L. Feller, Andrew D. Ellington, Claus O. Wilke

Abstract: Every protein has a unique stability landscape, but the physical consequences of mutation are governed by recurring biochemical constraints. We test whether a shared decoder, trained on measurements from diverse proteins, can interpret these constraints in an unseen target, enabling cross-protein transfer for initial experimental round prescreening. We present StabilityArc , which maps frozen ESMC-600M residue representations through a shared RoPE transformer to an Lx20 matrix of substitution effects; a symmetric, contact-aware residual aids in predicting epistasis in simultaneous substitutions. In 66 strict leave-one-protein-out evaluations covering 134,794 ProteinGym variants, StabilityArc achieves 0.7134 Spearman correlation, exceeding the strongest zero-shot baseline, ProSST-2048 (0.6526), by 0.0608. We further explore the utility of this method by providing the score as a prior for Kermut, achieving Spearman correlation of 0.8280 across three supervised split schemes, improving on Kermut's reported 0.8167.

replace-cross Calibrated Evidence Inference With a Small, Permissively Licensed Open Model

Authors: Johann Emmanuel Li

Abstract: Systematic reviewers who use a model to read randomized trials need each answer and a measure of how far to trust it. If the model is calibrated, so that its stated probabilities match how often it is right, a reviewer can accept its confident answers and check the rest by hand. This paper fine-tunes Qwen3-4B-Base with low-rank adapters and a classification head for evidence inference (Lehman et al., 2019; DeYoung et al., 2020): given a trial's article and a prompt that names an intervention, a comparator and an outcome, classify the result as a significant increase, a significant decrease or no significant difference. The articles of Evidence Inference 2.0 come from the open-access subset of PubMed Central, which includes articles under non-commercial and no-derivatives licenses, so the released model was fine-tuned only on the 1,431 of 2,657 training articles under CC BY, CC0 or the public domain; it is released under the Apache License 2.0 and runs on a laptop. On the test split, its expected calibration error is 0.017 and its log loss is lower than that of Gemma 4 31B-it given the same input zero-shot, meeting criteria registered on the Open Science Framework before testing. Its macro-F1, 0.925, is above every published result on that split that I found, though those systems used other inputs, scoring rules and prompt filters, and the test labels are public. Accepting its 80 percent most confident answers leaves an error rate of 2.6 percent among them. Context ablations show that the answers depend on the article, but the title alone carries some signal, which an informative title or recall of the trial could explain.

replace-cross TTS-Q: Perception-Guided Post-Training Quantization of Text-to-Speech Diffusion Transformers

Authors: Xiaoqin Feng, Yuqi Li, Weilun Feng, Yi-Cheng Lin, Kai Li, Xianglong Wang, Kuo Yang, Chuanguang Yang, Yingli Tian, Wei Li, Zhongwei Cheng, Lin Chen

Abstract: Zero-shot text-to-speech (TTS) can clone a voice from seconds of audio. Yet the quality comes from billion-parameter diffusion transformers whose size and multi-step sampling put them out of reach of the on-device, assistive, and real-time applications that need them most. Post-training quantization (PTQ) shrinks such models without retraining, but it has been built for language and vision, not speech. At 4 bits, a strong quantizer leaves intelligibility close to full precision but consistently erodes speaker timbre, so error rates stay low while the voice drifts from the target speaker. We ask why quantization hurts speech, where the damage lives, and whether it can be repaired without retraining. To find out, we run the first 4-bit weight-and-activation (W4A4) PTQ study of a TTS diffusion transformer at 1B and 3.5B. The timbre cost comes from both the weight and the activation quantization, but only the activation part has the timestep structure a precision schedule can exploit. We therefore introduce TTS-Q, which selects hard-to-quantize items from task-matched calibration candidates and applies a budget-balanced mixed-precision schedule that assigns higher precision to late-step feed-forward activations. TTS-Q recovers timbre best at both scales, at no cost to intelligibility. Separately, the deployed calibrated-W4A4 reference reduces peak memory on GPU, providing a practical reference for resource-constrained TTS deployment.

replace-cross EV-JEPA: Elastic V-JEPA for Any Device and Cross-Device Inference

Authors: Anxhelo Diko, Jieyu Lin, Stefano Ambrogio, Ziyun Li, Mahmoud Assran, Th\'eo Moutakanni, Barbara De Salvo, Chiao Liu

Abstract: Foundation models like V-JEPA are increasingly deployed across a heterogeneous edge-cloud continuum spanning smart glasses, phones, robots, and servers. Yet, they are typically trained as rigid, fixed-capacity networks bound to one device, supporting neither reconfigurable compute budgets nor collaborative execution. Existing approaches address only one axis at a time: slimmable and NAS-based methods provide width elasticity but assume single-device inference, whereas split-computing methods enable cross-device collaboration only for fixed partitions and static capacities. We introduce Elastic V-JEPA (EV-JEPA), a single model trained once and deployable everywhere along the continuum. Its width is reconfigurable, and it is natively partitionable across heterogeneous devices. At its core is Transition-Aware Triplet Training (T3). At each step we pick a narrow and a wide width, and train three paths: each width alone, plus a hybrid path that runs the early layers narrow and the rest wide. The hybrid switch point is where one device hands off to another, so the same weights learn both single-device and cross-device execution. Across various benchmarks, EV-JEPA falls within 1 accuracy point of independently trained baselines while improving over previous elastic training approaches by up to 11 accuracy points. Narrow-to-wide cross-device hybrid execution recovers to within 1.1 points of the highest-capacity standalone model and supports many-to-one, any-to-any, and variable-split configurations. Finally, we show that the elastic principle extends beyond the backbone to an elastic token compression mechanism that exposes controllable trade-offs among on-device computation, latency, and accuracy.

replace-cross Self-improving data synthesis enables scalable adaptation of embedding models

Authors: Zucan Lyu, Rui Tang, Jianlyu Chen, Peisheng Jin, Defu Lian

Abstract: AI agents require specialised retrieval across domains and tasks, but adapting embedding models is constrained by costly manual training-data design. Here we introduce SYNTRA, a self-improving agentic framework that turns a few retrieval examples into reusable synthesis instructions. Given an unlabelled corpus and a few relevant query-document pairs, SYNTRA infers implicit information needs and relevance criteria, then uses feedback on trial outputs to refine instructions for query generation and relevance assessment. Experiments across 24 datasets spanning 15 domains demonstrate that the same synthesis workflow produces effective training data, benefiting even strong embedding models with and without existing labelled training sets. Self-improvement strengthens this supervision on all six evaluated tasks, yielding better retrieval than human calibration on four. The training value persists at larger scales: on MS MARCO, retrieval gains continue across a 32-fold range of synthetic training-data sizes. SYNTRA thus enables scalable embedding-model adaptation with minimal human intervention.

replace-cross AlphaRLM: Aligning Language Models for Literature-Grounded Symbolic Factor Discovery

Authors: Jie Huang, Yuecheng Hong, Yao Chen, Hao Zhong, Linfeng Xiao, Xudong Liu, Qiang Ye, Hao Xia, Jianxuan Xin

Abstract: Formulaic alpha mining seeks to discover executable expressions that capture predictive market signals in a vast, noisy search space. Recent LLM-based approaches use prompting, iterative search, or seed-factor evolution, leaving direct factor construction through policy learning less explored. To address this, we introduce AlphaRLM, a reinforcement-learning framework that trains LLMs to construct executable symbolic factors from financial literature without input factor expressions. AlphaRLM incorporates Literature-Guided Operator Assembly (LEGO), which conditions factor construction on literature-derived ideas and executable operators, providing semantic priors for exploration. It further aligns the generation policy with market feedback through executable-validity and grouped-return-separation rewards that capture non-monotonic structures across factor quantiles. Extensive experiments on CSI 300, CSI 1000, and S&P 500 show that AlphaRLM outperforms strong factor-mining and LLM-based baselines in annualized excess return. Notably, the 8B model achieves strong cross-market performance, highlighting the promise of literature-conditioned, market-aligned factor generation.

replace-cross PMB-NN: Physiological-Model-Based Neural Network for Glucose Forecasting in Type 1 Diabetes

Authors: Yaowen Zhang, Shuhao Que, Ying Wang

Abstract: Accurate, physiologically consistent glucose forecasts could help people with type 1 diabetes respond to impending excursions. We applied our physiological-model-based neural network (PMB--NN) framework to glucose forecasting. A temporal convolutional network (TCN) used 180 min of history, constrained during training by a personalized four-parameter physiological model. The main PMB--NN combined forecast and neural--physiological agreement losses; a three-term variant additionally fitted physiological predictions to measured glucose. Data comprised 6--8 weeks of continuous glucose monitoring, pump, and meal records from 25 AZT1D participants. Person-specific models used chronological 70/10/20 train/validation/test splits and 30-, 60-, and 120-min horizons. Median mean absolute errors were 16.24, 25.00, and 30.20 mg/dL with basal insulin records and 15.38, 24.21, and 31.79 mg/dL before these records became available. In the latter setting, PMB--NN had the lowest median error at 30 and 60 min; it outperformed the physiological model alone in all six settings. In input-increase tests, glucose predictions changed in the expected direction for 85\% of correction boluses and 100\% of carbohydrate rescues, versus 57\% for active insulin and 56\% for active carbohydrates. Most standalone physiological fits showed locally distinguishable parameter effects, with glucose-related coefficients more stable under resampling than insulin- or carbohydrate-related coefficients. The three-term variant improved hyperglycaemia accuracy with basal records, but did not significantly change overall MAE and reduced directional plausibility in three tests. PMB--NN combines personalized forecasting with an explicit physiological model, allowing assessment of predictive accuracy, physiological response behavior, and participant-specific coefficients.

replace-cross A Bethe-Ansatz Source of Early Token Correlations: A marginal-preserving construction for parallel proposals

Authors: Abraham Chachamovits

Abstract: Parallel token proposals need a joint law, not merely accurate token marginals. We construct coordinate-Bethe-ansatz laws from the periodic XXZ chain and compare them with their maximum-entropy pairwise projections at identical one- and two-site moments. In an eight-site, three-excitation sector, symmetry makes the full probability-law difference one-dimensional, determined by a contiguous three-site moment $\delta$. Along the Bethe branch, this gap changes sign and vanishes exactly at $\Delta=(3+\sqrt3)/2$, where the eigenstate law becomes pairwise. A nonlinear two-token readout preserves separate token marginals yet exposes a distance $2|\delta|$; a direct three-token readout preserves all one- and two-token marginals yet exposes $4|\delta|$. At $\Delta=1.5$ these deterministic binary distances are $0.02654573$ and $0.05309145$. We give an exact target-alignment formula and prove a strict speculative accepted-prefix advantage throughout an unrestricted open neighborhood of a constructed target. These are finite-law existence results, not measured language-model or wall-clock gains.

replace-cross Morphology-aware deep learning predicts colorectal cancer survival from whole-slide images

Authors: Hikmat Khan, Metin N. Gurcan, Anil Parwani, Wendy L. Frankel, Wei Chen, Muhammad Khalid Khan Niazi

Abstract: Background: Five-year survival prediction from hematoxylin-and-eosin whole-slide images could support risk stratification, but the task is weakly supervised, and the tissue morphologies carrying prognostic information remain incompletely characterized. Methods: We conducted a retrospective secondary analysis using TCGA-COAD/READ (n=562) for morphology-specific analysis, Alliance/ CALGB 89803 (n=424) for model development, and SurGen (n=218) for external evaluation. We propose MorphContrast, a morphology-aware contrastive multiple-instance learning framework that uses tissue labels as structural supervision for five-year survival prediction. MorphContrast combines gated-attention pooling with three auxiliary objectives: patch-level morphology labels define within-patient contrasts, outcome concordance or discordance defines cross-patient contrasts within matched morphologies, and diversity regularization discourages representational collapse. Morphology-matched patches from patients with discordant outcomes serve as hard negatives, encouraging prognostic discrimination within comparable histologic contexts. Results: Morphology-specific models show weak but distributed prognostic discrimination: adipose tissue achieves an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.622, stroma achieves 0.604, and both perform comparably to tumor epithelium (0.601-0.605). On Alliance/CALGB 89803, the single model achieves an AUC of 0.642, while the post hoc ensemble reaches a pooled out-of-fold AUC of 0.70 and a risk-group hazard ratio of 3.79 (95% confidence interval: 2.55-5.63). On the external SurGen colon cancer cohort (n=218), the single model and ensemble achieve AUCs of 0.669 and 0.678, respectively. Conclusions: These findings support tissue morphology as structural supervision for outcome-aware representation learning in colorectal cancer prognosis.

replace-cross SPARCL: Spatially Adaptive Regional Coverage for Active Learning in Chest X-ray Analysis

Authors: Juho Jung, Seongjae Kang, Mijoo Kim, Sungduk Cho, Yunkyoung Jun, Sunghoon Joo, Yoona Hwang

Abstract: Pixel-level annotation for chest X-ray (CXR) lesion segmentation is expensive, making active learning (AL) essential. However, existing AL strategies fail to account for the spatial distribution of selected samples, resulting in inconsistent performance across focal (e.g., aortic calcification) and diffuse (e.g., nodules) pathologies. In this paper, we propose SPARCL (Spatially Adaptive Regional Coverage for Active Learning) to dynamically balance predictive uncertainty with cumulative spatial coverage. By employing a concave coverage function, SPARCL imposes diminishing returns on previously annotated regions, naturally directing the annotation budget toward under-labeled areas. Furthermore, an entropy gate reverts the algorithm to standard uncertainty sampling when spatial variation is absent. This coverage objective is monotone submodular, guaranteeing near-optimal greedy batch selection. To address how aggregate metrics like Dice obscure regional failures, we further introduce two metrics---Spatial Inconsistency (SI) and Performance Coverage (PC)---to quantify anatomical fairness across AL rounds. Evaluated on an in-house calcification cohort and public CXR benchmarks, SPARCL reaches 95% of fully supervised performance using only 24% of the data, compared to 29% for the best baseline. Ultimately, SPARCL ensures robust spatial reliability, surfacing regional gains that aggregate metrics miss.

replace-cross ReTerm: Recovery Training for Self-Improving Terminal Agents

Authors: Wei Xie, Kaituo Feng, Shiming Fu, Yong Liu, Xiangyu Yue, Shuangjia Zheng

Abstract: Terminal agents have shown strong abilities in complex coding tasks. However, they often struggle to recover from errors during long-running execution (e.g., command errors, faulty implementations), eventually leading to task failures. To this end, we present ReTerm, a self-improving terminal agent trained by learning how to recover from its own failed terminal tasks. To support its training, we construct a customized data pipeline and produce ReTerm-10K, a high-quality dataset that provides effective recovery demonstrations for SFT cold start. Furthermore, we propose ReTerm-GRPO, an online RL algorithm that forks from the agent's own failure states to generate multiple independent self-recovery attempts. By learning from whether these attempts successfully complete the task, the agent self-improves through its own recovery experience. Built on Qwen3.5-9B, our ReTerm-9B achieves 34.8% Pass@1 on Terminal-Bench 2.0, improving over the base model by 14.6 points. Further analysis shows that our method achieves consistently higher recovery rates across diverse error categories compared with standard GRPO training. All code, data and model are released at https://github.com/twothsnd/ReTerm.

URLs: https://github.com/twothsnd/ReTerm.

replace-cross Scaling Multilingual Mixture-of-Experts: How Language Coverage Changes Capacity and Compute Allocation

Authors: Joel Dag, Nikit Srivastava, Hamada M. Zahera, Axel-Cyrille Ngonga Ngomo

Abstract: Multilingual models share capacity across languages, so adding languages can hurt performance on existing ones. Mixture-of-experts models expand this capacity with little additional per-token computation. Given this extra capacity, does adding experts reduce what each additional language costs? To answer this question, we propose a repetition-aware joint scaling law relating number of active parameters, training tokens per language, number of experts, and language coverage. Fitted on 26,300 per-language loss observations from 64 pretrained configurations, our law reduces prediction error on held-out configurations by 28% relative to the strongest evaluated baseline. At matched number of active parameters and training tokens per language, 32 experts reduce the loss penalty of expanding from 12 to 200 languages by 83% compared with a dense model. Our law further shows that broader language coverage favors more experts across a wider range of compute budgets, guiding capacity allocation as multilingual models scale.

replace-cross Co-Evolving Acting and Forecasting for Language Agents

Authors: Haofei Yu, Yixing Chen, Yining Zhao, Xuyan Ye, Yuxiang Liu, Zhigang Hua, Yan Xie, Shuang Yang, Jiaxuan You

Abstract: Training language agents is bottlenecked by the high cost of long-horizon rollouts, yet standard agentic reinforcement learning (RL) learns only from a terminal scalar reward, leaving the supervision in intermediate steps largely unused. Prior work exploits this supervision through value targets that estimate per-step success probabilities or observation targets that fit environment response. Both are hard to construct well: (1) value targets come from the same single scalar signal for each step in the trajectory, and (2) observation targets, such as web pages and terminal logs, are long and noisy and reach only one step ahead. We propose CoPE, which adds a third kind of multi-step action targets. On successful on-policy trajectories, CoPE trains the policy backbone to generate the action sequences that follows each history. Each action is also supervised when forecast from the preceding histories, forming a temporal ensemble that the shared backbone absorbs during training, and this enables acting and forecasting to co-evolve. The policy still acts one step at a time at inference, with no overhead. Across five benchmarks (ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, $\tau^2$-bench, and AppWorld), CoPE (1) achieves stronger aggregate performance than GRPO and RL baselines with predictive auxiliary targets across simulated environments, while improving overall performance on both realistic applications; (2) delivers up to $1.9\times$ and $2.0\times$ higher sample efficiency than ECHO and GRPO, respectively, on ScienceWorld; and (3) achieves these gains with a single policy backbone, without additional models, separate pretraining, or inference overhead. The codebase is available at https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/CoPE.

URLs: https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/CoPE.
